## 📚 Paper List

- **[Enumeration-Guided Training of Neural Hamilton-Jacobi Safety Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27870)**  `arXiv:2610.27870`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Mihaela-Larisa Clement, Tosca Pierro, Ezio Bartocci, Luca Marzari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) reachability analysis offers formal safety guarantees for control systems, but training accurate neural certificates remains difficult, particularly when unsafe regions are hard to characterize. We propose a verification-guided training framework that addresses this by using probabilistic enumeration of safety constraints to detect forward-invariance violations, i.e., states where the learned certificate incorrectly classifies unsafe behavior. This produces a compact, targeted set of counterexamples that more precisely captures the failure boundary than inputs generated by standard adversarial attack methods. Crucially, using this representation to fine-tune a pretrained HJ value function yields larger certified regions and reduces certification time across all tested benchmarks. We further introduce a shielding mechanism based on the refined certificates to enforce safety constraints at deployment time. We evaluate the proposed safety filter in a realistic, physics-based Isaac Sim environment and through real-world autonomous racing experiments, achieving zero safety constraint violations compared to a nominal controller and a backup policy based on Linear Quadratic Regulation (LQR). These results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed shielding mechanism in ensuring safe operation under high-speed, safety-critical conditions.
  </details>

- **[Which Depth Estimator for Jetson AGX Orin? A 2026 Benchmark of Monocular and Stereo Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27199)**  `arXiv:2610.27199`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Leaf Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Jetson AGX Orin is a common computer for robots and vehicles, yet measured accuracy, speed and energy of current depth estimators on it are hard to find. We benchmark nine model families as of October 2026: the monocular Depth Anything V2 and 3, Depth Pro, MoGe-2, Metric3D v2 and UniDepth V2, and the stereo methods libSGM, NVIDIA ESS and Fast-FoundationStereo. Every method runs on the Orin as a TensorRT FP16 engine or native CUDA code at up to three input sizes, up to the full 5.7 MP, and is scored for metric accuracy against Middlebury 2014 ground truth (non-occluded pixels of the 10 scenes whose disparity stays below 256 px), GPU time, energy and licence. Stereo is far more accurate. At 30 fps, NVIDIA's ESS reaches 0.70% AbsRel in 8.6 ms and libSGM 0.73% in 25 ms; with more compute, Fast-FoundationStereo reaches 0.33%. The best monocular models reach 12% with the true focal length (Depth Anything 3 Metric-L in 33 ms, Depth Pro in 803 ms), and 3.8-4.0% only with an oracle per-image scale (MoGe-2-L, UniDepth V2-B, Metric3D v2-L). Stereo error falls as the input resolution grows; no monocular model improves, and the metric scale of most drifts away from the truth outside their training resolution. At depth discontinuities, which AbsRel barely registers, the monocular models scatter 17-46% of the edge points between foreground and background, learned stereo 2-9% and libSGM at full resolution 1%. We give recommendations per frame-rate budget and release all code, per-scene results and engine build scripts.
  </details>

- **[CAPSafe: A Benchmark for Contextual Action and Plan Safety Reasoning in Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28381)**  `arXiv:2610.28381`  `cs.RO` `cs.CL`  
  _Youmna Farag, Svetlana Stoyanchev, Simon Keizer, Mohan Li, Rama Doddipatla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning about safety is essential for embodied agents powered by large language models (LLMs). We introduce the Contextual Action and Plan Safety benchmark (CAPSafe), designed to evaluate safety reasoning at two levels: (1) an action-level dataset that tests whether a single action is safe given its environmental context, and (2) a plan-level dataset that evaluates whether agents can generate safe long-horizon plans for complex hazardous household tasks. CAPSafe models safety conditions where hazards are situational (inferred from object properties), multiple hazards can co-occur within the same environment, and each is annotated with a relative severity level for fine-grained analysis. Evaluating nine closed and open-source LLMs reveals a substantial gap between hazard recognition and safe long-horizon planning. Several models achieve high accuracy in recognizing unsafe actions (exceeding 90% in the best cases), yet safe long-horizon planning remains substantially harder. Gemini-3-pro achieves the highest safe success rate (78.19%), while other models remain below 65%. These results highlight that robust safety reasoning over long action sequences remains an open challenge.
  </details>

- **[UniWALM: Unified World Action Language Model for Embodied Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28626)**  `arXiv:2610.28626`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Rahul Sajnani, Diego Romeres, Mingyuan Zhang, Kai Gao, Carl Qi, Baichuan Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Motivated by the human ability to integrate experience across language, perception, and action, we introduce UniWALM, a unified world-action-language model that learns video, action, and language in a single shared representation, where each modality is both a conditioning signal and a supervision target. We ask: can jointly generating language enrich this shared representation, improving manipulation and supporting memory-dependent behavior? Unlike prior approaches that attach a reasoning or vision-language model to a video-action backbone and run them as distinct stages, UniWALM unifies all three modalities inside a single mixture-of-transformers backbone whose per-modality diffusion heads co-denoise synchronously, decoding subtask and textual memory in the same pass as action with no separate planning stage; the generated language is re-consumed on the next step, closing a language-as-memory loop without an external planner or memory bank. To keep the action stream reliable under this richer objective, we further introduce Flow Matching with Correction (FMC), a training objective that teaches recovery from perturbed action trajectories at no inference-time cost. UniWALM consistently improves over same-backbone baselines (e.g., FastWAM) on compositional pick-and-place (MolmoSpaces) and across the memory-dependent subset of RMBench, where it reaches 34.5% without any pretrained language or reasoning model and exceeds Mem-0's keyframe-equipped setup (28.5%) using only a single anchor frame. On a real-world Find-the-Item task, generating textual memory lifts success from 11.1% to 61.1%, while state-of-the-art VLAs stay at or below chance (<=33.3%).
  </details>

- **[Plausible Future, Wrong Choice: Steering World-Action Models toward Intended Future](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28431)**  `arXiv:2610.28431`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuohao Li, Jiawei He, Yuxuan Yan, Lang Zhou, Yinghao Li, Yanrui Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-Action Models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied generalist policies by jointly modeling future world evolution and robot actions across diverse manipulation tasks. However, representing diverse futures is only the first step: a capable WAM must also generate futures that match the requirements of the current task and execution progress. In long-horizon manipulation where similar observation-action segments occur across different tasks and stages, existing approaches may generate locally plausible yet misdirected rollouts to drift toward wrong task stage, i.e., a phenomenon we term Stage Confusion. To address this problem, we propose TaskWAM, a closed-loop, stage-aware framework that represents fine-grained task requirements with Mode Tokens and tracks execution progress with State Tokens, forming a structured task state to steer world--action generation toward intended future. We further introduce StageBench, a controlled multi-stage manipulation benchmark with parameterized stage variations and automatedthis http URLexperiments on both simulation benchmarks and real-world tasks demonstrate that TaskWAM effectively reduces confusion and achieves competitive performance with state-of-the-art methods. Moreover, TaskWAM provides strong plug-and-play transfer across heterogeneous WAM architectures and robot embodiments, including single-arm robots, dual-arm grippers, dexterous hands, and humanoid robots.
  </details>

- **[DA-WAM: Decision-Aligned Future Latents for Driving World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19085)**  `arXiv:2608.19085`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruiguo Zhong, Xiaolong Chen, Benshan Ma, Lang Zhang, Mingyue Feng, Yaonong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models for autonomous driving should anticipate the consequences that matter for driving decisions rather than reconstruct every detail of future scenes. However, predictive representations alone are not guaranteed to distinguish candidate actions that lead to different planningthis http URLpropose DA-WAM, a decision-aligned world action model that couples a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) with candidate-level trajectory scoring. During joint training, the JEPA objective grounds future representations in observed scene dynamics, while the planning objectives encourage these representations to capture distinctions relevant to safety and utility. An action-conditioned predictor generates a distinct future latent for each candidate trajectory, and a multi-head trajectory scorer uses the corresponding latent to estimate planning factors and overall utility. In addition, safety-critical post-training with hard negatives further improves discrimination between geometrically similar trajectories with different safety outcomes. DA-WAM achieves state-of-the-art performance among learned camera-based planners on NAVSIM-v1, NAVSIM-v2, and Bench2Drive. Ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of each component in driving decision-making.
  </details>

- **[SuperMimic: Scaling Humanoid Whole-Body Control with One Differentiable World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28480)**  `arXiv:2610.28480`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qiayuan Liao, Yiyang Shao, Junfeng Long, Kevin Zakka, Roberto Horowitz, Pieter Abbeel, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tracking large human-motion datasets has become the dominant route to general, expressive humanoid whole-body control, but scaling across many motions is expensive. Current pipelines use model-free reinforcement learning, either merging per-motion specialists through lossy distillation, or brute-forcing a single policy with cluster-scale compute. SuperMimic amortizes learning across all motions with one differentiable world model of their shared dynamics, fit from logged transitions of a standard rigid-body simulator. The world model and the tracking policy train together in one loop, and backpropagation-through-time over short imagined rollouts gives the policy analytic gradients. Fast imagined rollouts and analytic gradients make policy learning efficient. In 3 hours on a single GPU, SuperMimic surpasses the return of a state-of-the-art motion tracker baseline trained for 7 days on 128 GPUs. It reduces GPU-hours by more than $1000\times$ and fails on only 25 of 129,514 benchmark motions, versus 179 failures for the baseline. After 9.8 hours on two GPUs, SuperMimic achieves zero failures. From 1 to 264 hours of motion, the world model's prediction error stays stable, and GPU-hours to a target return grow slowly. Our policy transfers to a physical Unitree G1 across diverse and acrobatic motions. SuperMimic removes compute as the bottleneck to scaling humanoid whole-body control, making it practical to measure motion-tracking scaling laws.
  </details>

- **[Physical AI for Intralogistics: Benchmarking the NVIDIA Sim-to-Real Ecosystem for Cosmos-Enhanced COMPASS Navigation on Industrial Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28432)**  `arXiv:2610.28432`  `cs.RO`  
  _Charbel Abi Hana, Rayan Zaitouni, Kameel Amareen, Mohamad Mostafa, Hasan Sayour, Wei Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical robot navigation stacks are reliable but often require re-engineering for each new robot type and site. Learning-based navigation shifts this effort from hand-tuned parameters to data generation and training, where a policy adapts to each new embodiment or facility by fine-tuning in simulation. Whether policies adapted this way stay reliable on real industrial robots has seen little systematic testing. We study this for NVIDIA's X-Mobility and COMPASS workflow for training learned navigation policies on Idealworks robots. In simulation, we extend the COMPASS benchmark with two embodiments, the differential-drivethis http URLand an omnidirectional Humanoid Mobile Platform (HMP), and with a digital twin of the Idealworks R&D center. For the real world, we fine-tune X-Mobility on data from this digital twin, with and without sim-to-real variants generated by NVIDIA Cosmos, train COMPASS on top of it from scratch in the digital twin, deploy the resulting policies on thethis http URL's onboard Jetson AGX Xavier, and benchmark them in the Idealworks R&D center against COMPASS trained only on NVIDIA's environments and the existing Idealworks navigation stack on task success, efficiency, safety, smoothness and onboard compute. Running every static scenario both in a digital-twin replica and in the real facility measures the sim-to-real gap and how far Cosmos-augmented data closes it. Training in the digital twin with Cosmos sim-to-real augmentation raises the real-world success rate of learned navigation from 65.3% to 91.7% across four scenarios and nearly halves its weighted travel time compared with simulation-only training, bringing it within 8.3 points of the production navigation stack, fully onboard; with an integrated global planner, it also reaches far, occluded goals in every trial.
  </details>

- **[Large Multimodal Models for Direct Robot Control: GPT-6 as a Beginning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28259)**  `arXiv:2610.28259`  `cs.RO`  
  _Youwei Zhou, Bohan Hou, Yankai Fu, Yaoxu Lyu, Yifan Ye, Shilin Shan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in visuospatial understanding and agentic reasoning enable direct robotic manipulation with general-purpose multimodal models, but their embodied capabilities remain poorly understood. This paper systematically evaluate GPT-6 Astra for closed-loop robotic manipulation using a lightweight agent execution framework. We first probe three fundamental embodied capabilities-depth perception, embodied in-context learning, and tactile understanding-and demonstrate that the model can exploit depth information, acquire simple manipulation strategies from contextual demonstrations, and use tactile feedback to correct its actions. We then evaluate GPT-6 Astra across five established manipulation benchmarks along six capability dimensions. Despite competitive performance on several benchmarks-26.92\% success on RoboDojo and 76.0\% on RoboTwin 2.0-our analysis reveals a pronounced gap between semantic reasoning and physical execution, particularly in consequence-aware action planning, precision, and complex motion coordination. To further examine these limitations, we introduce LIBERO-GeoMotion, a new benchmark of geometry-constrained tasks that require precise pose alignment, coordinated translation and rotation, and anticipation of physical action consequences. GPT-6 Astra achieves only 11.1\% zero-shot success, exposing the difficulty of translating spatial understanding into physically feasible motion. Real-world experiments corroborate these findings: zero-shot execution is feasible for simple tasks, yet persistent deficits remain in motion feasibility, precise control, and physical safety, while substantial inference latency further hinders practical deployment. Overall, our results characterize the emerging embodied capabilities of general-purpose multimodal models and delineate the key challenges toward safe, efficient, and reliable robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[FIT-MAC: A Closed-Loop Multi-Agent Framework for Robust Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28109)**  `arXiv:2610.28109`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiatong Li, Jisheng Dang, Qiangqiang Ma, Bimei Wang, Jingwen Zhao, Wenjun Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models have achieved strong performance on standard robotic manipulation benchmarks. However, their reliability can degrade substantially when task conditions deviate from the nominal setting. Perturbations in object positions and task instructions may lead to complete task failure. Existing VLA policies generally lack an explicit runtime mechanism for distinguishing these failure modes and selecting an appropriate recovery strategy. To systematically mitigate this issue, we propose FIT-MAC, a failure-aware, closed-loop multi-agent control framework for improving the robustness of VLA policies at inference time. FIT-MAC augments a VLA policy with an inference-time control layer. First, the Planner Agent parses the language instruction into a structured task specification that captures the manipulation goal and spatial constraints, and the Grounder Agent resolves each instruction-level reference to a visual target. Next, the Verifier Agent tracks phase-specific execution progress, distinguishes target-binding errors from local execution failures, and determines whether intervention is warranted. The Correction Agent then routes permitted interventions to target rebinding or bounded grasping or placement recovery, depending on the failure type. When no intervention is required, the pretrained VLA remains the default action generator. Extensive experiments on LIBERO-PRO demonstrate that FIT-MAC improves robustness across multiple VLA backbones, consistently increasing success rates and achieving state-of-the-art performance under diverse perturbations.
  </details>

- **[EventSync: Event-Driven Memory Updates for History-Dependent Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27917)**  `arXiv:2610.27917`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jingjiao Zhao, Dong Jing, Mingyu Cao, Ruochuan Shi, Xiansheng Chen, Pengwei Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  History-dependent robotic manipulation requires task context and interaction history, as the current observation alone is insufficient to determine the next action. Hierarchical frameworks use task memory for high-level subgoal reasoning and low-level policies for execution. However, memory updates require accurate completion assessment: similar observations may correspond to different execution phases, and prematurely recording completion can mislead subsequent subgoal reasoning. We present EventSync, a hierarchical framework that coordinates task reasoning and action execution through event-driven memory updates. A vision-language model generates subgoals from task plans and interaction history, while a vision-language-action model executes them. A lightweight Event Monitor combines visual observations with end-effector trajectories to determine whether the current subgoal is complete. The framework records the current subgoal in its completed-subgoal history and triggers subsequent reasoning only after obtaining completion evidence. With visual history in the executor, EventSync achieves 64.5% overall success on RoboMME, exceeding the best baseline reported in the benchmark by 20.0 percentage points. Further analysis shows that this update mechanism helps build a reliable completed-subgoal history. Experiments on four real-robot tasks with different memory requirements validate the framework's effectiveness in real-world robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[High-Precision RCM Motion for Ophthalmic Robotics via Hierarchical QP Control and Authority-Guided Recovery Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27905)**  `arXiv:2610.27905`  `cs.RO`  
  _Huanyu Tian, Adriana Namour, Lingyun Zeng, Zicong Wu, Sebastien Ourselin, Lyndon da Cruz, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose manipulators can position ophthalmic instruments under a software Remote Centre of Motion (RCM). However, at some reachable configurations that are nonsingular for the tip task alone, the RCM and tip-tracking objectives conflict and the servo oscillates. We formalise this failure as a constraint-induced task singularity: the RCM-projected tip Jacobian loses rank while the unprojected tip Jacobian stays well conditioned. Thus, the effective tip authority collapses although the target remains reachable. We present a high-precision RCM servo architecture with built-in singularity recovery: for each commanded target, an authority-guided planner selects a well-conditioned arm configuration that reaches it and plans an RCM-consistent route, executed by a fixed hierarchical-QP controller. On a 200-scenario kinematic benchmark, the complete nominal-and-recovery protocol reaches 128/128 near-singular targets and 71/72 near-joint-limit targets (100% and 99%, respectively), with the software-RCM residual below 0.142 mm. On a physical 6-DOF arm it executes across four RCM entry points, with a worst-case kinematically reconstructed RCM residual of 178 {\mu}m and a median tip-regulation error of 32{\mu}m, within the 200{\mu}m control requirement adopted for the ophthalmic task.
  </details>

- **[RoboCasa-Shift: Stage-Aware Robustness Evaluation of Vision-Language-Action Policies in Long-Horizon Household Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27861)**  `arXiv:2610.27861`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haocheng Wang, Youquan Liu, Weiyu Guo, Aokai Zhang, Donghang Wu, Hongyu Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) robustness is commonly summarized by aggregate task success, which obscures how individual executions change under distribution shifts and where failures emerge in long-horizon tasks. We introduce \textbf{RoboCasa-Shift}, a diagnostic robustness benchmark built on RoboCasa365 that combines controlled single-factor shifts, matched clean-perturbed rollouts in which each pair shares the same underlying settings and differs only in the target shift, and simulator-grounded stage-aware diagnostics. We evaluate GR00T N1.5 on four long-horizon tasks that pass a predefined clean-performance gate under four perturbation factors and three calibrated severity levels, including conditions that drive the evaluated policy to complete task failure, totaling 1,040 formal rollouts. Robustness responses vary substantially across factors and tasks, and increasing severity does not always produce monotonic degradation. For example, one camera condition yields $\Delta\mathrm{SR}=0$ despite 8 of 80 matched episodes changing outcome. Stage-aware analysis further shows that the same end-to-end degradation can correspond to different execution-depth profiles. Camera $L_2$ and Gaussian blur $L_1$ both yield $\Delta\mathrm{SR}=0.188$, yet their first-stage failure rates are 19.0\% and 36.4\%, respectively. Our findings suggest that VLA robustness should be viewed as a multi-dimensional property of execution rather than a single end-to-end performance measure, motivating evaluation protocols that jointly capture outcome degradation, paired outcome sensitivity, and failure progression. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[JARVIS: From Visual Manuals to Autonomous Robotic Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27618)**  `arXiv:2610.27618`  `cs.RO`  
  _Fan Ding, Fucai Ke, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Xuewen Luo, Wenyu Liang, Vishnu Monn Baskaran, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual manuals communicate procedural knowledge through stepwise diagrams, but translating these instructions into reliable long-horizon robotic execution remains challenging. We introduce JARVIS, a method-agnostic benchmark for autonomous visual-manual-guided execution without additional language instructions. JARVIS comprises 236,679 LEGO assembly tasks across 8,987 scenes, with a carefully curated JARVIS-v1 suite of 50 tasks for interactive robotic evaluation. Tasks span varying initial structural complexities and assembly horizons, requiring agents to interpret manuals, ground actions in an evolving workspace, and execute the intended procedure. A structure-aware evaluation protocol captures geometric accuracy, structural relations, construction order, partial progress, and completion. We further introduce JARVIS-agentic, a tool-grounded embodied agent that composes robot primitives into executable programs and uses execution feedback for verification and recovery, without task-specific action trajectories or model parameter updates. Across nine multimodal and reasoning models, task-oriented primitives increase the average assembly score. Performance nevertheless declines sharply with assembly horizon, and execution errors remain difficult to correct. Prior-task experience improves initial performance, while repeated reflection provides inconsistent benefits, highlighting persistent limitations in connecting visual procedural reasoning with reliable physical execution.
  </details>

- **[HATCH: Hardware-Attested Task Chains for Verifiable Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27485)**  `arXiv:2610.27485`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shiza Charania_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Capability claims in robot manipulation are unverifiable, and existing leaderboards are each closed, simulated, or gameable. We propose HATCH, a robot escape room: an openly specified, physically replicable, sensor-scored, and unrehearsable benchmark. Tasks span five skill categories at three difficulty levels and are chained so that the sensor confirming one task's completion unlocks the next, turning a room into a single long-horizon evaluation. Success is confirmed by instrumentation rather than human judgment, scoring is embodiment-agnostic, and object placements are randomized by a run-day seed. Entrants submit attested self-runs (uncut video, sensor logs, and the seed), with occasional organized events for calibration. Task specifications, build guide, bill of materials, and scoring rubrics are released under an open license.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Be Different: Component Specialization for Compositional Diffusion Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27194)**  `arXiv:2610.27194`  `cs.RO`  
  _Alec Laprevotte, Haonan Chen, Xiaoshen Han, Yilun Du_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion policies with linearly composed scores have emerged as a promising approach to multitask robotic learning by combining separate factors to enable modular, task-wise adaptation, compositional generalization, and interpretable functional decomposition. However, current training objectives do not encourage factor specialization, often resulting in uninterpretable denoisers whose composed prediction is an average over identical copies, undermining their architectural premise. To address this, we propose a drop-in score-decorrelation auxiliary loss, motivated by projective composition theory. Our approach encourages specialization by penalizing overlap directly between component noise predictions, with little architectural change or computational overhead. We demonstrate across multiple benchmarks that our decorrelation loss consistently reduces measurable double-counting between components, improves task decomposition, and produces behaviorally meaningful specialization without compromising multitask performance. Website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ACE-Brain-0.6: Scaling Spatial Grounding for Physical Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27040)**  `arXiv:2610.27040`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yinhe Wang, Tao Tao, Zexin He, Shuang Luo, Wei Mao, Yao Mu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose multimodal models, including GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5, demonstrate broad capabilities in embodied manipulation. Our analysis indicates that this generality is accompanied by strong spatial perception, as Astra can determine where to interact with task-relevant entities amid visual distractors. Because spatial perception connects high-level reasoning to the physical scene, we investigate scaling visual grounding to strengthen the perceptual foundation of general embodied operation. We develop a unified data pipeline that combines robot manipulation recordings with complementary visual data from diverse sources. After automatically annotating the robot recordings, the pipeline converts the collected data into verifiable grounding supervision. Fine-tuning Qwen3.5-9B on approximately 1.5 million samples produced by this pipeline yields ACE-Brain-0.6. The model outperforms GPT-6 Astra on four of seven public grounding benchmarks, with an overall average score of 75.41 compared with Astra's 73.68. Under a fixed Qwen3.5-9B fine-tuning setup, aggregate grounding performance improves steadily as successive data releases expand the training collection to cover a broader range of grounding scenarios. These results show that scaling verifiable grounding supervision from diverse sources can strengthen the spatial perception of an open 9B model for general embodied operation.
  </details>

- **[Embodied Playground: A Long-Horizon Agentic Robotics Benchmark for Physical, Strategic, and Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27039)**  `arXiv:2610.27039`  `cs.RO`  
  _Feng Chen, Pei Zhou, Lixuan He, Yunchao Yao, Li Sun, Shenghua Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models offer a promising basis for robotic agents, but long-horizon tasks require them to sustain coherent reasoning and reliable physical execution across interdependent stages. We introduce Embodied Playground, a benchmark for evaluating how foundation-model agents coordinate physical, strategic, and spatial reasoning throughout such tasks. Agents must track task progress, satisfy intermediate constraints, and revise their plans using execution feedback as earlier actions shape subsequent decisions. The playground comprises 410 task environments spanning basic manipulation, sequential puzzles, and multi-stage household activities, with variations in planning depth, geometric constraints, and physical execution demands. We curate EP-100, a representative selection of 100 tasks across five suites and 16 task types, together with the EP-Easy and EP-Hard difficulty splits and the compact EP-20 selection. A shared protocol specifies observation and tool access, resource budgets, repeated trials, and independent checks of goal completion, task-rule compliance, and physical validity. Embodied Playground provides a unified framework for studying whether foundation-model agents can sustain effective reasoning and action over extended task horizons.this https URL
  </details>

- **[Replan for Error Recovery: Training Embodied VLMs through Verifiable Interaction with Action-Grouped Turn-GSPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27003)**  `arXiv:2610.27003`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhuoran Wang, Keqing Fan, Yang Li, Jinbo Su, Ke Wang, Sijia Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied VLMs can execute increasingly complex manipulation tasks, yet execution errors can leave the environment in states that require replanning for recovery. Existing recovery methods either rely on inference-time mechanisms with fixed policies or learn from failure examples and reference corrections rather than verified action outcomes. To enable recovery learning through verifiable interaction, we introduce the Embodied Replanning for Recovery Environment (ERR-Env), which provides feedback on task progress and execution errors. We further construct ERR-Bench, a benchmark with held-out tasks spanning nominal, intermediate, and error states for evaluating recovery capability and execution reliability. We then propose Action-Grouped Turn-GSPO (AGT-GSPO), an online reinforcement learning method combining turn-level rewards for task progress and error states with action-grouped advantage estimation, and use it to train ERR-VLM, a compact 2B embodied VLM. Evaluation on ERR-Bench reveals a mismatch between task progress and execution reliability across diverse VLMs. AGT-GSPO improves ERR-VLM by 10.25 percentage points in Strict Success Rate for error-free completion and 22.95 percentage points in Recovery Rate, while ablations and zero-shot transfer further validate the effectiveness of our method.
  </details>

- **[Comfortably Stuck: Evaluation Pitfalls in Bench2Drive's Closed-Loop Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21741)**  `arXiv:2610.21741`  `cs.RO`  
  _Emad Aghajanzadeh, Thomas Schwarzfischer, Moritz Thoma, Nael Fasfous, Manoj Rohit Vemparala, Pierpaolo Mori, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closed-loop evaluation is widely regarded as a more faithful way to assess end-to-end (E2E) driving models than open-loop evaluation, and Bench2Drive, built on the CARLA simulator, has become the dominant benchmark for it. We show that its evaluation protocol itself contains pitfalls that can misrepresent the models scored on it. Bench2Drive's comfort metric, adapted from nuPlan, has several implementation errors and a segmentation scheme that rewards standing still, so it mostly measures how long a vehicle stays stationary, correlating with stationary time at r = 0.97 across eight models. We propose a corrected, distance-based metric that cuts that correlation to r = 0.06. Under it, the strong driving-score/comfort tradeoff the original metric reports is no longer detectable once models are compared at matched driving speed, which suggests that comfort trades against how fast a model drives rather than against how well it drives. We also uncover two pitfalls in how the harness supplies navigation signals: the default controller can replace a model's own steering with an angle toward the route target point, and the target point supplied at evaluation time can differ substantially from what a model saw in training. We demonstrate both on UniAD and VAD, where their effects differ sharply, and argue that future closed-loop evaluations should check for them. We will release all fixes as standalone code, so corrected comfort scores can be obtained from logged trajectories without re-running closed-loop simulation.
  </details>

- **[PAKE: Learning Whole-Body Loco-Manipulation with Partial Kinematic Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11041)**  `arXiv:2607.11041`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhengmao He, Moonkyu Jung, Hyeongjun Kim, Jiseong Lee, Hui Zhang, Jemin Hwangbo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Loco-manipulation has recently shown promising capabilities; however, achieving high-precision control, managing the high-dimensional action space induced by many degrees of freedom (DoFs), and fully exploiting the inherent redundancy of whole-body systems remain challenging. In this paper, we propose a novel whole-body control framework that effectively addresses these challenges by decomposing the complex loco-manipulation problem into partial reference motion generation and low-level imitation control. We introduce a new Kinematic Normalizing Flow (KNF) model, trained on a large-scale kinematic dataset, that generates diverse partial kinematic references. A high-level controller is then trained to navigate the KNF's latent space to exploit redundant solutions, while a low-level controller learns dynamically consistent motion execution. We validate our approach on the quadrupedal robot equipped with a six-DoF robotic arm. In simulation, PAKE outperforms the evaluated baselines in tracking accuracy and feasible workspace coverage on the 4,000-target benchmark. For hardware deployment, we evaluate the system over 24 episodes across 8 different mobile loco-manipulation tasks. The system achieves end-effector pose-tracking errors of 4.5 cm and 0.14 rad, while maintaining accurate locomotion tracking with linear and angular velocity errors of 0.1 m/s and 0.01 rad/s, respectively. Our method represents a practical and powerful solution for accurate and generalized whole-body loco-manipulation in high-DoF robotic systems, with promising potential for diverse downstream robotic tasks.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Trajectory Optimization for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07170)**  `arXiv:2606.07170`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yihong Xu, Eloi Zablocki, Yuan Yin, Elias Ramzi, Ellington Kirby, Alexandre Boulch, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end planners for autonomous driving typically generate a set of candidate trajectories, score each one, and return the highest-scoring candidate. However, the scorer is applied only after the proposals are generated and cannot influence the set of trajectories: a weak set of candidates limits planning performance regardless of the scorer's quality. We instead treat the scorer as a learned trajectory-level reward function and search for trajectories that maximize it. Our method, TOAD, runs the Cross-Entropy Method at test time, warm-started from the planner's proposals. It requires no retraining and is plug-and-play for existing planners. Across seven base planners, TOAD improves results on NAVSIM-v1 (94.7 PDMS), NAVSIM-v2 (56.3 EPDMS), and the closed-loop HUGSIM benchmark. The code is made publicly available via the project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Not All Features Are Created Equal: A Mechanistic Study of Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19233)**  `arXiv:2603.19233`  `cs.RO`  
  _Bryce Grant, Xijia Zhao, Peng Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fine-tuned Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policy can easily place the alphabet soup in the basket on command, just as it saw during fine-tuning. However, if the basket is shifted five centimeters to the left, it drops the soup off the table. We developed an interpretability pipeline to investigate what such a policy is computing. Injecting task A's activations into the action expert while the policy looks at task B's scene makes pi0.5 execute task A's motor trajectory in 99.6\% of episodes ($n{=}1{,}968$). The injected program is bound to absolute workspace coordinates and ignores the visible scene, which provides a mechanistic account of the perturbation brittleness that concurrent benchmarks report. The binding holds on a physical UR10e: directed replay redirects the commanded task in 26/26 rollouts in one direction and 18/26 in the other, with no-injection and random-direction controls at the floor, and once the source object is moved 10--15\,cm the arm grasps and releases at the recorded coordinates in 50/50 trials, picking up the wrong block when one sits there and closing on empty air when none does. Applying the same interventions to six VLAs on LIBERO, MetaWorld, SimplerEnv, and ALOHA over 420{,}000+ rollouts yields three further findings: every architecture encodes the instruction, and four of the five weight it below vision whenever the scene alone identifies the goal; under the standard replacement write-back the SAE pooling that preserves behavior is architecture-specific (pi0.5 per-token, SmolVLA indifferent), and goal selection and motor generation separate into VLM and expert pathways wherever the two are distinguishable. We release \textbf{Action Atlas} (this https URL), an interactive platform covering all 520+ trained SAEs, 79 identified concepts, and the scored UR10e rollouts. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ROAR: Benchmarking LMM Agents under Real-Time Constraints in Multiplayer Action-Shooter Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24282)**  `arXiv:2610.24282`  `cs.MA`  
  _Dawei Wang, Di Zhao, Jianran Yu, Chengming Zhou, Marci Chi Ma, Xiaoyang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large multimodal models (LMMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that perceive, reason, and act in interactive multi-agent environments. Yet most existing benchmarks for LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings remain turn-based: the environment and other agents pause while an agent performs inference. Such protocols obscure a fundamental challenge of real-time constraints, where the world continues to evolve and delayed decisions may become outdated before execution. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce ROAR, a multiplayer action-shooter game environment for evaluating LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings under real-time constraints. ROAR covers cooperative, competitive, mixed cooperative-competitive, and basic single-agent scenarios. We further propose a Multi-Agent Asynchronous Gym interface that decouples environment evolution from agent reasoning by running the environment and each agent in separate threads, enabling the environment to advance at fixed time intervals without waiting for LMM inference and allowing agents to reason independently without blocking one another. ROAR also features convenient deployment, multimodal observations, and customizable levels. ROAR offers a practical testbed for advancing LMM-based agents beyond static, turn-based evaluation toward real-time multi-agent decision-making.
  </details>

- **[ECHO: Learning Epistemically Adaptive Language Agents with Turn-Level Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29745)**  `arXiv:2606.29745`  `cs.MA`  
  _Abhijnan Nath, Sifatul Anindho, Nikhil Krishnaswamy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective multi-turn agents must decide what information to seek, how to use new evidence, and when they are certain enough to act. We introduce Epistemic Decision Processes (EDPs), a belief-state formulation of multi-turn information seeking in which actions produce external observations that update the agent's posterior over a latent task variable. EDPs make epistemic adaptivity explicit: good policies choose actions that are useful under the current belief, not merely those that correlate with eventual success. We then introduce ECHO (Epistemic Credit for History-Conditioned Optimization), a practical clipped policy-gradient objective that assigns turn-level credit using posterior-sensitive rewards. In the Clue Selector Game, a novel controlled evidence-seeking benchmark, and in CRAFT, a challenging multiagent coordination game, we show that ECHO substantially improves resolution, information gain, and efficiency over trajectory-level GRPO, and matches or exceeds frontier baselines on epistemic metrics such as grounding, recovery, and calibration.
  </details>

- **[Planning under Noise with Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28623)**  `arXiv:2610.28623`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Michael Chen, Hong Chul Nam, Xi Deng, Anima Anandkumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Planning under uncertain control execution noise requires reliable feedback policies across diverse environments. We introduce Planning Under Noise (PUN), a neural operator that learns noise-aware value fields for planning in unseen geometries. Building on an established screened-Poisson formulation of stochastic optimal control, we address two challenges: specifying boundary conditions that support navigation across varying geometries and efficiently computing fields with reliable gradients. Zero-Dirichlet obstacle boundaries terminate sampled paths at walls, weakening the goal signal along long routes and making it harder to estimate reliably. We instead impose reflecting Neumann conditions on obstacles and a Dirichlet condition at the goal, allowing sampled paths to continue exploring free space until they reach the target. To solve the resulting PDE without a configuration-space mesh, we extend a Monte Carlo (MC) solver, Walk on Stars (WoSt), to higher dimensions. However, local MC fluctuations can reverse estimated gradient directions and cause planning failures, making direct planning require costly sampling. PUN amortizes this computation by learning geometry-to-field mappings from noisy, partially converged Monte Carlo labels, avoiding expensive convergence for every training example. At inference, test-time scaling uses bounded local search guided by the predicted global field. We evaluate PUN on planning tasks across dimensions. Across four noise levels, PUN matches or outperforms all compared planners, achieving 95-100% success on the 4D replanning benchmark while maintaining the lowest runtime. At the highest tested noise level in 4D, PUN achieves 10.7-60.6% relative gains in success rate over other methods while running 3.29-32.15 times faster.
  </details>

- **[HarnessPro: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement with Pareto Optimization for Performance-Cost Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26770)**  `arXiv:2610.26770`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhuoshi Pan, ZhiYuan Feng, Yukuo Cen, Honglin Lin, Junru Lu, Zhongxia Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness development plays a crucial role in building capable AI agents. Recent approaches improve harnesses through recursive self-improvement (RSI), but their performance gains often come with higher execution costs. We introduce HarnessPro, a label-free method for evolving harnesses to improve task performance while reducing execution cost. In this setting, candidate harnesses are evaluated using LLM-generated tests without access to ground-truth solutions or official tests. Specialized agents diagnose execution trajectories, plan updates with different performance-cost priorities, and implement them as candidate harnesses. Rather than returning a single harness, HarnessPro uses normalized weighted squared-distance selection to provide cost-focused (lite), balanced (medium), and performance-focused (heavy) harnesses for different deployment budgets. Selected harnesses enter the next iteration, where execution feedback guides further updates. Our experiments cover Terminal-Bench 2, SWE-Bench Pro, and ProgramBench, using GPT-5.5 with Codex and GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Across these benchmarks using GPT-5.5 with Codex, the heavy mode of HarnessPro achieves an average pass-rate gain of 2.0 percentage points and an average per-task cost reduction of 26.1% relative to RHO. This advantage also holds when using GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Generalization in Knowledge Graph Embedding: An Empirical Study and Sparse Peak Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23442)**  `arXiv:2505.23442`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Linyu Li, Zhi Jin, Yuanpeng He, Dongming Jin, Haoran Duan, Zhengwei Tao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graphs (KGs) provide structured representations of entities and relations, and underpin data management and Web applications such as search, recommendation, and question answering. Since Web-scale KGs such as Freebase and Wikidata are inherently incomplete, knowledge graph embedding (KGE) maps entities and relations into continuous vector spaces and is widely used to infer missing facts through link prediction. Despite extensive progress in KGE model design, the role of regularization has been less systematically examined, especially beyond tensor-decomposition models. The primary contribution of this paper is a comprehensive empirical study of regularization for KGE from the perspective of generalization. Across translation-based, tensor-decomposition-based, GNN-based, temporal, and multimodal KGE models, we show that overfitting is common across model families and that appropriate regularization can substantially improve both generalization and predictive performance. Motivated by these findings, we further propose Sparse Peak Regularization (SPR), a simple and model-agnostic regularizer that introduces rank-based selective sparsity into the regularization target. SPR concentrates the penalty on dominant high-energy embedding components, which contribute most to the embedding norm and overfitting risk, while leaving low-energy components unpenalized so that weak but useful signals are not unnecessarily suppressed. Experiments on eight benchmark datasets and diverse KGE models show that SPR achieves the best results in nearly all settings compared with existing regularizers such as N3, DURA, and ER. These results show that regularization deserves more systematic attention in KGE, and that selective regularization is an effective and broadly applicable strategy for improving the generalization of KGE models.
  </details>

- **[WeAct: Dynamic Weight Switching as Agent Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26847)**  `arXiv:2610.26847`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Rui Shi, Xunjian Yin, Jiacheng Sang, Zichuan Liu, Shuyan Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22236)**  `arXiv:2610.22236`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bangji Yang, Jiajun Fan, Zhaoyu Zhu, Hongbo Ma, Ge Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation consolidates specialist models into one student at the price of rollouts, teacher passes and gradient steps for every teacher. We show that the consolidation does not need training: with forward passes only, the capabilities of separately tuned specialists can be aggregated into one model that is, on average, more accurate than either specialist alone. When specialists share a base model, distillation has an exact layer-wise solution: each linear layer of the fused model must reproduce every specialist's output on the inputs that specialist receives while reading its own answers, a least-squares problem whose solution needs only the second moments of those inputs. One forward pass over a few hundred rollouts provides them. We call this training-free on-policy distillation. What the rollouts contain matters more than the solver: answers truncated in repetition loops are few among a mathematics specialist's rollouts but carry a large share of their tokens, and removing them lifts the fused model on AIME25 from task arithmetic's level to the specialist's at matched precision. On Olmo-3-7B, a few GPU-hours of forward computation per specialist yield a generalist that keeps most of each specialist's gain on each of seven mathematics and code benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Model Merging Made Slim](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18904)**  `arXiv:2605.18904`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Guodong Du, Wanyu Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model merging consolidates independently fine-tuned checkpoints without joint training or access to task data. Dynamic methods retain task-specific capacity, but must divide a limited storage budget between shared and expert parameters. We propose DiDi-Merging, which jointly allocates low-rank capacity to these components through differentiable rank selection, followed by data-free parameter-space refinement. On the eight-task ViT-B/32 benchmark, the small configuration reaches 89.3% average accuracy (98.7% normalized retention) at 1.24x the storage of one backbone, including that backbone; the medium configuration reaches 89.8% at 1.4x. Removing refinement reduces the medium configuration by 4.3 percentage points, while static-merge plus uniform-recovery controls are 4.7-8.9 points below the small configuration. Additional evaluations cover language and multimodal models at their separately reported budgets. The main results assume known task identity; practical routing and accuracy under larger task counts at fixed capacity remain distinct evaluation questions. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Detection Is Cheap, Routing Is Learned: Why Refusal-Based Alignment Evaluation Fails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18280)**  `arXiv:2603.18280`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Gregory N. Frank_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current alignment evaluation mostly measures whether models encode dangerous concepts and whether they refuse harmful requests. Both miss the layer where alignment often operates: routing from concept detection to behavioral policy. We study political censorship in Chinese-origin language models as a natural experiment, using probes, surgical ablations, and behavioral tests across nine open-weight models from five labs. Three findings follow. First, probe accuracy alone is non-diagnostic: political probes, null controls, and permutation baselines can all reach 100%, so held-out category generalization is the informative test. Second, surgical ablation reveals lab-specific routing. Removing the political-sensitivity direction eliminates censorship and restores accurate factual output in most models tested, while one model confabulates because its architecture entangles factual knowledge with the censorship mechanism. Cross-model transfer fails, indicating that routing geometry is model- and lab-specific. Third, refusal is no longer the dominant censorship mechanism. Within one model family, hard refusal falls to zero while narrative steering rises to the maximum, making censorship invisible to refusal-only benchmarks. These results support a three-stage descriptive framework: detect, route, generate. Models often retain the relevant knowledge; alignment changes how that knowledge is expressed. Evaluations that audit only detection or refusal therefore miss the routing mechanism that most directly determines behavior.
  </details>

- **[TTCS: Test-Time Curriculum Synthesis for Self-Evolving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22628)**  `arXiv:2601.22628`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Chengyi Yang, Zhishang Xiang, Yunbo Tang, Zongpei Teng, Chengsong Huang, Fei Long, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-Time Training offers a promising way to improve the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs) by adapting the model using only the test questions. However, existing methods struggle with difficult reasoning problems for two reasons: raw test questions are often too difficult to yield high-quality pseudo-labels, and the limited size of test sets makes continuous online updates prone to instability. To address these limitations, we propose TTCS, a co-evolving test-time training framework. Specifically, TTCS initializes two policies from the same pretrained model: a question synthesizer and a reasoning solver. These policies evolve through iterative optimization: the synthesizer generates progressively challenging question variants conditioned on the test questions, creating a structured curriculum tailored to the solver's current capability, while the solver updates itself using self-consistency rewards computed from multiple sampled responses on both original test and synthetic questions. Crucially, the solver's feedback guides the synthesizer to generate questions aligned with the model's current capability, and the generated question variants in turn stabilize the solver's test-time training. Experiments show that TTCS consistently strengthens the reasoning ability on challenging mathematical benchmarks and transfers to general-domain tasks across different LLM backbones, highlighting a scalable path towards dynamically constructing test-time curricula for self-evolving. Our code and implementation details are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning Computational Structure for Sample-Efficient Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28555)**  `arXiv:2610.28555`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hangyu Zhou, Constantine Dovrolis, Stephen Mussmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Statistical learning in high dimensions suffers from the curse of dimensionality. Fortunately, many functions of practical interest are compositionally sparse, meaning they admit a composition of local functions, each depending on only a few variables, allowing sample complexity to depend on local arity rather than ambient dimension. Yet realizing this benefit requires exploiting hidden compositional structure, which theory does not establish for practical gradient-based training. We study this gap with CSFRBench, a controlled benchmark that hides the target's compositional computation during training but retains it for diagnosis, and find that dense networks and sparsity-based baselines leave much of that benefit unrealized. Inspired by the spatial organization of biological neural circuits, we introduce Learned Wiring (LeWi): each neuron has a learned low-dimensional embedding, only nearby neurons connect, and embeddings and weights are trained jointly. LeWi improves sample efficiency on targets with hierarchical structure, intermediate reuse, or independent modules. Across 32 additional targets generated by independently sampled computation graphs, LeWi is the most sample-efficient method not given the target graph, with 27% lower mean learning-curve area than dense networks. Dense networks perform better on targets with a single high-arity function. LeWi's representations and connectivity align with the target computation. On 17 world-model prediction tasks spanning control, navigation, and manipulation, LeWi beats the dense baseline on 16 and wins 99/119 baseline comparisons. These results show that jointly learning connectivity and weights can exploit computational structure to improve sample efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Model- and Layer-Level Closed-Loop Forecasting with SSM-Based Spiking Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28495)**  `arXiv:2610.28495`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Erik Stolt, Sanja Karilanova, Ayca Ozcelikkale_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep State-Space Models (Deep SSMs) have shown promising performance and computational benefits in processing of time-series data with complex temporal dependencies. Spiking neural networks (SNNs) constitute a biologically inspired class of deep SSMs, where computing blocks communicate through spikes, discrete single-bit events rather than continuous activation functions. A particularly important task in time-series processing is forecasting, i.e. predicting future values of a sequence from past observations alone. However, how to best perform forecasting with SNNs is an open question, where the standard autoregressive nature of the data processing with SNNs requires values that are not available during the forecasting phase. In this article, we address this challenge. We explore two closed-loop forecasting strategies, in which the network's own generated values are fed back into the network as if they were observed past values: (i) model-level and (ii) layer-level closed-loop forecasting. We evaluate these approaches on the ETT-small datasets, a standard benchmark for time-series forecasting, under both spike-based and continuous activation functions. We compare the proposed closed-loop approaches against state-of-the-art methods such as SpaceTime, as well as the zero-masking baseline in S4. Both of the proposed approaches perform comparably to or better than zero-masking with S4, and in most cases match SpaceTime. These findings highlight the trade-off between continuous and low-bit quantized activations, and demonstrate that the proposed Deep SSM architectures provide a competitive framework for forecasting.
  </details>

- **[Rewards for Budget-Aware Multi-Turn Agents That Adapt to Arbitrary Test-Time Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28409)**  `arXiv:2610.28409`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tom A. Lamb, Adam Golinski, Eleonora Gualdoni, Sinead Williamson, Michael Kirchhof_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents are often given an inference budget and asked to maximise success within it. A multi-turn agent has to allocate this budget across many environment interactions. An external harness can enforce the budget, but it remains unclear whether the agent itself can learn when and how much to spend. We ask whether agents can learn this allocation across dynamically specified budgets and which rewards best teach this behaviour. We adapt existing length penalties to budget constraints spanning multiple resource types, introduce new reward formulations, and compare eleven objectives in a shared training pipeline. We train Qwen3.5 models at 4B and 9B on AppWorld with budgets over tokens, API calls, and environment steps, and on the BabyAI gridworld with turn budgets. We train a separate policy for each environment and resource type, with each policy trained across budget values. Training with rewards that penalise overspending substantially improves within-budget success and natural compliance over untrained models and over training on task success alone, with agents learning to adapt their resource use to stated budgets and task difficulty. These gains extend to unseen budgets and, zero-shot, to a shifted task distribution and a new benchmark. Reward objectives trade off success within budget and natural compliance. A sigmoid penalty for overspending most often achieves the highest within-budget success at an individual budget, while a fixed negative reward for overspending generally yields the strongest natural compliance. Combining penalties for overspend frequency and magnitude gives the smallest average gap to the best method at each budget. Overall, we show that a single agent can learn to allocate resources across turns and adapt its behaviour to user-specified budgets using budget feedback without external budget-allocation controllers.
  </details>

- **[Don't Skip the Baseline: A Controlled Comparison of Dynamic Expert Skipping in MoEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28369)**  `arXiv:2610.28369`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jakob Steimle, Thomas Elsken, Daniel Ehrhardt, Lukas Rinder, Daniel Cremers, Xi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A growing body of work proposes dynamic expert-skipping methods for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models that adjust the number of experts activated per token during inference, aiming to preserve model quality while further reducing the number of active parameters. These methods have added calibrated importance scores, inter-expert similarity metrics, or learned per-token allocators in order to identify experts to skip. However, different calibration regimes, different reductions in the effective number of experts, and evaluation suites make comparisons difficult. We classify four representative prior methods into a unified taxonomy and benchmark them against a natural, simple baseline: statically reducing the number of experts $k$ (i.e., same reduced $k$ across all layers and tokens). We provide a fair comparison of these methods at matched expert-compute budget, on two modern MoEs (Gemma4-26B-A4B and Qwen3-30B-A3B) in a text-only setting and a contemporary vision-language model (Qwen3-VL) in a multimodal setting. Surprisingly, we find that the simple baseline is hard to outperform consistently across models, modalities, benchmarks, and calibration settings. The methods we benchmark often underperform the baseline and achieved gains, when present, are minor. We further find that the effective $k$ achieved (or optimized for) in calibration can diverge from the effective $k$ in testing, resulting in over- or under-estimating the compute at inference time. Based on these findings, we recommend a calibration-free per-modality static $k$ reduction and validate this on a held-out model and modality. We further argue that a static control at matched measured compute is the baseline any dynamic skipping method must beat.
  </details>

- **[JAXbounce: A Continuous-Control Pong-Like Benchmark for Active Inference Beyond Low Dimensional Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28203)**  `arXiv:2610.28203`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Chaitanya Kaul, Markus Klar, Sebastian Stein, Fraser C. Paterson, John H. Williamson, Roderick Murray-Smith_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce JAXbounce, a continuous control pong-like benchmark designed to evaluate active inference beyond discrete, low dimensional environments. JAXbounce combines continuous states and stochastic dynamics in discrete time with flexible and composable observation models that allow representing the environment as a state vector or a pixel grid, corruptible through the rich family of noise models we provide. It is fully differentiable and parallelisable in JAX. We run over 10,000 agent simulations on our environment comparing a baseline deterministic solution, to a state-based active inference agent with Unscented Kalman Filter perception and expected free energy planning, and a pixel based agent whose measurement model is a pretrained neural network encoder. All agents are evaluated across three scenarios of increasing difficulty with eight different noise conditions. Our experiments characterise how performance of continuous state agents scales with planning, planning horizons, observation modalities and knowledge of the noise process, exhibiting preference-oriented behaviour even in the presence of increasing uncertainty. We release JAXbounce as a reproducible benchmark for continuous control active inference research.
  </details>

- **[The Shape of the DPO Hyperparameter Landscape: What to Measure, How It Moves with Scale, and When Not to Transfer It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27895)**  `arXiv:2610.27895`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hannan Mahadik, David Salinas, Jacek Golebiowski, Aaron Klein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct preference optimisation (DPO) is widely used for language model post-training, but its performance depends strongly on the choice of its hyperparameters, such as the learning rate and the reference-policy regularisation parameter $\beta$. To better understand the DPO hyperparameter landscape, we conduct extensive experiments using a dense hyperparameter grid across different model scales from the same family. This allows us to analyse how the optimal DPO hyperparameters evolve with model size and the number of update steps, with intermediate checkpoints providing an inexpensive signal to accelerate the hyperparameter optimisation process. Furthermore, by evaluating our models on both static and dynamic benchmarks, we identify which benchmarks provide the strongest signal for hyperparameter selection. Finally, we investigate how different state-of-the-art hyperparameter optimisation strategies perform in this setting. Our experiments show that the optimal learning rate varies with model size and that hyperparameters are not transferable across training datasets. We also find that validation loss is a poor proxy for hyperparameter optimisation, as hyperparameters that minimize validation loss only weakly correlate with optimal hyperparameters for downstream benchmarks. Therefore, DPO hyperparameters need to be tuned specifically for the target model, dataset, and evaluation objective. We also show that short candidate training runs can substantially reduce the cost of this selection. We release all our training configurations and evaluation results to support reproducible analysis of selection quality and training cost.
  </details>

- **[Physical-Variable-Informed Deep Learning for Robust Diagnosis in Industrial Fluid Transport Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27891)**  `arXiv:2610.27891`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Sha, Junqi Tao, Dixin Zhou, Horst Stoecker, Domagoj Vnucec, Andreas Widl, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial fluid transport systems are monitored through heterogeneous sensing streams, but robust fault diagnosis remains difficult because response signals vary with operating conditions and differ from physical variables in sampling rate, observation duration and data granularity. To address these challenges, we propose a physical-variable-informed deep learning framework (PhyNet) for robust fault diagnosis under real-world sampling heterogeneity. PhyNet treats physical variables as global operating constraints and response signals as local diagnostic evidence. Its physical-to-response cross-attention mechanism connects sequence-level physical-variable tokens with a learnable global response token, enabling interaction without strict temporal alignment. Across four benchmarks, including three real-world cavitation datasets and one public two-phase-flow dataset, PhyNet achieves the best performance across all evaluated metrics. Class-wise analyses, together with attention and masking analyses, show improved discrimination of ambiguous states and indicate that physical variables meaningfully constrain local response interpretation. These findings demonstrate a deployment-oriented approach to robust diagnosis in heterogeneous industrial environments.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free Continual Learning for Multimodal Forecasting via Prompt Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27468)**  `arXiv:2610.27468`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Laurent Mombaerts, Jonathan Taws, Jacopo Gargano, Dinesh Gaddam, Kevin George, Srijan Tiwari, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting a forecasting system to a changing world classically means retraining. We ask whether the task of continually learning regimes for time series forecasting can be represented by a piece of text: a guidance block, evolved by outcome-driven reflection and used in a frozen LLM forecaster's prompt. On a refreshed multimodal benchmark (weekly search indices, daily currencies, daily commodities, each window paired with web-retrieved news), we run GEPA-style prompt evolution under strict contamination control to prevent the data leakage common in timeseries benchmarks: the forecaster's knowledge cutoff predates every evaluated window. A 900-word evolved guidance carried by Llama-3.3-70B beats TimeSeries Foundation Models (TSFMs) on held-out data (GM MASE 0.776 vs. 0.898 for TabPFN-TS-3), transfers to more recent models without measurable loss, and costs dollars rather than GPU-days to update. We characterize the loop: out-of-loop gains concentrate in the first reflection, in-loop improvements overstate out-of-loop gains 2-3x unless an untouched monitor arbitrates, and error-only objectives silently suppress the text modality. We provide two extensions. First, compiling the guidance into executable code with a TSFM as a callable tool recovers most of the gain deterministically. Second, ablations show the advantage widens at short horizons and that removing the news text does not hurt the guided forecaster, consistent with the text's value being distilled into policy during learning. We argue that continual prompt evolution is a practical, training-free alternative to retraining for multimodal forecasting.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Graph Reasoning in Language Models with Learnable Graph Positional Encodings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27439)**  `arXiv:2610.27439`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Javier Porras-Valenzuela, Arush Arora, Alejandro Ribeiro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Solving reasoning tasks supported on graphs typically requires providing a Language Reasoning Model (LRM) with a serialized description of the graph. This approach results in the input length growing with the number of edges, which limits the size of the problems that can be solved. Furthermore, even when graphs fit in context, performance sharply decays as the graphs grow. We propose to address this by augmenting LRMs with scalable and permutation equivariant graph positional encodings that modulate attention logits. An additional post-fusion-style module incorporates structural information into hidden states. We compute these positional encodings using a GNN followed by a Graph Transformer. This eliminates the need for serialized adjacencies, reducing the context burden to $O(N)$. We jointly fine-tune the positional encoder and the LRM LoRA adapters using optimal solutions generated by graph-theoretic solvers, resulting in a computationally efficient method. Trained on small graphs, the model exhibits improved size-generalization across graph-theoretic tasks on graphs with up to 1,000 nodes, and produces fewer tokens than a conventional LRM. Additionally, we observe that our model remains robust to node relabelings. Evaluations on graph-theoretic benchmarks also show that performance on small graphs is preserved.
  </details>

- **[VPFlow: Value-Predictive Planning with Imperfect World Models for Offline Goal Reaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27280)**  `arXiv:2610.27280`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunzhe Hu, Dong Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning enables skill acquisition by reaching any goal from any state without dense rewards. Model-based methods allow for planning by scaling test-time compute to solve complex tasks. Combining both holds appealing promise for pre-training on suboptimal demonstrations and zero-shot adaptation via goal specification and planning. Yet existing methods assume effective planning requires accurate next-state prediction in pixel or latent space. In this work, we show that value prediction is more useful for planning, whereas accurate state prediction is often unnecessary. Our key insight is that offline goal reaching naturally admits a closed-form Monte Carlo return, enabling value regression to replace temporal-difference bootstrapping. We use this return as the sole supervision for latent world models: imagined latents are trained to predict goal-reaching value rather than match future states, implicitly preventing representation collapse. These planning-oriented representations let imperfect world models imagine on the level sets of value field beyond dataset transitions. We then present VPFlow, a value-predictive planner that pairs the world models with a one-step flow policy and plans in its noise space via evolutionary search. It enables policy improvement with imagined generalized advantage and reduces out-of-distribution queries common in action-space planning. On 30 state and pixel OGBench datasets, VPFlow surpasses model-free baselines by at least 30.3 and 10.1 percentage points in success rate, respectively, and outperforms latent planners LeWorldModel, PLDM, and TD-MPC2. It achieves this despite a latent prediction error orders of magnitude larger than with state prediction, while minimizing this error lowers success rate. This demonstrates that value-predictive representations, not exact state prediction, are what offline planning requires.
  </details>

- **[Exploration via Activation-Space Steering for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27012)**  `arXiv:2610.27012`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunan Zeng, Jinhao Jing, Xi Cheng, Yuwang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) trains a language model by sampling responses, scoring them with an automatic verifier, and updating the policy from the resulting rewards. The policy therefore receives direct learning signals only from trajectories that appear in its rollouts. Yet repeated sampling from the same policy can concentrate on similar reasoning paths, leaving useful alternatives unexplored under a fixed rollout budget. We introduce Activation-Space Exploration (ASE), an online method for diversifying training rollouts. By clustering activation differences between correct and incorrect responses to the same problem, ASE builds a structured bank of latent exploration vectors. Different vectors are then applied to subsequent rollouts to explore alternative reasoning paths. On Qwen3-1.7B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base trained on DAPO-Math-17k, ASE improves average Mean@8 across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks over GRPO by 4.87 and 1.87 percentage points, respectively, under the same rollout budget.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Policy Discrepancy: Capability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26950)**  `arXiv:2610.26950`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xuanfeng Yang, Le Gui, Jiaming Wang, Xuan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) integrates domain specialists into a shared student, but determining how strongly each teacher should supervise remains challenging. Existing discrepancy-based allocation can assign strong supervision even when the student already matches or exceeds the teacher's task performance, since behavioral mismatch does not necessarily imply a remaining capability deficit. We propose Capability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (CA-MOPD), which combines specialist merging with capability-guided allocation to account for what the student has already learned. CA-MOPD first merges reinforcement-learning specialists into a strong student initialization, then uses verifier-estimated teacher--student capability gaps to adjust domain-level distillation strength. Supervision decreases as capability gaps close and increases when estimated student capability regresses, while dense token-level guidance remains provided by the teachers. Across six benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, and instruction following, CA-MOPD achieves an average score of 32.22, outperforming naive MOPD, Open-MOPD, and the merged initialization by 0.86, 0.83, and 0.74 points, respectively. Controlled comparisons further support the benefits of merged initialization and capability-gap guidance under matched training budgets.
  </details>

- **[A Stronger Teacher Within: Strengthening MoE Models via On-Policy Bidirectional Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23185)**  `arXiv:2610.23185`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyu Chen, Jun Bai, Yang Liu, Jianfei Zhang, Wenge Rong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, but its outcome-level rewards provide only sparse supervision. On-Policy Distillation offers dense token-level feedback, yet requires a stronger compatible teacher, while on-policy self-distillation relies on hand-designed privileged information. We observe that the MoE architecture itself offers a way to resolve this dilemma, as increasing the activation budget per token exposes latent expert capacity and yields a potentially stronger teacher within the model. However, naive expansion degrades performance because newly co-activated experts are not optimized to work together.
