Moreover, they over-predict triggers that come with easy surface cues, and most of the passages they mark for these triggers are not triggers, yet carry the trigger's surface cue far more often than random text. These findings suggest that the evaluated LLMs respond to surface cues rather than to the mechanisms that make a passage manipulative. We release the schema, the codebook, standoff annotations, and the evaluation pipeline.
  </details>

- **[Polarity Blindness in LLM Safety: A Systematic Study of Negation, Antonym Substitution, and Adversarial Framing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27908)**  `arXiv:2610.27908`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Diego Miguel Lozano, Erik Derner, Juan Antonio Pérez-Ortiz, Nuria Oliver_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Negation remains a persistent challenge for language models across NLP tasks and modalities. We show that this weakness can undermine LLM safety guardrails through a structural vulnerability we term polarity blindness: the failure to recognize harmful intent after polarity-altering linguistic transformations. We study two forms of polarity inversion: grammatical negation and antonym replacement, and use synonym replacement as a polarity-preserving control. We further test whether an adversarial textual wrapper amplifies these failures. Using a 260-prompt benchmark across 10 state-of-the-art LLMs and 3 languages (English, Spanish, Czech), stratified by harmfulness potential and evaluated on a validated 0-5 harm scale, we find that polarity blindness affects most evaluated models, with mean harm scores reaching 4.12 for the most vulnerable model. The effect persists across typologically differing negation systems, suggesting that polarity blindness reflects a broader limitation in semantic robustness rather than a language- or grammar-specific failure.
  </details>

- **[CollabSciReM: Aligning Language Models for Scientific Conversations via Science-Aware Reward Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27901)**  `arXiv:2610.27901`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sasi Kiran Gaddipati, Gollam Rabby, Sören Auer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as research collaborators for hypothesis refinement, methodological planning, and technical analysis across multi-turn interactions. In this setting, a useful assistant must be accurate, methodologically justified, logically consistent, and token-efficient. Current alignment approaches rarely achieve all of these together. Instruction-tuned models gain conversational quality through verbosity, and we show that science-agnostic general collaborative rewards compress responses at the expense of scientific rigor. We propose CollabSciReM, a goal-conditioned reward framework that decomposes the session-level reward into conversational quality, scientific rigor, and structural efficiency. We also introduce SciQuality, a pairwise evaluation of multi-turn research conversations grounded in 300 held-out scientific papers and scored on six dimensions: methodological soundness, justification, logical consistency, factual correctness, novelty, and responsiveness. Fine-tuned on 8.62K synthetic research conversations, CollabSciReM is preferred on SciQuality over a science-agnostic reward baseline trained on the same data for all four Llama and Qwen models, including a 65.3% win rate at matched response length. For the 7B and 8B models, CollabSciReM outperforms the science-agnostic reward baseline on the primary metric in 8 of 10 single- and multi-turn benchmark settings. In a blind study, domain experts rated CollabSciReM significantly higher on novelty than the general-reward variant and reported fewer hallucinated responses than both the general-reward variant and the base model.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Calibration: Temporal LLM Confidence Assessment Across Languages and Local Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27702)**  `arXiv:2610.27702`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sahil Kale_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are expected to estimate their own uncertainty, with confidence signals used to guide retrieval, adaptive compute allocation, routing, and selective abstention. We investigate whether confidence signals measured at different temporal stages of problem solving encode distinct forms of epistemic information and remain reliable beyond English-centric settings. Inspired by classical meta-memory theory, we distinguish anticipatory pre-solve confidence (Feeling-of-Knowing, FOK) from retrospective post-solve confidence (Judgement-of-Learning, JOL). We evaluate these signals across two frontier LLMs, multiple reasoning domains, translated Marathi benchmarks, and a newly constructed benchmark of genuinely local Marathi factual questions. Across settings, the cheaper pre-solve signal remains weakly calibrated, while post-solve confidence generally provides stronger epistemic alignment despite its additional inference cost. Confidence behaviour transfers surprisingly well under direct language translation, with calibration trends largely preserved between English and Marathi, but degrades substantially on local Marathi knowledge, suggesting that linguistic transfer alone is insufficient for region-specific information needs. Finally, hallucinated responses maintain persistently elevated confidence after answer generation, highlighting important limitations of current LLM self-assessment mechanisms.
  </details>

- **[ABench: Adversarily Benchmarking Large Language Models across Taxonomized Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27611)**  `arXiv:2610.27611`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Liping Yang, Yongqi Leng, Jingting Zheng, Yuqi Ren, Shaolin Zhu, Deyi Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ensuring the alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) with diverse cultural values is critical for their safe global deployment. However, existing alignment benchmarks are predominantly English-centric or confined to simple discriminative tasks. Moreover, they often fail to effectively evaluate model robustness against complex, adversarial inductions within specific cultural contexts. To address these limitations, we propose ABench, a fine-grained, handcrafted benchmark specifically tailored to taxonomized values in Chinese. Grounded in a rigorous hierarchical taxonomy comprising 158 derived value subcategories, ABench consists of 2,895 open-ended questions. These questions employ techniques such as role-playing and logical traps to probe the stability of models' stances in adversarially induced contexts. Furthermore, we propose a weighted automated evaluation framework that utilizes question-specific scoring criteria for nuanced assessment. Extensive experiments on 16 mainstream LLMs reveal that most models exhibit significant vulnerabilities when subjected to sophisticated adversary attacks on values (scoring below 60). This underscores the need for more robust, culturally specific alignment strategies. Our data will be publicly released to promote further research on taxonomized value alignment.
  </details>

- **[WORTH Benchmark: Evidence Discovery under Not-Fully-Cooperative Information -- A Two-Episode Pilot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27597)**  `arXiv:2610.27597`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Bo Zou, Chao Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing reasoning evaluations typically provide task-relevant information to models as available premises, rarely examining concurrently how models discover evidence autonomously and how information presentation within narratives guides or obscures judgment. This paper proposes the WORTH Benchmark: models read original mystery narratives without clue annotations, autonomously select key clue sentences, identify case participants, and explain their reasoning process. The narrative materials are designed by professional authors such that evidence facilitating reasoning coexists with narrative arrangements that delay the revelation of truth. We report a pilot experiment under full-story conditions across two episodes, conducting a total of 70 formal runs on models, of which 67 runs are content-scorable. Scoring separately records hits on author-designated key clue sentences and participant selections; natural language explanations are used for case diagnosis and are not scored independently. The results show that clue hits and participant judgments are not synchronized; across 37 content-scorable runs in Episode 02, 31 runs simultaneously selected the same set of incorrect participants, while adjacent suspicion-oriented and exoneration-oriented sentences were selected 25 times and 0 times, respectively. These phenomena illustrate the diagnostic value of component-wise analysis and suggest that not-fully-cooperative informational relationships may influence evidence selection and judgment. This study only implements full-story conditions for two episodes, and does not establish stable model rankings or infer general mechanisms on this basis; human player records have not yet been cleaned, and this version does not report human-model comparisons.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Context Compaction on the Questions That Come Later](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27301)**  `arXiv:2610.27301`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Junwei Yao, Yongxi Zhou, Zike Yang, Zhijun Wang, Jianan Liu, Yanxian Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model agents compact their context for the question at hand, but the compacted context must also serve questions that come later. We evaluate compaction on these later questions, using four corpora that annotate the evidence of several questions per text and recorded agent trajectories, and compare every method with random deletion at the same budget. On MultiRC, keeping the sentences that gpt-6-luna (through the OpenAI Decisions API) judges necessary for the current question raises its accuracy on that question by 11.4 points at 25% of the tokens. On the later questions, however, no method that selects sentences for the current question does detectably better than deleting sentences at random. Deleting words instead of sentences does better: LLMLingua-2, which ignores the question, beats random sentence deletion at the same token count by 12.5 points on the later MultiRC questions. It keeps about twice random deletion's share of the evidence's content words, and at equal content-word retention its gain shrinks to 7.3 points (exploratory). On FEVER, label accuracy can overstate what the kept text supports: the gain of sentence selection is no longer detected when answers are scored against the kept evidence, and the 21.0-point gain of LLMLingua-2 is not shown to rest on the kept text. The ranking also depends on the setting: on Evidence Inference sentence selection beats LLMLingua-2 at a nominal 25% budget, and on $\tau$-bench trajectories, whose later tool calls need exact identifiers, it beats random deletion while LLMLingua-2 shows no detected gain. Scores on the question a compaction was made for do not reliably show how it serves later ones.
  </details>

- **[MMedAgent-Plus: Scaling Multimodal Medical Agents across Modalities, Tasks and Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27233)**  `arXiv:2610.27233`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruoxin Wang, Yuanting Pan, Binxu Li, Ruiyang Ji, Yaqian Chen, Jiayuan Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical workflows often require multiple interdependent analyses, but existing specialist and generalist AI models are not designed to coordinate them. We introduce MMedAgent-Plus, a multimodal medical tool-use agent that combines a vision-language planner with 18 leading specialist models spanning 12 tasks and 8 modalities. Through a unified tool interface, the planner selects specialists, executes them in sequence, and synthesizes responses grounded in their outputs. The planner is instruction-tuned on 208,928 samples from 32 public medical benchmarks, covering single- and multi-tool interactions across single and multiple rounds. We separately evaluate tool orchestration, response synthesis, and end-to-end execution on held-out samples. MMedAgent-Plus achieves tool-call accuracies of 91.0%, 98.1%, and 80.7% in single-tool multi-round, multi-tool multi-round, and multi-tool single-round settings, respectively. Given correct tool outputs, MMedAgent-Plus achieves scores of 14.4-14.8 out of 15 for faithfulness, completeness, and conciseness across 600 evaluation samples. In end-to-end execution without pre-supplied tool outputs, MMedAgent-Plus correctly invokes the required specialist in 90.6% of 350 evaluation cases spanning seven tasks. Its final-answer accuracy reaches 0.98, matching the dedicated leading specialist and exceeding the strongest baseline (0.28).
  </details>

- **[LoWAgent: A Low-Resource Multilingual Benchmark for Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27205)**  `arXiv:2610.27205`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ocean Monjur, Daud Ahmad Nisar, Shahriar Kabir Nahin, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Sushruti Mishra, Israel Abebe Azime, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web agents are typically evaluated on English-language interfaces, leaving their ability to operate across the world's multilingual websites largely unexplored. This gap is especially significant for low-resource languages, where standardized resources for evaluating web agents remain limited. To address this gap, we introduce Low-Resource Web Agent Benchmark (LoWAgent), a benchmark for evaluating web agents across different low-resource languages (LRLs) in comparison to the default, English. LoWAgent evaluates web agents across English and four LRLs: Hindi, Bengali, Urdu, and Amharic. The benchmark comprises of six fully localized websites, instantiated across five languages to yield 3,000 task instances. We evaluate three closed-source and five open-source models across all five languages on all six websites. Our findings show that both closed-source and open-source models experience performance degradation for LRLs compared to English, with substantially greater performance drops observed among the open-source model agents. We further evaluate the robustness of the models on realistic interface disruptions, such as cookie notices and advertisement pop-ups, and find that these further degrade agentic performance. Finally, we show that post-training on a small subset of LoWAgent traces can significantly improve low-resource language agentic task performance, demonstrating the usefulness of our benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Lingual Reusability of LLM Pipelines: Financial Extraction, Multilingual Reasoning, and Semantic Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27043)**  `arXiv:2610.27043`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Joong Ho, Choi, Sherine George, Jiayang Zhao, Jayesh Rathi, Ashwin Kandath, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial institutions increasingly deploy large language models (LLMs) in multilingual markets, raising a practical question: can pipelines developed and validated in English be reused across languages, or must they be rebuilt per locale? We test cross-lingual reusability along three dimensions--accuracy, reasoning-effort transfer, and behavioural stability--through three complementary tasks. Task 1 (cheque extraction) and Task 2 (Mintaka QA with pinned evidence across nine languages and nine model-effort configurations) test whether accuracy and effort settings transfer; Task 3 (FinanceBench pilot, 50 items over SEC 10-K filings in four languages) validates whether these patterns generalise to financial content. Accuracy reusability is broadly confirmed: Gemini 3.5 Flash achieves 95.2-97.6% extraction accuracy across prompt languages, and FinanceBench per-language means span 5.8pp (77.1-82.9%), though the language-difficulty ranking reverses between domains (Hindi is worst on Mintaka, best on FinanceBench at 82.9%). Effort reusability is domain-dependent: higher reasoning effort yields marginal gains on general-knowledge QA but up to 8pp on financial content, and effort configurations that help in English help proportionally in other languages. Behavioural reusability holds: citation-set overlap with English averages 77-79% Jaccard, with no single language consistently worse. Together, these results support qualified reuse of English-built pipelines, but per-domain, per-language validation remains necessary--neither semantic similarity nor self-reported confidence reliably predicts cross-lingual accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Muse Glimmer 30B: An On-Device Agentic Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24628)**  `arXiv:2610.24628`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Arash Ebrahim Abtahizadeh, Muna Aghamelu, Adil Ahmed, Juan C. Angeles Acuna, Alexis Audran-Reiss, Asaf Avrahamy, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Muse Glimmer, an open-source 30B parameter causal language model with perception encoder, purpose-built for autonomous agentic tasks on consumer hardware. Muse Glimmer integrates multi-step reasoning, reliable tool use and failure recovery into a single model that runs locally on consumer devices without requiring cloud infrastructure. The model accepts text and image inputs and produces text outputs, was trained on data from over a hundred languages, and is quantized to approximately 4-bit precision for efficient single-GPU inference. Muse Glimmer achieves state-of-the-art performance for its size class on agentic benchmarks, setting a new standard compared to the models of its size: Gemma4-31B and Qwen3.6-27B on tool use, multi-step planning, and agentic task completion. We release the model under the Apache 2.0 license to foster open-source development of capable, safe, local AI agents.
  </details>

- **[StartLux-Decision: AutoThink Decision Models with Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Deliberative Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17309)**  `arXiv:2610.17309`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xialie Zhuang, Zunhai Su, Jia Pan, Ngai Wong, Guanhua Chen, Chuan-Wei Kuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce StartLux-Decision, decision models from 0.8B to 27B parameters that combine efficient single-pass prediction with adaptive, confidence-guided reasoning. The Instant models use bidirectional attention in full-attention layers to improve decision accuracy while keeping single-pass inference. The AutoThink models invoke explicit reasoning for uncertain decisions and answer confident cases directly. A three-stage training pipeline establishes shared decision capabilities, specializes the two variants, and equips AutoThink with reasoning through reasoning supervision and reinforcement learning. We introduce Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Deliberative Decisions (RLCD$^2$), which uses a frozen model and proper scoring rules to reward improvements in decision probability quality and applies a soft penalty to overlong reasoning traces. RLCD$^2$ raises the share of reasoning traces that terminate within the generation limit by up to 48 percentage points and cuts average reasoning length by up to 65%, while largely preserving decision accuracy. On the public subset of Decision Index 0.3, the Instant models outperform the strongest evaluated comparable-scale open-weight decision models by 7.4-16.9 points, and the 27B AutoThink model reaches 73.4. We also introduce DecisionPivot, a programmatically verifiable benchmark of computational decisions built from contrast pairs that differ in a single answer-changing detail. On DecisionPivot, AutoThink outperforms its Instant counterparts by 11.5-18.4 points in pair accuracy and leads among evaluated systems. The models can be run using frameworks such as vLLM and MLX, with quantized versions available to reduce memorythis http URLcode and links to model weights are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Constructing Challenging Browser-Use Tasks by Controlled Environment Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35814)**  `arXiv:2609.35814`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xunjian Yin, Tianchen Guan, Jinao Wang, Weili Cao, Daisy Xinlei Lin, Royce Cheng-Yue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As browser-use agents improve, benchmarks keep pace by adding new tasks, websites, and applications. Doing so is costly, and because many factors change at once, it is unclear what makes the new tasks harder. In this work, we instead make existing tasks harder by changing the environment in which they are performed, so that difficulty becomes a programmable property of the environment. We introduce BreakingWeb, in which each task is run twice: once on an unmodified website and once under an intervention, a controlled change to the website's content, server state, network responses, or rendered page. Each intervention is reproducible, keeps the user instruction and success criterion fixed, can be detected and worked around by a competent user, and is labeled with the behavior that recovery from it mainly requires, such as verification. BreakingWeb contains $519$ task pairs on seven self-hosted websites, and each run is scored on the website's final backend state. We evaluate six strong browser-use agents, three agents that observe only screenshots, and human participants. Interventions lower the pass rates of the six browser-use agents by $22.9$ percentage points on average, and each agent fails on nearly half of the tasks it solves on the unmodified website. Humans are affected much less: their pass rate drops by $10.0$ points on the first attempt and by $5.7$ points when they repeat the task. Most failures of the six agents ($75\%$) are \emph{belief failures}, in which the agent declares the run finished although a required change was never made. Our code, data, and environments are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[An Empirical Study of Development-Tuned Representation Sensitivity for Medical QA Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09684)**  `arXiv:2609.09684`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuexin Wu, Dayou Yu, Vasile Rus_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study medical question-answering (QA) data selection when a developer already has questions, labels, and auxiliary explanatory texts but can retain only $B$ examples for a final LoRA update. Representation-Sensitivity Prioritization (RSP) first restricts the candidate bank to its top-$4B$ predictive-entropy examples, then combines the frequency of answer flips with non-flipping contraction of the top-two answer margin under hidden-state perturbations. We evaluate this operational score on five medical QA datasets with google/medgemma-4b-it, $B=128$, and three seed branches. After removing four MedExpQA test questions that also occur in its acquisition pool, dataset-specific development-tuned RSP attains the highest observed five-dataset mean accuracy (53.44%) and Macro-F1 (48.21%) among the evaluated selectors. Relative to random selection within the same entropy-restricted pool, the descriptive differences are +1.49 and +2.10 percentage points, respectively; paired 95% bootstrap intervals include zero. The audit mixes benchmarks with different relationships to documented backbone post-training sources, and the same test cohorts had been used in earlier analyses before the expanded tuning grid was designed. Results are therefore exploratory, modest, and dataset-dependent rather than evidence of statistical superiority. RSP point estimates also exceed an archived no-update reference, but unpinned model revisions and incomplete historical input identifiers limit attribution of that difference to fine-tuning. Because every selected training example includes auxiliary text and no matched answer-only control is evaluated, the experiments also do not identify a causal benefit from auxiliary-text conditioning itself.
  </details>

- **[What a One-Hour Interview Does Not Tell an LLM About You: Interviews Elicit Traits, Not Decision Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29458)**  `arXiv:2605.29458`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruoxi Su, Yuhan Liu, Jingyu Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly asked to stand in for particular people, and interviewing the person is the natural way to tell a model who they are. We test what a one-hour LLM-led adaptive interview tells a model about an individual's value-laden decisions. 46 adults completed such an interview and then answered 25 dilemma questions, which are the ground truth. We predict their answers from different parts of the transcript, compare adaptive follow-up questions with fixed ones on the same participants, and benchmark every variant against a model given no information about the person and against the majority answer. No part of the interview is reliably better than either baseline, and adaptive follow-ups are no better than fixed ones. Two audits of the transcripts show why. For 85% of the errors an auditor finds no evidence that distinguishes the person's choice from the alternatives, and where a transcript does contain related evidence, the option it favours matches the person's own answer less often than the majority answer does. The same transcripts do improve recovery of the person's MBTI type. Interviews of this kind elicit traits, not decision policies. We discuss what decision-aware elicitation would require and why persona agents should not be delegated value-laden decisions, and we release the consenting participants' transcripts with all predictions.
  </details>

- **[CHIMERA: Compact Synthetic Data for Generalizable LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00889)**  `arXiv:2603.00889`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Zhu, Yihao Feng, Yanchao Sun, Xianzhi Du, Pingzhi Li, Olli Saarikivi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have exhibited remarkable reasoning capabilities, largely enabled by supervised fine-tuning (SFT)- and reinforcement learning (RL)-based post-training on high-quality reasoning data. However, reproducing and extending these capabilities in open and scalable settings is hindered by three data-centric challenges: (1) the cold-start problem, arising from the lack of seed datasets with detailed, long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) trajectories needed to initialize reasoning policies; (2) limited domain coverage, as most existing open-source reasoning datasets are concentrated in mathematics, with limited coverage of broader scientific disciplines; and (3) the annotation bottleneck, where the difficulty of frontier-level reasoning tasks makes reliable human annotation prohibitively expensive or infeasible. To address these challenges, we introduce CHIMERA, a compact synthetic reasoning dataset of 9K samples designed to support generalizable reasoning across domains. CHIMERA is constructed with three key properties: (1) it provides rich, long CoT reasoning trajectories synthesized by state-of-the-art reasoning models; (2) it has broad and structured coverage, spanning 8 major scientific disciplines and over 1K fine-grained topics organized via a model-generated hierarchical taxonomy; and (3) it employs a fully automated, scalable evaluation pipeline that uses strong reasoning models to cross-validate both problem validity and answer correctness, removing the reliance on human annotations. We use CHIMERA to post-train a 4B Qwen3 model with SFT and RL. Despite the dataset's modest size, the resulting model achieves strong performance on a suite of challenging reasoning benchmarks, including GPQA-Diamond, AIME 24/25/26, HMMT 25 and Humanity's Last Exam, approaching or matching the performance of substantially larger models such as DeepSeek-R1 and Qwen3-235B.
  </details>

- **[Attention-Aligned Reasoning for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03223)**  `arXiv:2510.03223`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hongxiang Zhang, Yuan Tian, Tianyi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) tend to generate a long reasoning chain when solving complex tasks. However, as the reasoning chain extends, critical intermediate steps and the original prompt will be buried in the context, receiving insufficient attention and leading to errors. In this work, we present ATAR, a novel reasoning method that leverages the inherent reasoning structure to steer LLM attention. Our experiments show that ATAR outperforms SOTA methods across six benchmarks, achieving up to 15.39% absolute improvement. Furthermore, with ATAR, "non-reasoning" models achieve comparable or even better performance compared to reasoning models of the same size in most benchmarks. Finally, our ablation studies show that the attention alignment component contributes significantly, and that these improvements are persist under different attentionsteering backends.
  </details>

- **[NarraSpace: Multimodal Scene-Grounded Evidence for Picture-Description Dementia Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28616)**  `arXiv:2610.28616`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qingming Li, Ruiyan Sun, Satoshi Nakamura_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Picture-description dementia classification uses a shared visual scene, but a transcript-level prediction alone does not identify the scene evidence underlying its inputs. NarraSpace links words and speech times to named picture elements and retrieves utterances for predefined scene events. Narrative-spatial descriptors summarize coverage and temporal organization. A frozen language model contrasts each event's text under examples from Alzheimer's disease and healthy-control training participants, alongside transcript surprisal and coverage measurements. Retained source records link event scores to their source utterances, speech intervals, and lexical matches. On the official 48-participant test set of the Alzheimer's Dementia Recognition through Spontaneous Speech benchmark, a fixed support vector classifier achieves $80.88\%\pm2.56\%$ mean accuracy over fifty seeds; averaged probabilities yield $81.25\%$ accuracy and $91.32\%$ area under the receiver operating characteristic curve. NarraSpace makes the construction of scene-linked classification inputs explicit and inspectable by connecting event-indexed language-model measurements to their source utterances, speech timing, and extraction records. Such source-linked measurements could support clinician review of picture descriptions by presenting quantitative summaries alongside the original speech evidence.
  </details>

- **[Cache Engineering for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28577)**  `arXiv:2610.28577`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianyu Fu, Ruoxi Wang, Zihan Min, Zhongqi Ren, Zhihao Li, Tengxuan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents can handle increasingly complex tasks, navigating long trajectories of observations and actions. As these trajectories grow, key-value (KV) cache I/O and storage become the dominant cost. Trajectories can also exceed the LLM's maximum context length, requiring control over active cache growth. Prior work uses context engineering to reduce cache size by trimming or summarizing history. However, modifying the trajectory prefix invalidates subsequent cached states and triggers costly re-prefilling of retained context. Task accuracy can also suffer when too little context is retained to limit re-prefill costs. We propose cache engineering as a new scheme. It allows agents to directly control their active KV cache without modifying the trajectory or re-prefilling retained context. Specifically, agents can proactively use drop to exclude selected cache from future attention and repos to adjust retained keys' position encodings. We formalize these cache operations and identify history dependence as a serving challenge: identical token sequences can yield different cache states under different cache operation sequences, making prefix matching insufficient for correct cache reuse. Thus, we co-design the AKV serving engine. It provides a stateless interface compatible with existing serving APIs, while internally managing stateful cache reuse, eviction, and restoration. Experiments show that a tool-response rolling-drop cache policy improves average benchmark scores by 1.7 points and reduces average cost by 27.7%, compared with conventional threshold-triggered summarization. With AKV, it achieves up to 1.2x the output throughput and a 1.5x speedup in P95 request latency. Recursive self-improvement of cache policies further reduces cost by 7.1% over expert design. Our code is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OptionWorld: A Benchmark for Diagnosing Failures of LLMs as Long-Term Personal Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28491)**  `arXiv:2610.28491`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruoyu Zhang, Zhaocen Liu, Weiyi Tian, Melissa Feng, Shilong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term assistants must follow unfinished tasks across days as information and circumstances change. Finding information or starting an action, however, does not ensure that the task gets completed. We introduce OptionWorld, a benchmark that traces information acquisition, judgments, actions, and outcomes in simulated daily-life tasks to identify where current LLMs fall short as long-term personal assistants. In runs with archived decision inputs, the key facts remain in the model's last input before the deadline in 35.5% of failed instances. Failure analysis identifies the three most frequent problems: needed information is not obtained or combined, available opportunities to act go unused, and agents impose restrictions that the task does not require. Three follow-up experiments show that supplying missing facts restores task success in some cases; action reminders can restart work while subsequent steps still miss the deadline; and correcting a supplied note that wrongly forbids contact restores permitted inquiry. These findings locate shortcomings in maintaining task state: connecting facts to unfinished work, following through before deadlines, and separating actual restrictions from the agent's assumptions.
  </details>

- **[Asymmetric Looped Transformers: Fixed Global Representations for an Iterative Decoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28489)**  `arXiv:2610.28489`  `cs.CL`  
  _Cheng Liang, Tengfei Zhang, Wenjie DU, Jianyu Wu, Changlian Ma, Shuchen Pu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying shared layers, offering a way to scale computation without increasing parameter count. However, full-stack looping places context construction and iterative state refinement within the same recurrent computation, without explicitly distinguishing their roles. We introduce the Asymmetric Looped Transformer (AsymLoop), which separates single-pass context encoding from recurrent state refinement. A single-pass causal encoder produces layer-specific Global key-value (KV) caches that remain fixed across iterations, while a weight-tied recurrent decoder generates Local KV from its evolving state. Joint attention over both sources under a single softmax allows each update to integrate persistent contextual information with the current intermediate state. For training, we apply a heavy-tail-guided layerwise learning-rate method to the encoder and decoder. Experiments on FineWeb-Edu across four model scales, from 60M to 1B, show consistently lower validation perplexity and higher average downstream accuracy than Full Loop under matched effective depth and training token budgets. At 1B, AsymLoop improves average zero-shot accuracy across seven commonsense reasoning benchmarks from 49.58% to 50.85%. Further experiments extend these benefits to longer training budgets, an OLMo-style backbone, and sparse mixture-of-experts models. These results support asymmetric context encoding and state refinement as an effective design for recurrent language models.
  </details>

- **[When Copying Is Hard: Copy-Constrained Decoding for Exact Span Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28393)**  `arXiv:2610.28393`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jinghui Zhang, Lang Gao, Zongfang Liu, Ruihong Zeng, Zirui Song, Rui Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precisely reproducing a target span verbatim within a long context is a fundamental capability test for LLMs: even when the target string is present in context, models often still drift within the span or fail to stop correctly, revealing limitations in source grounding and boundary control. This capability has broad practical relevance. For instance, code generation often requires embedding sensitive strings such as API keys exactly as they appear in context. In this work, we construct a benchmark to test exact span reproduction ability across contracts, scientific literature, code, and random sequences, and find that current models still make surprisingly frequent errors. To address this, we propose CopyGen, a lightweight copy-constrained plugin for frozen LLMs that turns copying from a byproduct of next-token prediction into a controlled process. Our key idea is to decompose copying into three distinct decisions: when to copy, how far to copy, and when to stop. Concretely, CopyGen first determines whether to enter copy mode, then predicts a tentative copy length, and finally verifies the generated span token by token. During verification, we adaptively adjust the logits of copy continuation tokens to incentivize or suppress continuation, and emit only the longest valid prefix. Across three LLM backbones and four benchmark tasks, CopyGen improves average exact match by 35% relative without model finetuning, and integrates seamlessly with SFT to further improve it by 4%, while substantially accelerating generation. These results suggest that reliable exact copying is not something LLMs obtain for free, but a decoding-time control problem that requires explicit modeling. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Abstraction to Action: DSLs for Natural Language Abstraction Interpretation and Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28311)**  `arXiv:2610.28311`  `cs.CL`  
  _Amit Lisha, Reut Tsarfaty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural-language instructions often rely on high-level abstractions, such as asking an agent to ``stack blocks in a pyramid.'' Interpreting abstract instructions requires models to both identify the abstraction intended by the speaker, and unroll it into the many concrete actions that instantiate it. Language models struggle here: prior work has shown that performance degrades as the level of abstraction in the instruction rises. To bridge the gap between natural language abstraction and concrete actions, we propose to borrow from coding environments the notion of a {\em domain-specific language} (DSL), an explicit representation layer whose documented primitives name recurring abstractions that expand into concrete actions. We demonstrate the efficacy of DSLs for NL interpretation on \textsc{Hexagons}, a visually-grounded instruction-following task graded for abstraction. We further ask whether language models can induce such DSLs themselves from domain data. Our results show that while code generation consistently improves over direct inference, code generation over a human-curated DSL yields dramatic gains, up to 10 absolute F1 points, reaching the human skyline, with gains concentrated at {\em higher} abstraction levels. We confirm this pattern on the \textsc{Text2CAD} benchmark. At the same time, model-induced DSLs recover only part of this gain, remaining inconsistent across runs and short of their human-curated counterpart. Overall, the right representation closes much of the gap caused by high abstraction --- but inducing that layer of representation automatically is where models still fall short, and where we see a strong opportunity for the community to make progress.
  </details>

- **[UvA-MT's Participation in the WMT26 General Translation Shared Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27852)**  `arXiv:2610.27852`  `cs.CL`  
  _Di Wu, Sergey Troshin, Wafaa Mohammed, Seth Aycock, Evgeniia Tokarchuk, Vlad Niculae, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents UvA-MT's submission to the WMT 2026 General Machine Translation shared task, competing in the unconstrained track across all 23 translation directions. This year, we fully leverage the test-time methods of Large Language Models (LLMs) for machine translation. Specifically, (1) we use sequential sampling with a refinement prompt to generate a pool of translation candidates for each source segment, a strategy shown to outperform traditional parallel sampling; and (2) we employ LLMs as pairwise judges to select the best candidate via a round-robin voting mechanism, with offline evaluation on the WMT25 benchmark showing a very clear improvement over point-wise best-of-N selection. Combined, these two strategies form our WMT26 submission.
  </details>

- **[SciDiagBench: A Type-Specific Benchmark for Human-Aligned Evaluation of Scientific Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27841)**  `arXiv:2610.27841`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hao Wang, Zixuan Li, Long Bai, Xiaolong Jin, Jiafeng Guo, Tat-Seng Chua, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in generating images from textual prompts, motivating their utilization for scientific diagram generation. Scientific diagrams naturally span diverse types, each characterized by distinct visual structures and content organization. Consequently, during peer review and other scientific evaluation, human experts naturally consider type-specific criteria when assessing their quality. However, existing VLM-based evaluation benchmarks fail to reflect the above human evaluation practices for two main reasons: 1) benchmark datasets typically lack a systematic categorization of different diagram types and thus adopt the same generic criteria for all diagrams, and 2) their evaluation criteria are often designed heuristically, without explicit alignment to human evaluation preferences. Motivated by these limitations, we introduce SciDiagBench, which consists of a benchmark dataset that systematically categorizes scientific diagrams into 11 types and a human preference-guided evaluation method. Specifically, SciDiagBench contains 165 scientific diagrams across 10 broad domains, with types including graphical abstracts, technical roadmaps, and illustrative cases. Based on this dataset, SciDiagBench develops an evaluation method that leverages a preference-guided calibration process to incorporate human preferences into the generation of type-specific criteria. Ultimately, SciDiagBench enables more reliable automated evaluation that better aligns with human evaluation across diverse scientific diagrams.
  </details>

- **[Tracing Recurrent and Emergent Hallucination Patterns in Decomposed Text-to-SQL Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27720)**  `arXiv:2610.27720`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hayden Moore, Abhinav Verma, Anshuman Dutt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can achieve strong Text-to-SQL benchmark performance while still producing schema and query-logic errors. Most evaluations characterize such failures only in the final generated query, making it difficult to determine whether a related error was also observed in an earlier, simpler step or appears only at the final stage. We present a drill-down evaluation framework that decomposes Text-to-SQL queries into progressively more complex executable sub-queries and corresponding natural-language questions, allowing rule-based hallucination categories to be examined across a generation path. We distinguish recurrent hallucinations, in which the same rule-based category is observed in an earlier step and again in the final query, from emergent hallucinations, in which the category is observed at the final step without an earlier detection in the same path. Across five LLMs evaluated on an expanded BIRD-mini benchmark, several categories show appreciable recurrence probabilities, while clause abuse shows comparatively higher conditional emergence. Under the conversational-history condition, recurrence probabilities are generally higher and emergence probabilities are generally lower. These observations indicate that the two conditional measures do not move uniformly in the same direction under additional context and motivate trajectory-level analysis in addition to final-output evaluation. Code and evaluation artifacts are available in the project repository.this https URL
  </details>

- **[A Two-Way Street: Surveying the Interplay between Code-Mixing and Native Languages in Text and Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27631)**  `arXiv:2610.27631`  `cs.CL`  
  _Debajyoti Mazumder, Akash Anil_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Systems for code-mixed (CM) text and speech rarely start from scratch. CM data is scarce, so most work borrows from the languages being mixed, which we call native languages. This transfer can also run in reverse, when CM data is used to improve models for the native languages. Existing surveys focus on processing CM itself, and none brings the two directions together. This paper fills that gap with a systematic survey of transfer in both directions, across text and speech. We organise the surveyed work in a taxonomy of transfer direction, route and modality. Through this taxonomy, we find that most transfer runs from native languages into the CM setting. The reverse direction is younger, and in it code-switching mainly helps by aligning the two languages. Text and speech work share methods but rarely cite each other. On a shared speech benchmark, native data helps more than new architectures. These findings point to open challenges, such as how much native and CM data to mix in training, and how to evaluate CM text written in different scripts.
  </details>

- **[Self-Decomposing Diffusion for Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27600)**  `arXiv:2610.27600`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lize Shao, Zichen Xie, Zhizhen Chen, Zhe Zeng, Wenxi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) are emerging as a promising paradigm for code generation, offering the potential for fast, parallel decoding. However, generating complex programs remains challenging, as their interdependent components must maintain consistent interfaces, data flows, and shared assumptions. Aggressive parallel decoding can compromise these dependencies and undermine program correctness, while conservative decoding sacrifices efficiency. To address this trade-off, we propose planning program decomposition before implementation to coordinate interdependent units during parallel generation. We introduce Self-Decomposing Diffusion for Code (SDDC), a novel framework that decomposes complex programs and implements their units in parallel, entirely through diffusion. SDDC first generates a contract specifying the program's decomposition and inter-unit dependencies, then uses it to guide coordinated parallel implementation. Across five benchmarks and two 7B diffusion backbones, SDDC substantially improves the accuracy--latency trade-off, raising pass@1 by up to 31.1 percentage points while decoding 7.5--13.7$\times$ faster than the base models, and outperforming a fine-tuning baseline by up to 23.5 points with lower latency.
  </details>

- **[TeCoP: Accelerating Diffusion Large Language Model Decoding via Tentative Context Pipelining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27583)**  `arXiv:2610.27583`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jui-Chien Tsou, Hsuan-Chi Liu, Wei-Chin Wang, Yu-Syuan Xu, Chun-Yi Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) predict multiple token positions per forward pass, yet block-wise decoders serialize generation and repeatedly recompute stable predictions. We attribute this inefficiency to coupling a prediction's readiness for irreversible commitment with its utility as context. We introduce Tentative Context Pipelining (TeCoP), a training-free decoder that decouples these roles through staggered block-parallel pipelining and draft-and-verify. TeCoP reuses stable but uncommitted predictions as provisional cross-block context, with commitment governed by confidence and stability checks. Across four LLaDA and Dream dLLMs on GSM8K, Minerva MATH, and MBPP, TeCoP reduces number of forward evaluations by up to 87% and achieves up to 7.50-fold throughput without KV caching and 11.77-fold with caching over the uncached original decoder. It outperforms Fast-dLLM and DSB in throughput across all 12 model-benchmark settings, with and without caching, while keeping average cached accuracy within one percentage point of the original decoder.
  </details>

- **[EviDial: When Correct Depression Predictions Lack Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27541)**  `arXiv:2610.27541`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junjie Xu, Jin Zhu, Yuzhe Yang, Luhan Wang, Xingjiao Wu, Daoguo Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Depression dialogue benchmarks commonly assess models by comparing predicted symptom labels with clinical scale scores. However, a prediction may agree with a benchmark label even when the dialogue lacks enough evidence to justify it. We introduce EviDial, a benchmark that distinguishes label agreement from evidence-grounded assessment. EviDial comprises 382 depression dialogues from three sources and 2,744 participant-symptom cells, each classified as having absent, partial, conflicting, or sufficient evidence. Because evidence sufficiency lacks a directly observable gold standard, we derive calibrated evidence references from multiple judgments using generalizability theory, many-facet Rasch measurement, and random-effects latent class analysis. Our audit reveals that 82.8% of participant-symptom cells lack sufficient textual evidence for the corresponding symptom assessment, with substantial variation across sources. Across model families and prompting strategies, 69.2%-96.9% of predictions judged correct by benchmark labels are nonetheless unsupported by sufficient dialogue evidence. Further analyses show that strong agreement among LLM evaluators need not reflect independent evidence judgments, underscoring the need to calibrate automated reference panels. These findings demonstrate that label-based evaluation can overestimate evidence-grounded performance by conflating agreement with clinical labels and support in the observed dialogue. EviDial provides a measurement layer for separating these constructs and auditing LLM-based depression assessment more faithfully.
  </details>

- **[Judging the Justification: Decision Certificate Supervision for Generative Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27528)**  `arXiv:2610.27528`  `cs.CL`  
  _Guiquan Sun, Bing Bai, Martin Renqiang Min, Dongjin Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative reward models (GenRMs) generate natural language rationales to justify preference judgments, yet a correct verdict does not guarantee a sound justification. Existing approaches often rely on reference explanations to supervise rationales, but obtaining such references can be costly and does not necessarily establish whether generated rationales support the resulting verdict. To address these limitations, we propose DeCert, a framework that evaluates the validity and verdict consistency of generated rationales without requiring reference explanations. Crucially, DeCert translates these assessments into preference supervision that accounts for both verdict correctness and justification quality. In particular, when multiple sampled judgments share the same correct verdict, differences in certificate quality enable DeCert to construct rationale preference pairs that distinguish stronger from weaker justifications. Extensive empirical studies across two model families and multiple in- and out-of-distribution benchmarks demonstrate that DeCert consistently outperforms strong generative judge baselines while improving rationale quality. Moreover, DeCert effectively mitigates positional bias, improving position-consistent accuracy by up to 14.8 points. These results highlight the importance of supervising not only which verdict is correct, but also how that verdict is justified.
  </details>

- **[SHAM-Bench: A Span-level Hallucination Benchmark Across Multiple Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27370)**  `arXiv:2610.27370`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sicong Huang, Ian Lane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Span-level hallucination detection pinpoints unsupported content in LLM outputs, enabling actionable feedback and targeted mitigation. Existing evaluations for span-level hallucination detection methods are fragmented across tasks, annotation formats, and metrics, and most span-level datasets cover only a single NLG domain, making it difficult to assess whether a detection method actually generalizes across tasks and domains. In this paper, we introduce SHAM-Bench (Span-level Hallucination Benchmark Across Multiple Tasks), a unified benchmark for span-level hallucination detection spanning six NLG tasks, aggregated from existing human-annotated datasets totaling 6,461 examples under a common schema and evaluation protocol. Using SHAM-Bench, we evaluate existing hallucination detection methods and find that fine-tuned detectors often perform well in-domain but degrade under distribution shift, whereas prompt-based methods with reasoning are more robust across tasks. Our analysis further shows that subtle unsupported content remains difficult to detect and span-level evaluation is still challenged by ambiguous span boundaries and noisy labels. We release the dataset, evaluation code, and a public leaderboard to support future research on broadly generalizable hallucination detection.
  </details>

- **[Explore More, Validate Less: Efficient Reflective Prompt Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27367)**  `arXiv:2610.27367`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaofan Zhou, Lu Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflective prompt optimization improves instructions using task feedback, but coupling candidate generation with full-validation-based selection can make search costly and sequential. We propose a two-stage framework that decouples reflective candidate-pool construction from validation allocation. During construction, we sample multiple parents and reuse each parent's training responses across parallel reflection branches. Each revision is screened against its parent on a small training minibatch, allowing locally improved candidates to support further revisions without full validation. After construction, an exploration policy selects candidates from the frozen pool under a fixed full-validation budget, and the prompt with the highest measured validation score is returned. This separation allows candidate-pool size and validation budget to be set independently. We evaluate random selection and learned score prediction with similarity-based exploration on five benchmarks using Qwen3.5-9B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. With 100 retained candidates and 20 full validations, our framework improves over the initial solver by up to 11.22 percentage points. Learned selection improves over the initial solver in all ten comparisons, although neither selection policy consistently outperforms the other. These results support separating reflective search from budgeted validation while leaving room for stronger candidate-selection policies.
  </details>

- **[RealControlBench: A Benchmark for Interpretability-Informed Fine-Grained Control of LLMs and Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27295)**  `arXiv:2610.27295`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinying Cai, Bin Hu, Mingyu Jin, Qiming Zhang, Yang Ouyang, Bangwei Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing steering benchmarks are built around simplified, isolated generation tasks and evaluate a single behavioral attribute under controlled settings that do not reflect the diversity and complexity of practical deployment. As a result, performance on these benchmarks provides only a limited picture of how steering methods perform in the real world. We therefore introduce RealControlBench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating fine-grained steering of LLMs and LLM agents across diverse behavioral targets and interaction settings. RealControlBench extends steering evaluation beyond isolated generations to complex, execution-grounded, multi-turn agent interactions, covering five complementary dimensions: instruction-following control, agentic tool-use decision control, reasoning-performance steering, reasoning-length control, and trajectory-conditioned safety awareness. Rather than measuring steering success alone, the benchmark jointly evaluates target controllability and capability preservation, including instruction following, reasoning performance, and downstream agent utility and safety. It further examines whether steering effects remain specific and robust across prompt variations, domains, and model architectures. Our evaluation shows that existing steering methods remain unreliable across realistic control objectives: they often fail to consistently induce the intended behavior and can substantially degrade the model's underlying task performance. These results suggest that strong steering effects in controlled settings do not necessarily translate into reliable control in more realistic evaluations. By providing a unified, model-agnostic steering evaluation framework, RealControlBench aims to support the development of steering methods that are not only effective but also precise, robust, and useful in realistic AI deployments.
  </details>

- **[African Languages AI Safety Readiness Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27269)**  `arXiv:2610.27269`  `cs.CL`  
  _Israel Abebe Azime, Jesujoba O. Alabi, Tajuddeen Gwadabe, David Ifeoluwa Adelani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the widespread adoption of large language models (LLMs) across languages and domains, AI safety has emerged as a critical global concern. However, due to resource constraints, research and development efforts tend to prioritize a limited number of high-resource languages, leaving many others, especially those spoken in Africa, underrepresented. While existing research on language "resource-ness" has largely examined whether sufficient resources are available to develop language technologies, the question of whether languages are adequately represented in AI safety research has received attention only more recently. This report examines the readiness of African languages for inclusion in AI safety discussions, drawing on existing literature and data. It considers four factors: the availability of AI-relevant resources, including text data; national AI policy support; performance on established text benchmarks; and speaker population. Based on these factors, languages are grouped into four readiness categories: fully ready, mostly ready, emerging, and foundational support needed. This classification is intended to inform the inclusion of African languages in AI safety benchmarks, highlight varying levels of preparedness, and guide strategic investment in data development, policy, and community engagement for underrepresented languages.
  </details>

- **[The Culture Within: Towards Mechanistic Understanding of Cultural Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27202)**  `arXiv:2610.27202`  `cs.CL`  
  _Samee Arif, Furkan Danisman, Angana Borah, Honglak Lee, Dehan Kong, Lajanugen Logeswaran, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly operate across culturally diverse settings, yet existing evaluations largely measure cultural behavior at the output level. We introduce CultureLens, a reference-grounded benchmark and mechanistic study of how LLMs use cultural information. CultureLens contains 9,261 scenarios across 75 countries and systematically varies country, value, true-norm, and contradictory-norm evidence. Across Llama-3.1-8B, Gemma-3-12B, and Qwen-3.5-27B, stating a contradictory norm lowers aligned answers by 32-47 percentage points, even when the model answers correctly from the country alone. Mechanistically, models default to Anglophone norms when no country is given. The failure under a contradictory norm is not one of missing knowledge, as blocking the norm inside the model restores the correct answer in most cases. Models accept a stated norm according to how it is presented and not whether it is true, which leaves them most exposed to errors in retrieved content. Supervised fine-tuning with contradictory-norm examples raises aligned answers under a contradictory norm to 85-93%, including on unseen countries, at a small cost under a true norm.
  </details>

- **[SITO: Sifting a Commercial Text-Safety API for Missed Harm and False Flags in English, Ukrainian and Surzhyk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27178)**  `arXiv:2610.27178`  `cs.CL`  
  _Andrii Tuzhykov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Platforms that host young users screen messages with commercial text-safety APIs, but independent evidence on how often such systems miss harm or flag harmless text is scarce, especially outside English, and the documentation of the one studied here leaves its false-positive rate unquantified. We evaluated one such API, Tuteliq Detect as deployed under the release identifier tuteliq-detect-v5.20261011 (not the separately published v6 model), on six harm categories: authorised push-payment fraud, money-mule recruitment, social engineering, romance scams, gambling harm and bullying. We built SITO (Synthetic Independent Text-safety benchmark with Overflagging tests; sito is Ukrainian for 'sieve'), 2,332 synthetic cases in English, Ukrainian and mixed Ukrainian, with each harmful case matched by hard harmless ones: same-topic messages, minimal-edit contrasts, warnings and retrospective accounts. On English text the system flagged 84.9-94.1% of harmful cases and 0-4.3% of harmless ones per category. It missed third-party reports of ongoing harm far more often than perpetrators' messages (63.6% against 95.1% recall, all languages), when sent without the settings the API offers for reports of harm. Ukrainian and mixed-language input was detected at least as often as English, recall was lower on text from a held-out generator family, so headline recall may be optimistic, and 3.2% of cases received a different decision in repeated runs. A platform should measure false positives on its own traffic and check how bystander reports are handled. The datasets, generation prompts and code are released.
  </details>

- **[Fluent but Fallible: Benchmarking Hallucination and Factual Reliability in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27034)**  `arXiv:2610.27034`  `cs.CL`  
  _Osama Shabih, Dayam Nadeem, Shahab Saquib Sohail, Mohammad Nadeem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly advanced natural language processing and artificial intelligence applications through their capabilities in reasoning, text generation and knowledge retrieval. However, LLMs are still vulnerable to two important challenges: hallucination, generating factually incorrect or fabricated content, and response deviation, meaning systematic deviations from ground-truth responses. These limitations raise serious concerns regarding reliability and trustworthiness in academic and research environments. This study offers a thorough comparative analysis using a variety of metrics of ten advanced LLMs ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude~3.5~Sonnet, LLaMA~3, Mistral, Grok, Perplexity, DeepSeek~V3, Falcon, and Gemma based on a benchmark consisting of 70 carefully designed academic prompts across four complexity levels: Basic, Explanation, Advanced, and Tricky, evaluated in February 2025 on specific model versions. Each response was independently evaluated by three reviewers using five metrics: Accuracy, Precision, Recall, Response Deviation Index, and Hallucination Index. Perplexity and DeepSeek~V3 achieved the highest overall accuracy, while Gemini demonstrated strong recall and a well-balanced performance profile across all five metrics. LLaMA~3 showed strong resistance against hallucinations through a conservative method of generation. Advanced-level prompts proved most challenging across all models, while Tricky prompts were handled more reliably owing to their binary true/false structure. Falcon achieved perfect precision and zero hallucinations, but at the cost of the lowest recall and the highest response deviation. The results show a fundamental trade-off between response completeness and factual reliability, intensified by prompt complexity.
  </details>

- **[Decomposed Relation Extraction from Aviation Accident Narratives: Evaluating Transformer-Based Safety Knowledge Discovery under Relational Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27013)**  `arXiv:2610.27013`  `cs.CL`  
  _Aziida Nanyonga, Mustafa Tahsin Yilmaz, Saad Ezzini, Ugur Turhan, Graham Wild_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Relation extraction from aviation accident narratives is challenging because genuine safety relations are sparse among possible concept pairs. This study evaluates a decomposed framework separating relation classification from concept identification. A benchmark of 300 National Transportation Safety Board (NTSB) narratives containing 2,524 unique concept-text nodes was expanded into 21,038 ordered candidate pairs, with only 248 (1.18%) representing RESULTS_IN or CONTRIBUTES_TO relations. A TF-IDF Linear Support Vector Machine (SVM) and weighted RoBERTa classifier were developed using an independent graph-annotated resource with hard-negative sampling and frozen before sealed evaluation. Development Macro-F1 scores of 0.4738 (SVM) and 0.4809 (RoBERTa) declined to 0.2741 and 0.2420 under exhaustive evaluation, with Matthews correlation coefficients of 0.0128 and 0.0197. False positives dominated: the SVM recovered 96 reference relations but generated 7,018 false positives, while RoBERTa recovered 125 but generated 9,824. Post-test diagnostics revealed overlapping RoBERTa confidence distributions and false positives even within valid relation-category directions. Complementary end-to-end SmolLM2 evaluation identified missed and unsupported relations alongside malformed outputs. These findings demonstrate that known concept endpoints do not guarantee reliable relation extraction and that sampled-negative development performance may poorly reflect exhaustive evaluation. Controlling false positives and managing candidate-space sparsity remain essential for aviation safety knowledge extraction.
  </details>

- **[TRACE: Training-aware Robustness and Challenge Evaluation for Text-Vision Embedding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26993)**  `arXiv:2610.26993`  `cs.CL`  
  _Pume Tuchinda, Tinnakit Udsa, Peerat Limkonchotiwat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing benchmarks for text-vision embedding models report aggregate scores that obscure whether performance comes from general capabilities, task-specific adaptation, or robustness to challenging tasks. We introduce TRACE, a Training-aware Robustness and Challenge Evaluation framework that reorganizes 168 tasks from various sources of datasets into three categories: General, Adaptability, and Challenge. Through controlled experiments on two text-vision embedding models, we show that combining general and adaptation data improves Adaptability performance by 7.67 and 7.09 points, respectively, while largely preserving General performance. However, Challenge performance remains below 5.0, with limited improvements from additional training data. Further analysis identifies multilingual and visual-document tasks as major weaknesses. Our findings demonstrate that data adaptation alone is insufficient to address these challenges, highlighting the need for methodological advances beyond data-centric improvements.
  </details>

- **[Do We Need the Largest LLMs for Every Clinical Decision?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26937)**  `arXiv:2610.26937`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ali H. Lazem, William Teahan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large clinical language models often require multi-GPU infrastructure, limiting local deployment. We introduce CERTA, a decision-level statistical certificate that determines when a quantized model may substitute for its native BF16 reference and escalates decisions that cannot be certified to it. Across eleven instruction-tuned models, both general-purpose and domain-adapted, from three families (7-72B), we evaluated 2,500 clinical-verification decisions from de-identified patient contexts (2,484 patients), with patient-disjoint calibration and testing. In the higher-power operational analysis, round-to-nearest 8-bit quantization at BF16 storage served 99.8-100% of held-out decisions across all eleven models; formal finite-sample guarantees were assessed separately using the exact split-sample procedure. In the same operational analysis, the class-conditional 4-bit frontier reduced the amortized, BF16-equivalent deployment cost to 25.8% and 27.3% of the BF16 reference for Qwen2.5-72B and HuatuoGPT-o1-72B, respectively, with measured peak memory of 43.0 and 42.7 GB, below the nominal capacity of a single 48 GB accelerator. Deployment on that specific hardware was not directly tested. Under the exact split-sample certificate, which also accounts for configuration selection, these costs were 26.7% and 27.9%. On external disease named-entity benchmarks against human annotations, the frontier-selected NF4 configurations had a mean absolute F1 change of 0.045 on both datasets, with substantial model-specific variation. CERTA converts compression from a fixed precision choice into a risk-controlled deployment policy for this verification task.
  </details>

- **[When Does Better Acceptance Pay? A Controlled Study of Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24993)**  `arXiv:2610.24993`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yongchao Long, Chunrui Zheng, Yuxuan Zhou, Guangkun Nie, Tian-Chi Ma, Chao Pang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The throughput gains of speculative decoding depend on the trade-off between acceptance length and execution latency. We ask when additional acceptance is worth the cost of obtaining it. We establish a comparison protocol spanning training, inference, and evaluation, remeasuring released checkpoints and retraining six drafter recipes on shared target-generated data across ten benchmarks and two target models. Alignment changes the picture: gaps that appear large in published comparisons shrink substantially under shared training and inference conditions. This narrowing motivates two deeper questions: how is acceptance improved at different costs, and when do these gains improve throughput? We find that acceptance can be improved through nearly free objective and context changes or more expensive token-to-token conditioning. DCore illustrates the former route as a strong baseline with little additional measured drafting overhead. The payoff is conditional: task uncertainty and serving conditions affect acceptance returns, execution costs, and the preferred drafter and budget. These findings inform future drafter design and deployment choices.
  </details>

- **[DiscoveryRSI: Recursively Learning to Discover Valuable Hypotheses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23839)**  `arXiv:2610.23839`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jianyuan Zhong, Chenwei Wu, Zhaochen Yu, Xixian Jiang, Xuewei Li, Hao Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery requires developing worthwhile hypotheses into practical investigations, yet learning from this evolving research process remains challenging. We introduce an operational definition of valuable and testable research hypotheses: evidence-grounded claims about consequential uncertainties, coupled with feasible investigations, explicit decision criteria, and alternative explanations. Our DiscoveryRSI framework develops hypothesis trees through multi-agent evolutionary search, compiles selected investigations into executable Harbor tasks, and recursively learns through three mechanisms: hypothesis credit optimization attributes scientific assessments to documented research contributions; harness replay identifies productive strategies and recurring failures to refine research workflows; and rediscovery applies accepted improvements to new investigations, generating further experience for subsequent learning. We also introduce DFM-Bench, which provides comprehensive assessment of hypothesis quality and cumulative research progress at the levels of hypotheses, revisions, development paths and research runs. Across four life-science domains, automated evaluation shows that hypothesis quality rises from 4.86 for the base model to 5.39 after two rounds of self-improvement, with the large gains in falsifiability and data resolvability. We further validate hypothesis quality through expert review, comparing our DiscoveryRSI-27B with other research agents. Preliminary human assessments show that DiscoveryRSI-27B scores higher than GPT-6 Luna on hypothesis importance and whether claims are plausible and evidence-bounded. DiscoveryRSI-27B achieved a 6.3% rate of hypothesis correspondence with research published after the model cutoff, largely outperform GPT-6 with 2.5% and Claude Opus 5 with 2.3%, while delivering greater human-rated improvement along research paths.
  </details>

- **[No Single Tokenizer Feature Reliably Predicts Downstream Language Model Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21679)**  `arXiv:2610.21679`  `cs.CL`  
  _Antonio Lopardo, Luis Frentzen Salim, Shikhar Srivastava, Kevin Jiang, Mridul Sharma, Catherine Arnett_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tokenization is a critical component of the language modeling pipeline, but it is poorly understood. Tokenizers can impact downstream performance, but there is currently mixed evidence about which tokenizer features predict a better language model without requiring a full training run. In this paper, we analyze approximately 200 language models trained on nine different languages. We evaluate both models and their tokenizers and test whether there is a relationship between any proposed intrinsic tokenizer evaluations and model performance, as measured by a wide variety of benchmarks. Despite the quantity and variety of proposed intrinsic tokenizer metrics, we find that none consistently predicts downstream performance across model families, tasks, and languages. This motivates the need for novel tokenizer evaluation metrics and approaches. We propose novel linguistically grounded metric introduced in this work shows that new metrics can capture aspects of tokenizer quality that existing metrics miss, though their predictive power is likely to vary across model families, languages, and tasks.
  </details>

- **[SoK: Grading Open-Text Legal Answers, from Law School Exams to Trustworthy Rubric Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19992)**  `arXiv:2610.19992`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sebastian Nagl_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Law students are examined through open-text answers: case solutions, essays and memos that apply the law to a set of facts. Such answers are expensive to grade, human graders disagree widely on them, and large language models (LLMs) now offer to grade them at scale, both in teaching and in the benchmarks that test legal AI. This paper systematizes how open-text legal answers can be evaluated. We derive eight desiderata from educational measurement and from the law that governs exams, among them reasons, credit for defensible alternative solutions, robustness to gaming and human oversight. We then compare eight evaluation paradigms against them, from unstructured human grading to LLM judges with instance-specific rubrics and pairwise comparison. Three findings stand out. A rubric written for the task is the strongest lever for agreement between an LLM judge and human graders, but studies measure that agreement against different references and rarely report how far the human graders agree with each other. The central validation gap lies where examination law is most demanding: defensible alternative solutions, follow-on errors, and the examiner's correction of model judgments. Rubric-centred grading is the most auditable family, because it makes the standard, its application and the final decision checkable. From this we derive the trustworthy rubric judge: an instance rubric that credits arguments and alternative paths, evidence checked in code, probes, repeated runs, calibration against independent human grades, logging, and an examiner who reads every script. Today the evidence supports such a judge for formative feedback and for benchmarks, and as an assistant to the examiner, not a replacement, where credit is at stake.
  </details>

- **[PersonaFactor: Preference Factors with Dynamic Selection for LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16372)**  `arXiv:2610.16372`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hang Su, Zequn Liu, Jianghan Chao, Qiuyu Tian, Xuesong Lu, Yingce Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although users may express different personalization needs across tasks, these task-specific behaviors can reflect shared underlying preference mechanisms. Existing LLM personalization methods often focus on surface-level interactions or undifferentiated histories, overlooking the shared mechanisms underlying these behaviors. We introduce PersonaFactors, a framework that distills reusable and interpretable personalization factors from heterogeneous user interactions. We construct a library of thirteen PersonaFactors by extracting evidence-grounded rationales and clustering recurring patterns across tasks, and develop a dynamic factor-guided generation framework that selects factors relevant to each user's history and current request. Across four personalization benchmarks and four backbone models, Dynamic PersonaFactor achieves the best score on every reported metric. Representation analyses further show that PersonaFactors and user history induce partially overlapping low-dimensional representations, and that their joint use yields complementary shifts. Taken together, these findings suggest that factor-based and history-based personalization can mutually reinforce each other.
  </details>

- **[LongHisDoc: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Chinese Long Historical Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14531)**  `arXiv:2610.14531`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zeyu Shan, Yongxin Shi, Shihang Wu, Peirong Zhang, Yuanyuan Fang, Zhaopeng Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chinese historical documents are invaluable cultural treasures, but their complexity poses significant comprehension challenges for non-specialists. Despite the success of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) in modern document tasks, their capabilities in Long (full-book) Historical Document understanding remain largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce LongHisDoc, the first benchmark tailored for long historical document understanding. LongHisDoc comprises 1,012 expert-annotated QA pairs derived from 101 PDF documents, with an average of 85.1 pages and 42,776.4 tokens per document. It spans 10 categories and features four QA types: single-page, cross-page, multi-hop, and unanswerable. Answering these questions requires integrating multimodal evidence from text, layout, and visual elements. Our evaluation reveals that the top-performing LVLM and LLM achieve only 62.9% and 69.9% accuracy, respectively, highlighting a substantial performance gap.
