Furthermore, we assess various modal retrievers and the end-to-end RAG performance to provide references for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. We believe that LongHisDoc serves as a comprehensive benchmark to catalyze future research in full-book historical document understanding. The benchmark and evaluation code are available atthis https URLandthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Agentic Data for Long-Horizon Terminal Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13166)**  `arXiv:2610.13166`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shun Zou, Ziyu Ma, Yi Zou, Yong Wang, Lin Chen, Zehui Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Terminal agents are moving beyond local operations toward completing real-world professional tasks. Short trajectories can demonstrate commands and tool calls, but they do not fully show how agents accumulate evidence through exploration, use it to inform judgments, continually adjust strategies based on feedback, and turn intermediate results into final deliverables. High-quality long-horizon trajectories capture these agentic behavioral patterns and their connections within a single problem-solving process, providing richer experience for sustained autonomous work. However, environment synthesis may complete or simplify the key work underlying these patterns in advance and thus remove the need for exploration, implementation, and verification that the agent was meant to perform. To address this, we introduce TerminalHorizon, a fully automated data synthesis engine for long-horizon terminal agents. Starting from real-world professional work, it uses independent execution to identify key work omitted during construction and uses the resulting evidence to guide environment reconstruction. To extend these patterns across stages, it progressively expands tasks under the same goal, introducing new requirements that build on prior results and call for further exploration and improvement. Using this engine, we construct 1.5K task environments and perform supervised fine-tuning of Qwen3.5-27B on the resulting dataset to obtain TerminalHorizon-27B. We achieve substantial performance gains on all 12 benchmarks spanning coding-agent tasks, agentic tasks in real-world production settings, and general reasoning. Multiple RSI case studies, each with a 12-hour research budget, further demonstrate the model's ability to continue experimenting beyond an initial solution, improve its methods based on feedback, and retain effective improvements in its final deliverables.
  </details>

- **[Pair Difficulty Matters: Rethinking Pairwise LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation and Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37577)**  `arXiv:2609.37577`  `cs.CL`  
  _Bruno Brocai, Maria Becker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model judges are widely used to rank texts and text-generating systems through pairwise comparison, and their reliability is typically assessed via three proxies: position bias, transitivity, and pairwise agreement (self- or human-labeled). Because these proxies drive judge selection and benchmarking, a substantial literature reporting that judges perform poorly on them risks steering practitioners away from otherwise capable evaluators. We argue this assessment is misleading. Under the Bradley--Terry geometry underlying pairwise aggregation, each proxy is dominated by close-rank-gap pairs, where inconsistency is information-theoretically expected and individual verdicts contribute little to the aggregate ranking; far-gap pairs carry the ranking signal but barely move the proxies. We formalize this argument and validate it in a controlled simulation and on two human-rated corpora: the proxies correlate only weakly with ranking accuracy against gold, and their predictive component concentrates in the far-gap regime. Judges should therefore be assessed on rank-gap-conditional metrics, ideally against human rankings. Code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Machine's Internal Clock: Do LLMs Share Human Temporal Illusions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15394)**  `arXiv:2608.15394`  `cs.CL`  
  _Catherine Bao, Vivek Srikumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human perception of time is subjective. Temporal illusions show that the brain relies on context and relational cues when judging duration. Prior studies established these effects with visual and auditory stimuli. We ask whether written narratives alone can cause these illusions, and whether LLMs respond as human participants do. We introduce a benchmark of 6,684 paired scenarios spanning five illusions, each judged from the character's and the reader's perspective. In our human study (60 participants), participants' judgments matched the predicted directions for all five illusions in at least one perspective (7 of 10 conditions). Across 14 LLMs, models reproduce every effect humans show in the same direction, but on average more strongly. Most effects persisted even after cue words were removed and after controlling for 15 lexical and semantic features. These results show that models and humans agree in direction but not in strength, which cautions against treating LLM judgments as substitutes for human responses for subjective temporal reasoning.
  </details>

- **[SINT-Flow: Schema Integration using Large Language Model Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24492)**  `arXiv:2607.24492`  `cs.CL`  
  _Keti Korini, Christian Bizer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The goal of schema integration is, given a set of input schemata or tables, to derive a global, unified schema that is able to represent the concepts, attributes, and relationships of all input tables in a coherent fashion. This paper presents SINT-Flow, a schema integration framework composed of five LLM-based operators that can be combined into workflows to perform fully automated, end-to-end schema integration. In contrast to existing approaches, SINT-Flow can process denormalized source tables that contain attributes describing multiple entity types. During the schema integration process, these tables are decomposed into separate entity-specific relations. To evaluate SINT-Flow, we introduce SINT-Bench, a schema integration benchmark comprising 10 schema integration tasks consisting of altogether 93 relational tables, including tables that describe multiple types of entities. We evaluate SINT-Flow using GPT-5.2 as well as the open-weight model Qwen-3.6-27B as alternative backbone models. Using these models, SINT-Flow achieves a Completeness rate of at least 97% in entity type detection, 81% for attribute detection, and an F1-score of 84% for schema mapping. Furthermore, we perform an ablation study to prove the utility of the applied self-consistency strategy as well as the inclusion of a review loop into the Schema Matching Operator.
  </details>

- **[Randomized YaRN Improves Length Generalization for Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23687)**  `arXiv:2606.23687`  `cs.CL`  
  _Manas Mehta, Fangcong Yin, Greg Durrett_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are typically pretrained on short sequences and then extended to work on longer sequences with additional training. However, such LLMs still struggle to further generalize to very long sequences. We propose Randomized YaRN, a training method that improves length generalization by combining YaRN-based positional extrapolation with randomized positional encoding and a length curriculum. During training on short context data, tokens are assigned YaRN positional encodings sampled from a larger position range, exposing the model to out-of-distribution positional representations even on short-context inputs. We evaluate Randomized YaRN on three challenging long-context reasoning benchmarks, BABILong, Multi-Round Coreference Resolution (MRCR), and LongBench v2. When training on data with short context, Randomized YaRN consistently improves reasoning performance on context lengths from 16K to 128K and outperforms standard fine-tuning, with the largest gains appearing at far out-of-distribution lengths. Our results suggest that progressively exposing models to OOD positional distributions provides an effective recipe for generalizable long-context reasoning.
  </details>

- **[When Is 0.1% Enough? Analyzing the Combined Effects of Dimensionality Reduction and Quantization on Text Embedding Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01074)**  `arXiv:2606.01074`  `cs.CL`  
  _Riku Kisako, Hayato Tsukagoshi, Ryohei Sasano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent high-performing text embedding models often produce high-dimensional real-valued vectors, resulting in substantial storage and computational costs. To address this issue, embedding compression methods based on dimensionality reduction or quantization have been proposed; however, the effects of their combination have not been sufficiently investigated. In this paper, we systematically investigate how dimensionality reduction and quantization jointly affect embedding compression across four task families in the Massive Text Embedding Benchmark (MTEB), using five pretrained embedding models. Our results show that combining dimensionality reduction and quantization can preserve near-original performance with substantially smaller storage footprints than either method alone. In particular, for classification, several models retain at least 99% of their original classification score using less than 0.1% of the storage required by the original embeddings. The preferred balance between dimensionality and bit-width varies across tasks, with retrieval generally being more sensitive to compression.
  </details>

- **[Performance-Driven Policy Optimization for Speculative Decoding with Adaptive Windowing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14978)**  `arXiv:2605.14978`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jie Jiang, Xing Sun, Ruotian Chen, Jianan Su, Kaixin Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by having a lightweight draft model propose speculative windows of candidate tokens for parallel verification by a larger target model. In practice, speculative efficiency is often bottlenecked by hard-to-draft positions, where an early mismatch truncates the accepted prefix and invalidates the rest of the speculative window. Most learning-based drafters are still optimized with token-level supervised objectives, even though speculative utility is inherently window-level and prefix-sensitive. We propose PPOW (Performance-Driven Policy Optimization with Adaptive Windowing), a reinforcement learning framework that shifts drafter optimization from token-level imitation to window-level optimization. PPOW combines a Cost-Aware Speedup Reward, a Distribution-Based Proximity Reward, and Adaptive Divergence-Aware Windowing, which prioritizes informative windows with high confidence-weighted draft-target divergence. PPOW achieves average acceptance lengths of 6.29-6.52 and speedups of 3.39-4.36$\times$ across multiple model families and benchmarks under a unified decoding protocol. These results show that performance-driven window-level optimization is a practical approach to improving speculative decoding efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Interruption-Aware Recurrent Reinforcement Learning for UAS Navigation Under Temporary Overrides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28121)**  `arXiv:2610.28121`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Christopher Conrad, Deepak Panda, Weisi Guo, Antonios Tsourdos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe autonomous navigation of UASs requires tactical deconfliction to address conflicts that strategic coordination cannot prevent. This task is challenging under partial observability, when sensor or traffic data are missing, delayed, or degraded. Geometry-based methods can offer separation assurances but face limitations in dense encounters. Recurrent reinforcement learning has therefore been proposed to learn navigation policies that integrate observation-action histories to optimise long-term safety-efficiency trade-offs. However, operational safeguards may require a remote pilot, onboard detect-and-avoid system, or runtime assurance mechanism to temporarily override the learned policy. A research gap remains in understanding how these interruptions affect recurrent policies when the agent regains authority. Benchmark evidence shows that interruptions make task variables less decodable from the recurrent hidden state and increase post-interruption collision risk. The findings suggest that altered recurrent context prolongs post-handback degradation through an action-observation feedback loop, in which changed actions alter the flight path and subsequent recurrent updates. To address this vulnerability, we propose three interruption-aware architectures that condition recurrent processing on interruption context through separate feature streams, dedicated interruption memory, or gated environmental features. Across simulations, all three architectures preserve more decodable task information than a standard recurrent policy. Moreover, the strongest architecture reduces post-interruption collisions by 74% and interruption-induced mission failures by 69% relative to the recurrent baseline, without degrading interruption-free performance. These results show that safe interruption does not guarantee safe handback, and support interruption-aware recurrent design for resilient UAS autonomy.
  </details>

- **[VLA-Rolling: Fewer Denoising Steps, Better Control via Adaptive Action Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25043)**  `arXiv:2610.25043`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Sicheng He, Yi Pan, Qiuting Yu, Jiaming Huang, Qi Lu, Shuyan Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-matching vision-language-action (VLA) models generate an action chunk by iteratively denoising Gaussian noise, but typically execute only a short prefix before replanning with updated observations. Under this receding-horizon paradigm, the unexecuted remainder---the action tail---is discarded, and the next chunk is regenerated from fresh noise. We show that this discarded tail retains transferable denoising progress: action-tail initialization can match or outperform a native 10-step Gaussian baseline using fewer denoising steps. However, the optimal denoising budget varies substantially across samples, indicating that tail reuse should be adapted to the updated environment. Based on this observation, we introduce VLA-Rolling, a lightweight inference framework that reuses action tails through three stages: action-tail inheritance, per-action confidence estimation, and confidence-guided refinement through renoising and denoising budget allocation, while the underlying VLA remains frozen. Across $\pi_{0.5}$, SmolVLA, X-VLA, and MolmoAct2 on LIBERO, MetaWorld, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboTwin 2.0, VLA-Rolling reduces action-chunk inference cost in all 16 model--benchmark pairs, achieving up to a $1.48\times$ speedup, while improving success rate in 14 pairs by up to $15.08$ percentage points. These results show that action tails are not merely discarded predictions, but reusable intermediate states that enable more efficient and accurate VLA inference. These results demonstrate that the action tail can support the generation of better actions with fewer denoising steps. Our project is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Does Multi-Agent Collaboration Pay When Agents Serve Users? Testing Agent Scaling in Dual-Control Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27655)**  `arXiv:2610.27655`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Ethan Yang, Xiangcheng Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) built from language models are deployed on the premise that collaboration beats a single-agent system (SAS), yet when it pays is unclear. The most systematic study to date gives a capability-saturation rule: coordination helps while the SAS is weak and hurts once it is strong, with the crossing near 45% single-agent accuracy. That rule was derived on autonomous benchmarks, where no user takes part. We test it where agents now work: in conversation with a user under a written policy, on $\tau^2$-bench, the dual-control benchmark. On its airline, retail and telecom domains we compare the stock SAS with a centralized MAS built from the same model for six models from three families, over about 7,950 simulated conversations, with one completion-token ledger per episode per system. The study was pre-registered with a public change log; the MAS is reported as built, with one validity bar unmet (its planner never saw the tool schemas). Under a fixed simulated user no cell shows a MAS benefit: the MAS is worse in 12 of the 13 valid cells and tied in the thirteenth, and the fitted gain is negative across the observed capability range (weighted mean -28 points, 95% CI [-44, -12]). On the two cells where the rule predicts a benefit, the MAS loses by 13 and 22 points, and without a budget ceiling it at best draws level at 5.5 times the SAS's token spend. The one gain appears on the cell nearest 45%, under a second simulator. Whether the gain declines with capability is unresolved (slope -0.41, 95% CI [-1.51, +0.68]), because model-by-domain effects dominate. The MAS does not detectably reduce wrongful database writes; it converts other failures into budget exhaustion and inaction. For agents that serve people, the SAS is the default; a multi-agent design must show compute-matched evidence from the setting itself. We release all trajectories, ledgers, prompts and code.
  </details>

- **[FDE-Bench: Evaluating End-to-End Delivery from Underspecified Real-World Business Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14539)**  `arXiv:2610.14539`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Huaiming Li, Can Huang, Chufan Wu, Danyu Liu, Xiaomin Yuan, Qinze Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world business requests often leave key requirements unstated because frontline practitioners struggle to articulate rules they know from experience. Forward-deployed engineers (FDEs) must elicit these requirements through dialogue and iteratively optimize solutions until they meet customer acceptance criteria. Existing optimization benchmarks typically start from explicit objectives, while clarification benchmarks often introduce information gaps into existing tasks. We introduce FDE-Bench, the first benchmark built from real customer projects to evaluate agents on end-to-end delivery tasks that require interactive requirement clarification and iterative optimization. Its 49 cases span combinatorial optimization and machine learning. Clarification targets are distilled from discussions between frontline practitioners and FDEs. Agents elicit missing requirements from a simulated customer, and case-specific evaluators assess final artifacts against acceptance criteria using customer-accepted FDE solutions as quality references. Across 18 agent configurations, the highest mean delivery score is only 0.5510, compared with an FDE reference of 1.0. The highest delivery success rate (DSR), defined as the fraction of planned runs reaching this reference, is only 6.12%. These results show that reliable FDE-level end-to-end delivery remains out of reach for the evaluated agents on underspecified real-world business tasks. The benchmark project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Agents-as-Peers: Bias-Controlled LLM Evaluation as Multi-Agent Peer Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02589)**  `arXiv:2602.02589`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yanki Margalit, Nurit Cohen-Inger, Erni Avram, Ran Taig, Seffi Cohen, Oded Margalit_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) evaluation commonly relies on fixed human-authored benchmarks or centralized human/model judges. Fixed benchmarks can age and enter model training corpora, while centralized judges introduce their own scoring preferences.We cast LLM evaluation as a multi-agent peer-assessment problem and introduce Agents-as-Peers, a distributed evaluation protocol in which every agent generates tasks, answers the shared task set, and judges the answers of its peers, with no privileged evaluator.Self-ratings are excluded from the ranking, and judging is repeated under controlled identity and presentation regimes so that self bias, identity effects, presentation-position sensitivity, and judge severity are measured rather than left implicit.In an instantiation with 12 LLMs and 420 agent-generated questions, Agents-as-Peers aggregates 55,440 non-self peer judgments per regime.Most agents assign their own answers higher scores than those answers receive from peer judges; visible identity changes scores for some agents; the first fixed answer slot is associated with scores 0.39 points higher than the shuffled baseline; and judges differ markedly in severity.On labeled benchmarks, peer scores align strongly with accuracy on TruthfulQA (Pearson r=0.904) and GSM8K (r=0.873). On TruthfulQA, the peer-score correlation is substantially higher than the self-score correlation (r=0.538).A benchmark-overlap screen and item-level human audit further characterize the endogenous task set.These results support Agents-as-Peers as a cohort-relative measurement protocol whose ranking is reported together with the biases and rater effects that shape it.Code and dataset:this https URL
  </details>

- **[The Reward Model Selection Crisis in Personalized Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23067)**  `arXiv:2512.23067`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Fady Rezk, Yuangang Pan, Chuan-Sheng Foo, Xun Xu, Nancy Chen, Henry Gouk, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized alignment from preference data has focused primarily on improving personal reward model (RM) accuracy, with the implicit assumption that better preference ranking translates to better personalized behavior. However, in deployment, computational constraints necessitate inference-time adaptation such as reward-guided decoding (RGD) rather than per-user policy fine-tuning. This creates a critical but overlooked requirement: reward models must not only rank preferences accurately but also effectively guide generation. We demonstrate that standard RM accuracy fails catastrophically as a selection criterion for deployment-ready personalized rewards. We introduce policy accuracy; a metric quantifying whether RGD-adapted LLMs correctly discriminate between preferred and dispreferred responses and show that upstream RM accuracy correlates only weakly with downstream policy accuracy (Kendall's tau = 0.08--0.31). More critically, we introduce Pref-LaMP the first personalized alignment benchmark with ground-truth user completions, enabling direct behavioural evaluation. On Pref-LaMP, we expose a complete decoupling between discriminative ranking and generation metrics: methods with 20-point RM accuracy differences produce almost identical output quality, and methods with high ranking accuracy can fail to generate behaviorally aligned responses. These findings reveal that the field has been optimizing for proxy metrics that do not predict deployment performance, and that current personalized alignment methods fail to operationalize preferences into behavioral adaptation under realistic deployment constraints. In contrast, we find simple in-context learning (ICL) to be highly effective - dominating all reward-guided methods for models $\geq$3B parameters, achieving $\sim$3 point ROUGE-1 gains over the best reward method at 7B scale.
  </details>

- **[Thinker-WM: A Unified World Model with Shared Multimodal Representations for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18447)**  `arXiv:2610.18447`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Thinker Team Ubtech, Peng Gao, Jichao Jiao, Chen Liao, Daqin Luo, Yiming Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied AI, yet existing systems often remain fragmented in the modalities they model and the functions they support. In particular, visual dynamics, geometric structure, and executable action are commonly coupled through separate interfaces or downstream modules rather than being jointly represented and evolved within a shared dynamics model. In this work, we present Thinker-WM, a unified multimodal world model that jointly models and generates RGB, metric depth, and robot action through a shared intermediate Unified Dynamics Transformer with modality-specific encoders and decoders. The model performs multimodal unification at the representation level: appearance, geometry, and action are transformed into a common dynamic representation and jointly evolved by shared parameters, while their distinct output structures are preserved. The same parameterization supports multiple functions, including joint video-action generation, world prediction, policy inference, action-conditioned generation, video-to-action generation, and inverse dynamics. To examine whether the shared representation captures action-related information, we introduce a lightweight motion probe that shows the frozen visual representation contains recoverable motion information. Thinker-WM is trained on a heterogeneous corpus comprising approximately 20K hours of egocentric video, 10K hours of real-robot data, and 1K hours of simulation data, including approximately 4K hours of real-robot data with aligned depth supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong performance across embodied world-model evaluations. Thinker-WM ranks first on EWMBench, WorldOlympiad and WorldArena, while controlled studies further demonstrate the benefits of action conditioning, geometric interaction, and multi-function training within the shared representation.
  </details>

- **[S-Agent 2.0: Spatial Reasoning with a General Coding Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28354)**  `arXiv:2610.28354`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Rui Gong, Ruoyu Wang, Yalun Dai, Hao Li, Sidi Yang, Qihao Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning asks a model to work out where objects are in 3D space, how they relate to one another, and how they move. Agentic VLMs have recently brought large improvements on such tasks by letting the model actively gather evidence with 3D perception tools instead of answering in a single pass. Even so, on many questions the tools return the right evidence and the model still does not arrive at the correct answer: the gap between model and tools, where raw readings become usable conclusions, is left unfilled. We present S-Agent 2.0, a spatial reasoning agent that equips an off-the-shelf general coding agent with a command-line perception toolkit, and we design the interface around one principle: help the VLM close the evidence loop: acquire scene evidence, remember it in a persistent scene memory, and interpret each reading in plain spatial language. This design keeps the full flexibility of a coding agent, and every reading it acts on stays grounded in the tools' geometry. On MMSI-Bench and ReVSI, the toolkit improves a strong open-source model, Qwen3.8-27B, by +13.9 and +20.5 points over the same agent without tools, and a strong closed-source model, Gemini-3.8-Flash, by +11.2 and +9.6 points. With the open backbone S-Agent 2.0 reaches 66.5 on MMSI-Bench and 82.3 on ReVSI, ahead of the specialized system SpatialClaw on the same backbone (62.6 and 74.5), and with Gemini-3.8-Flash it reaches 74.1 and 90.5 against its 67.0 and 79.7. Both tool gains shrink as the backbone grows more capable, but SpatialClaw's shrinks far more on ReVSI, from +13.2 to +2.7, while ours stays at +9.6. S-Agent 2.0 also works across different general coding agent harnesses, such as Codex and Claude Code.
  </details>

- **[ChartEvo: Self-Evolving Agent Harnesses for Long-Tail Chart Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28282)**  `arXiv:2610.28282`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Qi Wang, Peijie Wang, Jun Cao, Haoyang Tong, Xiao-Hui Li, Fei Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have substantially advanced chart understanding and reasoning, yet their performance remains highly uneven across chart types. While modern MLLMs perform well on common charts, their accuracy drops sharply on long-tail charts with diverse visual structures and reasoning requirements. Tool-augmented agents can improve complex chart reasoning by introducing specialized visual capabilities, but manually engineering strategies and tools for the heterogeneous and evolving long tail is difficult to scale. We introduce \textbf{ChartEvo}, a recursive self-improvement framework that enables chart agents to adapt to underperforming chart types through experience-driven harness-level self-improvement. ChartEvo jointly evolves reusable chart-specific Skills and executable Tools, while learning reusable local tool-call chains to compose evolved capabilities and control the inference overhead of tool-augmented reasoning. On long-tail charts from ChartBench and ChartX, ChartEvo improves accuracy over its base MLLM, Qwen3.7-Plus, by \textbf{45.6\%} and \textbf{32.5\%}, respectively, demonstrating the effectiveness of recursive self-improvement for long-tail chart adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Slot-JEPA: Object-Structured Prediction Interfaces for Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28074)**  `arXiv:2610.28074`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Peisong Wen, Qianqian Xu, Minye Lei, Siran Dai, Yang Liu, Qingming Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As a prototypical latent world model, Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) provide strong semantic representations. However, dense patch representations expose a redundant interface to downstream predictors, which must implicitly infer spatial organization and temporal correspondence, presenting three challenges. First, object information is distributed across patches, forcing each downstream predictor to redundantly recover object structure that could otherwise be reused across tasks before modeling interactions. Second, the lack of explicit entity correspondence across time makes historical transitions expensive, restricting the predictor's flexibility in utilizing context. Third, without explicit transport structure, existing JEPA models typically use pointwise l1/l2 distances, which are insensitive to spatial displacement when predicted and goal supports are largely disjoint. We propose the Slot-based Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (Slot-JEPA), which decomposes dense representations into stable semantic slots and time-varying spatial masks. Pretrained once and frozen downstream, this decomposition exposes a reusable object-structured interface. Building on it, our Transition Memory Predictor (TMP) stores visual transitions as compact masks and retrieves useful context using statistics of corresponding slots. Its two branches separate slot-specific dynamics from shared cross-slot dynamics. We further use Unbalanced Optimal Transport (UOT) to supervise mask prediction and evaluate candidate plans by transporting mass between aligned predicted and goal masks, providing a signal better aligned with spatial progress. Across seven visual-understanding benchmarks, the slot-reconstructed features remain transferable. Slot-JEPA also improves goal-conditioned planning across six planar environments and four robotic arm planning tasks on the REALM-JEPA benchmark.
  </details>

- **[MultiWorldBench: Do Independently Controlled Views Describe One Shared World?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11723)**  `arXiv:2610.11723`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhangbo Xu, Ruoxi Zhang, Rui Hu, Yisong Wang, Xitong Li, Jingdong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiplayer world models must ensure that independently controlled views remain consistent with a single shared and persistent world. We introduce MultiWorldBench, a diagnostic Minecraft benchmark comprising 495 case configurations across seven task suites and ten capabilities, including independent control, cross-view motion, shared-state synchronization, persistence, structural reasoning, concurrent interaction, and delayed revisit. Using Engine GT as a reference, we evaluate two multiplayer world models, Solaris (MP) and Gamma-World, together with a single-player generation variant, Solaris (SP). Among the generated systems, Gamma-World achieves the highest ten-capability average at 21.39, followed by Solaris (MP) at 20.88 and Solaris (SP) at 10.06, while Engine GT reaches 91.69. Gamma-World performs better on several control, shared-state, and revisit capabilities, whereas Solaris (MP) leads in cross-view motion and race-condition consistency. Nevertheless, all generated systems score at most 8.00 on state persistence and 1.33 on structural consistency, and none succeeds in spatial reasoning or building-identity preservation. Human pairwise evaluation yields the same overall system ranking and shows strong alignment with the automatic evaluation, with a mean dimension-level Spearman correlation of 0.95. These results show that current multiplayer world models still exhibit a substantial gap relative to Engine GT in maintaining a coherent shared world.
  </details>

- **[CUA-Recovery: Benchmarking Error Recovery in Long-Horizon Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27645)**  `arXiv:2610.27645`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xu Hu, Haoming Li, Yuxiang Wang, Jinxin Yang, Kangshuo Li, Licheng Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As computer-use agents (CUAs) move to long-horizon workflows spanning multiple applications, undetected errors can silently corrupt persistent state and cause cascading failures in subsequent actions. Reliable error detection and recovery have become critical bottlenecks for building robust CUAs. However, existing benchmarks focus primarily on end-to-end task performance, providing limited insight into whether agents can detect and recover from errors during execution. The few benchmarks that evaluate recovery rely largely on manually injected errors, which may not reflect the types or horizons of failures induced by real agent policies. We introduce CUA-Recovery, a benchmark comprising test and training suites across multiple desktop applications. We construct it using ReRail, a framework for synthesizing long-horizon tasks and trajectories in a persistent desktop environment. ReRail composes MyPCBench tasks into new long-horizon workflows with executable ground truth, collects verifier-confirmed failures from multiple policies, and turns them into verified recovery trajectories. CUA-Recovery comprises a human-verified test suite of 2,250 erroneous states over 100 tasks and a training suite of 8.6K trajectories over 377 tasks. Evaluation on the test suite shows that agents struggle both to recognize inherited errors and to complete the task. Across six agents and six takeover depths, Error Awareness Rate (EAR) reaches at most 44.0% and Pass@3 at most 25.5%. After fine-tuning Qwen3.5-35B-A3B on the training suite, its average EAR rises from 19.9% to 35.2% on held-out recovery states, and its Rubric Score goes from 5.0% to 16.1%. It also gains 15.3 points on MyPCBench and 10.8 points on Odysseys for Pass@3, showing that the training trajectories help both with recovery and with completing tasks from a clean start. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TEX: Test-Time Scaling Testing Agents via Execution-based Cross-Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27290)**  `arXiv:2610.27290`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Akash Gokul, Srijan Bansal, Yingbo Zhou, Semih Yavuz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-case generation is critical but remains challenging for language models. Existing test-time scaling methods either isolate candidates or aggregate traces, which do not scale to long-horizon tasks. We present TEX, a collaborative test-time scaling framework based on execution-driven cross-validation. TEX parallelizes agents to generate tests and patches, cross-executing each test against every patch to form a $K \times K$ feedback matrix. Agents use their matrix slices for refinement, enabling objective validation without verbose trace sharing. On SWT-Bench Verified, TEX achieves 86.4%, establishing a new state-of-the-art and improving +9.0% over single-agent baselines. Moreover, even random selection from TEX outputs (84.4%) surpasses the previous SOTA (84.0%), showing collaborative execution feedback improves solution quality, not just best-case samples. Our results highlight execution-grounded feedback as an effective scaling mechanism for long-horizon agentic tasks.
  </details>

- **[TxnQA: Benchmarking Analytical Reasoning and Evidence Grounding over Transaction Event Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27256)**  `arXiv:2610.27256`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Rabimba Karanjai, Yiming Bian, Yun-Shiuan Chuang, Jenna L. Ballard, Zijie Pan, Shivani Shekhar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analyzing merchant transaction histories requires dynamic temporal filtering, entity resolution, and aggregation over irregular event streams--operations poorly captured by static tabular or time-series forecasting benchmarks. We introduce TxnQA, an analytical QA benchmark comprising 27 task families (81 templates) alongside a 5-scenario fraud-risk reasoning suite that evaluates both verdicts and supporting evidence over synthetic, privacy-mitigated transaction data. Ground-truth answers are computed programmatically, and model performance is decomposed into format-following rate (FFR), conditional content accuracy (CA), and end-to-end correctness (E2E). Evaluating prompted proprietary baselines and six LoRA-adapted open-weight models shows that adaptation reliably enforces output schemas but leaves arithmetic bottlenecks: Gemma-4-31B-IT and Qwen3-8B attain E2E scores of 0.867 and 0.816, whereas evaluated MoE checkpoints reach only 0.632-0.713. Furthermore, on the 500-instance fraud suite, verdict accuracy frequently exceeds 80%, yet joint accuracy (requiring exact evidence attribution) collapses to at most ~23%. These findings identify schema compliance, semantic calculation, and evidence attribution as three distinct bottlenecks in transaction-sequence QA.
  </details>

- **[ReuF2F: Benchmarking Coding Agents on Formalized Undergraduate Research Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27046)**  `arXiv:2610.27046`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zeyu Zheng, Yiyang Liu, Yuyang Zhao, Congmin Zheng, Jiachen Zhu, Jianian Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI systems begin to resolve open research problems, reliable verification becomes increasingly important. Formal mathematics provides a trusted framework for mathematical reasoning, enabling agents to produce machine-checkable proofs while conducting mathematical exploration. Existing formal mathematics benchmarks often focus on known results or a small set of prominent open problems, which can become difficult to evaluate reliably once solutions are discovered and widely disseminated. Existing evaluation protocols are also not always aligned with how modern coding agents operate. We therefore introduce ReuF2F, a Lean 4 benchmark of 244 formalized open problems drawn from Research Experiences for Undergraduates (REU), spanning entry-level open research problems across a broad range of mathematical subfields. We expect these less prominent open problems, which tend to attract less attention and may remain unsolved for longer, to be better suited for held-out evaluation of end-to-end open problem resolution in Lean. We adopt a standard coding-agent evaluation setting, placing each problem and its logical negation into a Lean project in a Docker container. We also build Leanimum-agent on mini-SWE-agent to provide a unified harness for fair comparisons across models. Our evaluation finds that even the best-performing model resolves only ~40% of the problems, leaving substantial room for improvement on formalized open research problems.
  </details>

- **[Staged Continual Adaptation of Multimodal Foundation Models for Japanese Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27904)**  `arXiv:2610.27904`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Genshin Kakimoto, Atsushi Yanagisawa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Staged post-training of multimodal foundation models chains vision-language alignment, reasoning distillation, and domain tuning, yet how each phase trades capabilities against the others remains under-characterised. Tracking an 8.4B-parameter model from an un-fine-tuned baseline through three training phases on Japanese financial documents (evaluated on five text-input benchmarks), we find the phases interact non-additively: math-only distillation overwrites the financial knowledge acquired earlier even as procedural math skill survives, and the domain phase repairs more than it installs. A skip-phase ablation is best for domain knowledge but forfeits numerical-reasoning gains, so per-phase contributions are not decomposable and the best checkpoint is task-dependent, with implications for checkpoint retention and scheduling rather than added stages.
  </details>

- **[Harness Self-Evolution for Long-Horizon Terminal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27435)**  `arXiv:2610.27435`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ruhan Wang, Yucheng Shi, Zongxia Li, Junyao Yang, Zhongzhi Li, Yue Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon terminal agents may solve a task in one run yet fail across repeated runs. Stochastic errors can propagate through actions and context, creating a gap between capability and reliable execution. We present RiseHarness, a framework for iteratively improving agent harnesses with model weights fixed. It focuses on flipping tasks with both successful and failed runs under the same configuration, using successful runs as references for failure analysis. RiseHarness combines agent trajectories with internal harness traces from a runtime monitor to examine how model actions interact with command execution, feedback, context management, and completion decisions. A Harness Handbook links this evidence to harness behaviors and source code, helping identify recurring problems in mechanisms shared across tasks. RiseHarness then searches other harness frameworks for relevant implementations and adapts them as design references to the current model and execution protocol. Candidate changes undergo behavioral checks and repeated task evaluation; versions with measured gains seed the next iteration. This loop integrates suitable mechanisms from different frameworks into a single harness and uses new execution evidence to guide further changes. We evaluate task success and reliability across models, ablate harness execution evidence and external research, and test transfer to unseen benchmarks with the same model. RiseHarness treats a specific model as the unit of harness adaptation, studying how changes to shared interaction mechanisms can make existing capabilities more reliable across tasks.
  </details>

- **[More Memory, Worse Agents: Error Reproduction and Anti-Persistence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27406)**  `arXiv:2610.27406`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ruhan Wang, Kishan Panaganti, Dongruo Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can improve across tasks without updating model parameters by storing experience from their own executions in external memory and reusing it to guide later decisions. A central design question is whether to retain only observed trajectories and outcomes or also the rules, summaries, and skills inferred from them. Retaining these abstractions allows agents to reuse strategies without repeatedly deriving them from raw trajectories. However, errors in stored abstractions can propagate to subsequent decisions through retrieval and reuse. They can also shape execution trajectories, which then become the basis for new abstractions that reproduce the original errors. We term this feedback mechanism error reproduction: errors are regenerated through a cycle of abstraction, execution, and re-abstraction, extending their persistence beyond direct reuse of the original faulty knowledge. We propose Anti-Persistence, a memory design that accumulates trajectories and outcomes while reconstructing task-specific guidance on demand. An adaptive selector uses prior outcomes to decide whether and how to synthesize guidance, which is discarded after execution. This prevents its direct reuse as a stored object, although indirect feedback through retained trajectories remains possible. Across different benchmarks and backbones, Anti-Persistence achieves higher overall held-out success and the lowest average LLM-call cost among the evaluated memory designs. These results support separating experience accumulation from abstraction persistence in memorybased self-improvement.
  </details>

- **[VibeTraining-Bench: Data-Trainer-Harness Co-Design for Iterative System Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26342)**  `arXiv:2610.26342`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ruotian Peng, Yuanyi Ren, Shuaiqi Liu, Xin Guo, Fang Zhu, Yangyi Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires agents to diagnose system bottlenecks and coordinate improvements across training and inference. We refer to the agents' capability to jointly optimize training data, the trainer, and the inference harness as cross-component co-design. We introduce VibeTraining-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this capability across tasks spanning terminal operation, software engineering, multi-turn tool use, and mathematical reasoning. Within each task, research agents start from an identical initial agent system under a fixed resource budget. Guided by validation feedback, they iteratively refine data, trainers, and inference harnesses, then select and submit a final agent system for evaluation on the test set. To prevent reward hacking, we enforce three safeguards: test data isolation, separate research and grading containers, and network restrictions for research agents. Our evaluation results show that frontier research agents substantially improve target agent systems. For example, on SWE-bench Verified, GPT-6-Astra raises the target agent system's performance from 32.9% to 53.7%, comparable to the human-developed instruct model's 55.4%. However, there is still room to improve their ability to co-design and iteratively refine data, trainer, and harness to achieve cumulative performance gains. We hope that VibeTraining-Bench will serve as a unified platform for advancing RSI, deepening our understanding of cross-component optimization for agent systems. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Observability for Autonomous Systems Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28611)**  `arXiv:2610.28611`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jitendra Solanki, Anand Todkar, Ziran Min_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern distributed microservice deployments generate massive volumes of heterogeneous, multi-format observability logs across physically and logically partitioned infrastructure. Isolating root causes during cascading failures remains an arduous task for Site Reliability Engineers (SREs), bottlenecked by manual keyword filtering and disjointed temporal correlation across monitoring dashboards. We introduce Semantic Observability, an autonomous multi-agent framework that unifies disparate log streams into a dynamic, queryable Property Graph Database. By modeling physical infrastructure topologies, logical software dependencies, and chronological event sequences as attributed nodes and directed relations, it bridges the semantic gap inherent in flat, unstructured log repositories. Building on this unified substrate, we deploy a stateful multi-agent system orchestrated through Directed Cyclic Graphs (DCGs). The system autonomously navigates the topology, applying causal graph algorithms and temporal back-traversal to isolate the precise blast radius of anomalies. The pruned subgraph is then interpreted by a Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning engine to generate contextualized, code-level Root Cause Analyses (RCA) and actionable remediations. This paper establishes the foundational system architecture, formal relational ontology, and multi-agent control loops of Semantic Observability. We show conceptually how the framework eliminates ambient telemetry noise, reduces Mean Time to Resolution (MTTR), compresses LLM token consumption, and prevents structural hallucinations. We also specify an open-source validation framework on standard benchmarks (LogHub and Train-Ticket), laying the groundwork for a forthcoming companion paper on exhaustive quantitative evaluation and production benchmarking.
  </details>

- **[What Do RTL Repair Benchmarks Actually Measure?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28595)**  `arXiv:2610.28595`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yeyin Eva Zhu, Rishabh Ranawat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RTL repair benchmarks should measure whether a model can fix broken RTL given a specification. To this end, we construct and hash-bind RTLRepairDataset, 5,784 (broken, fixed) training pairs; RTLRepairBench, 459 held-out cases with 374 syntactic and 85 behavioral mutations; and an evaluation harness supporting simulation and formal equivalence. We then use this infrastructure to identify two measurement confounds. First, simulation and formal scoring give opposite signs for the apparent effect of providing a specification in one evaluated cohort; reset-aware rescoring attributes almost all of the simulation-formal disagreement to reset and first-cycle comparison semantics. Second, on 274 mined model failures, the specification-only minus specification-conditioned recovery point estimate ranges from -8.5 to +18.3 percentage points across six models. Thus, a conventional repair score need not isolate whether the model used the faulty RTL. We translate these findings into evaluation controls: report initialization, reset, first-cycle, and unknown-value semantics; disclose oracle coverage and undecided cases; and include a specification-only baseline. Together, the resources and audit provide both a benchmark for behavioral repair and a protocol for diagnosing what contributes to its measured score. We release the benchmark, the training set, construction code, sanitized aggregate evidence, and reproduction materials atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Sequentiality Bottleneck: A Hybrid Diffusion-Autoregressive Framework for Efficient Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28584)**  `arXiv:2610.28584`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Cai, Jian Zhao, Yuchen Yuan, Xuelong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive models (ARMs) face a "Sequentiality Bottleneck" in reasoning, while diffusion language models (DLMs) offer parallelism but lack semantic coherence. To addresses both limitations, we propose Collaborative Reasoning Asymmetric Learning (CoRAL), a hybrid framework via an Asymmetric Planner-Executor (APE) architecture: a DLM Planner generates K diverse reasoning trajectories in parallel, while a large ARM Executor selects the optimal plan and synthesizes the final answer. To train the Planner effectively, we introduce Reward-Guided Denoising for CoT (RDC), which integrates outcome-based rewards into the diffusion process to steer it toward goal-directed reasoning. Experiments on vision-language benchmarks show that CoRAL significantly outperforms prior diffusion-based methods and achieves competitive performance against strong ARMs, achieving both superior efficiency and quality.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Ensemble with Vision-Language Models for Visual Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28560)**  `arXiv:2610.28560`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shangquan Sun, Wenjun Luo, Xu He, Yoonsoo Lee, Shijian Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual anomaly detection aims to detect abnormal objects, while anomaly samples are often scarce during model training. Assembling multiple pretrained detectors can help by leveraging their transferable image scores and anomaly maps. However, most existing ensemble methods apply fixed fusion rules over saved predictions which struggle when pretrained detectors clearly disagree due to different training objectives, priors, and visual representations. To this end, we design AnomalyAgent, a post-training fusion framework for accurate visual anomaly detection. AnomalyAgent aligns heterogeneous scores and maps, summarizes score- and region-level disagreement, and treats fusion as a finite action-selection problem. It implements two types of actions: 1) score actions that determine how to fuse image-level detector confidence via consensus, robust aggregation, detector weighting, or score-map consistency; 2) map actions that fuse dense anomaly responses via consensus, detector weighting, geometric aggregation, or disagreement correction. A constrained vision-language model (VLM) is introduced to inspect high-conflict samples and select valid score action and map action to produce the final prediction. We evaluate AnomalyAgent on MVTec AD and VisA as primary benchmarks, together with seven additional public anomaly benchmarks and Real-IAD for large-scale score-level evaluation. Across these benchmarks, AnomalyAgent consistently improves over equal-weight fusion and achieves the best mean image AUROC and pixel AP among the compared fusion methods on MVTec AD and VisA, while demonstrating robust transfer across additional anomaly domains.
  </details>

- **[DecisionPivot: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Decision Models in LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28522)**  `arXiv:2610.28522`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xialie Zhuang, Mingcong Lei, Zunhai Su, Ri Liu, Jianjin Li, Peixian Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models support routing, guardrails, and output verification in LLM systems. However, item accuracy alone cannot establish whether models make correct decisions across controlled variations or assign reliable confidence to their predictions. We introduce DecisionPivot, a diagnostic benchmark comprising 48 tasks and 40,033 items across seven decision roles. Its core track contains 7,800 items from 13 generated task families, organized into contrast groups with computed answers verified by independently implemented solvers. The complementary reference track contains 32,233 items from 35 public test sets, preserving their original evaluation metrics. We evaluate 40 decision models and 14 general models using documented, model-specific protocols, assessing item and contrast-group accuracy, probability ordering, calibration, and empirical selective coverage. Our results reveal substantial differences obscured by aggregate scores: two 27B models separated by only 2.6 points on the reference track achieve contrast-group accuracies of 70.1% and 44.2%. Across 2,400 binary contrast pairs, Perplexity Decider correctly ranks the positive member above the negative member in 92.3% of pairs but answers both members correctly in only 74.1%, exposing a gap between probability ordering and decision correctness. Models with similar item accuracy also differ in the reliability of their high-confidence predictions. On a separate 1,296-item subset, GLM-5.3 achieves 93.5\% contrast-group accuracy at a higher reasoning setting, compared with 81.5% at its lowest effort. By linking errors to pivotal details and difficulty levels, DecisionPivot provides fine-grained diagnostics of decision failures that aggregate evaluation alone cannot reveal.
  </details>

- **[Stratified Sparse Attention: Reducing the Sparse-to-Dense Gap at Extreme Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28441)**  `arXiv:2610.28441`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sahil Joshi, Prithvi Dixit, Aryan Katiyar, Agniva Chowdhury, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Anshumali Shrivastava, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model inference over long contexts is increasingly bottlenecked by the cost of attending to a growing Key-Value (KV) cache. Sparse attention reduces this cost by accessing only a fraction of the cache, but existing methods face a trade-off between deterministic retrieval, which captures high-scoring tokens but discards residual attention mass, and sampling, which improves coverage at the cost of higher variance. Hybrid methods combine top-$k$ retrieval with uniform sampling, yet still exhibit a substantial gap from dense attention at aggressive sparsity. We introduce \emph{Stratified Sparse Attention (SSA)}, a unified framework combining deterministic retrieval with score-guided sampling. SSA deterministically attends to high-scoring tokens while sampling the residual using proxy attention scores and correcting for their inclusion probabilities, thereby recovering attention mass beyond the retrieval set. SSA encompasses top-$k$, top-$p$, and hybrid top-$k$+sampling as special cases. Across RULER, LongBench, LOFT, and AIME, and five models ranging from 3B to 27B parameters, SSA consistently improves over the baselines at matched sparsity. On RULER-HARD with Ministral-3-8B, SSA improves accuracy over the strongest practical baseline from 81.8\% to 84.2\% at 32K and from 63.8\% to 69.3\% at 128K under $50\times$ attention sparsity, recovering 94\% and 85\% of dense-attention performance, respectively. With optimized CUDA kernels, SSA achieves up to $2.96\times$ decode speedup over FlashInfer at 1M context, demonstrating that its accuracy gains translate into practical long-context inference acceleration. We open-source our code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DIVE: Benchmarking Full-Duplex Interaction with Visual Engagement in Continuous Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28405)**  `arXiv:2610.28405`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhang Chen, Guangyue Liu, Shilong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have substantially improved spoken interaction and video understanding. Existing online and streaming benchmarks primarily assess causal multimodal perception through timestamped question answering, whereas real-world interaction requires models to continuously identify task-relevant multimodal cues and decide whether, when, and how to respond. This leaves open how well current models can sustain real-time interaction throughout an evolving multimodal stream. To address this gap, we introduce DIVE, a benchmark for evaluating full-duplex interaction with visual engagement under continuous and realistic multimodal streams. It organizes six tasks into three complementary interaction scenarios. (1) Proactive Intervention evaluates whether models can identify emerging visual or semantic cues and intervene at the appropriate moment. (2) Continuous Engagement evaluates sustained participation through continuous visual tracking and visually grounded query interaction. (3) Interaction Coordination evaluates how models coordinate listening and speaking under overlapping and multi-party interactions. DIVE contains 525 audio/video samples with fine-grained human annotations and precise temporal metadata. Its evaluation protocol separates response quality, participation decisions, and response timing. We evaluate representative real-time models using response quality, window response rates, and reaction latency. Experiments show that leading real-time models achieve sub-second average response latency, yet their average window response rates remain at or below 53.77%. Response quality varies substantially across models and tasks, with adapting ongoing responses to newly arriving information remaining particularly challenging. We hope DIVE facilitates progress toward more natural and capable real-time multimodal interactive systems.
  </details>

- **[FALCA: Falsification-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Hierarchical Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28361)**  `arXiv:2610.28361`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zijun Dou, Kun Luo, Chaofan Li, Shuqi Lu, Zheng Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon deep search requires LLM agents to iteratively decompose complex information needs, gather evidence from external sources, and maintain a reliable research state under multiple constraints. A central challenge is that early mistakes can be amplified over the search trajectory: agents may anchor on an incorrect candidate, collect partially supporting evidence, overlook contradictions, and accumulate errors that misguide subsequent reasoning. We propose FALCA, a falsification-aware framework that formulates deep search as a propose-falsify process. FALCA adopts a collaborative Searcher-Falsifier architecture, where the Searcher proposes candidate answers or sub-answers, while independent Falsifiers actively seek counterevidence, check unmet constraints, identify conflicts, and produce concise falsification reports for research-state revision. To strengthen this loop, we introduce falsification-aware training, combining SFT warmup on falsification trajectories with agentic RL based on parallel rollouts and hierarchical credit assignment over research stages, state transitions, and actions. Experiments on five search benchmarks show that FALCA improves long-horizon deep search performance over backbone and harness ablations, while reducing confirmation bias and error accumulation through more effective falsification and research-state revision.
  </details>

- **[Quantum-JEPA: Quantum Born Priors for Latent-Action JEPA World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27921)**  `arXiv:2610.27921`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyuan Liu, Sicheng Liu, Haiyu Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A single observation can admit several plausible futures, making uncertainty an essential part of world modelling. However, quantum generative models remain underexplored for this task. We introduce Quantum-JEPA, a latent-action JEPA framework that learns distributions of future representations using a conditional quantum Born prior. Circuit measurements generate binary latent actions, which a shared predictor maps to alternative future representations. We train the quantum prior through the quality and diversity of these predictions using a sampled energy objective. Our model learns distinct continuations on constructed multimodal tasks and captures latent-action correlations that support coordinated multi-agent futures. On D3IL, a benchmark of diverse human demonstrations, our trained models generate multiple obstacle-crossing routes from a common starting point without observed motion. On the controlled tasks, our classical and quantum prior implementations improve prediction validity and distributional fidelity over the evaluated variational baselines. Our quantum priors combine competitive predictive quality with a parameter-efficient sampling structure: they avoid an exponentially sized categorical output layer while supporting correlated latent actions. We further validate finite-shot parameter-shift training, including training with readout noise. Unlike most prior work, which focuses on simulation, we also verify the feasibility of inference with trained priors on a real QPU. These results establish a trainable quantum component for JEPA future prediction and a basis for exploring quantum-enhanced world models at larger scales.
  </details>

- **[MMEnterprise-Bench: Evaluating Enterprise Agents over Evolving Multimodal Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27911)**  `arXiv:2610.27911`  `cs.AI`  
  _Seohyun Lee, Jongha Kim, Dohwan Ko, Ji Soo Lee, Seoung Choi, Jayun Ku, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise agents answer questions over workplace records that evolve through team collaboration. As information is shared, corrected, and revised across documents, chats, and figures, multiple versions of the same information accumulate in the workspace. Reliable question answering therefore requires agents to reason over sufficient evidence, resolve cross-source and temporal conflicts, and recognize when the available evidence is insufficient to establish an answer. To systematically evaluate these capabilities, we introduce MMEnterprise-Bench, a benchmark comprising 1,613 questions grounded in evolving multimodal records from simulated collaboration projects, covering three categories: Evidence Reasoning, Conflict Resolution, and Unanswerability. We evaluate 10 proprietary and open-source multimodal LLMs and find that even the strongest models struggle when evidence contains cross-source or temporal conflicts or spans multiple images. Moreover, increasing the retrieval budget or employing a larger retriever improves evidence recall but does not consistently translate into higher answer accuracy. These findings highlight the persistent challenges of reasoning over evolving workplace records and establish MMEnterprise-Bench as a testbed for developing more reliable enterprise agents. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[COMPASS: Routing via Capability-Oriented Model Profiling with Adaptive Search, Selection, and Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27822)**  `arXiv:2610.27822`  `cs.AI`  
  _Amin Rezaei, Pouya Parsa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM routing aims to select a capable model at low cost, but specialized routers can require updates as models and tasks change. We propose COMPASS, an agentic router in which an LLM follows a routing recipe, gathers performance and cost evidence through tools, and optionally inspects candidate answers before selecting a model. Our evaluations on LLMRouterBench and DeepSWE show that agentic routing can improve accuracy and lower model-call cost relative to specialized and trained routing baselines, without routing-specific training. On LLMRouterBench, COMPASS reaches 69.85% accuracy at \$139.57, compared with RLCascadeRouter's 69.14% at \$180.22. On DeepSWE under fixed five-fold cross-validation, it reaches 78.1% at \$215, compared with 74.1% at \$500 for the best single model. COMPASS also supports routing across benchmarks and adapts to an expanded model pool by reading new evaluation records, without changing the router's weights or prompt.
  </details>

- **[GRADUS: Discovering and Recovering Agent Lessons from the Environment's Latent Syllabus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27781)**  `arXiv:2610.27781`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qing Ping, Wei Yang, Panpan Xu, Johannes Kirmayr, Lukas Stappen, Junjie Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training LLM agents for policy-constrained tool environments, such as an airline backend with booking, refund, and cancellation policies, requires tasks that exercise the environment's rules individually and in combination. Existing synthesis pipelines let a model propose tasks and retain only those it immediately solves, biasing training data toward easier behaviors. We introduce Gradus (Graded Rungs of Ascending Difficulty from an Underlying System), which decouples what a task should teach from the concrete task and solution used to teach it. Without seed tasks, Gradus treats a fixed environment as a latent syllabus, extracting guarded behavioral operators that capture how actions depend on current conditions and how their effects reshape subsequent decisions. It composes these operators into increasingly demanding tasks while using corpus-level feedback to target missing outcome combinations. Crucially, Gradus jointly calibrates tasks and solutions against the environment: execution feedback identifies which side to refine while preserving the intended behavioral requirements. Verification thus becomes a recovery signal rather than a terminal filter, coupling coverage-guided exploration with the preservation of difficult examples. From off-the-shelf backbones, the resulting curriculum lifts a 30B model from 59% to 89% on $\tau^2$-Bench and from 47% to 83% on CAR-Bench with an Opus 4.5 proposer, and to 76% and 62%, respectively, with an open-weight Qwen3-235B proposer. These results show the value of jointly controlling which behaviors are explored and which survive into verified training data.
  </details>

- **[RITE: Evaluating End-to-End Resilience to Silent Tool Errors in E-Commerce Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27777)**  `arXiv:2610.27777`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenshuo Zhang, Yuquan Lin, Yushi Sun, Yishi Lin, Rui Sheng, Peng He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using LLM agents increasingly answer e-commerce queries through multi-step tool interactions. The expected output of a tool call can shift between agent development and execution, allowing a plausible but incorrect value to be returned without an explicit failure signal. These silent errors may propagate through subsequent decisions into final answers. To study agents' resilience across this end-to-end process, we formulate Silent-error Resilience Task, an interactive task that evaluates whether they complete the original query after a silent error corrupts an intermediate state. We introduce RITE, a benchmark built from human-authored industrial workflows and production-log-derived inputs. To enable controlled and reproducible evaluation, we use a semantic error taxonomy to inject errors into fixed workflow prefixes, freeze recorded tool behavior, and let agents continue freely. It contains 167 matched units across nine e-commerce workflows, yielding 501 silent-error, correct-value, and explicit-failure instances. Across 10 recent LLMs, silent errors roughly halve task success and are consistently harder than explicit failures. Recognition, use of an available verification path, and integration of the resulting evidence form successive bottlenecks. We also propose a light State Checker as an intervention to test whether resilience can be improved.
  </details>

- **[E8-QAT: Efficient 2-Bit Quantization-Aware Training with Implicit Lattice Codebooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27699)**  `arXiv:2610.27699`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Lei, Hong Huang, Jiebin Zhang, Hongyu Li, Jinlong Song, Decheng Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-bit quantization reduces the memory footprint of large language models, but preserving accuracy with efficient training remains challenging. Scalar quantization encodes weights independently, while explicit-codebook vector quantization introduces costly search during training. We propose E8-QAT, a 2-bit quantization-aware training framework that uses the $E_8$ lattice as an implicit eight-dimensional codebook. It jointly quantizes weight groups through arithmetic projection without storing or searching an explicit codebook during the training forward pass. A 2-bit core encoding and sparse extension preserve the selected lattice points exactly, adding less than 0.1\% to core index storage. On Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, E8-QAT matches the performance of the full-precision model in terms of the average score across the four tasks. E8-QAT improves over the state-of-the-art baseline by 2.03\% on IFEval and by 1.51\% on LiveCodeBench, under the same data, schedule, and bit width. The 2-bit representation reduces peak memory by 80\% and supports a $4\times$ larger batch. At each representation's largest feasible batch, E8-QAT reaches $1.97\times$ the decoding throughput of BF16.
  </details>

- **[EASformer: Environment-Action-State Alignment for Industrial Generative Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27691)**  `arXiv:2610.27691`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bohao Li, Dinglun Cao, Pengcheng Huang, Yingda Tang, Zhiting Zhao, Yi Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern industrial ranking systems increasingly adopt generative recommendation (GR) paradigms, in which user behavior sequences and non-sequential (NS) features are tokenized and jointly modeled by Transformer-style backbones. This unified formulation provides a scalable interface for modeling long histories and heterogeneous features. However, sequence and NS representations often carry asymmetric semantics: historical representations may combine environment context with observed-action semantics, whereas candidate-side NS representations contain no observed action but incorporate richer supplementary context, such as user-item interaction features and recent behavior lists. Direct interaction between such representations can make query-key dot products compare different semantic objects, limiting meaningful cross-token interaction. To fill this gap, we propose EASformer, an Environment-Action-State disentangled architecture for industrial app ranking. EASformer constructs an explicitly aligned context environment space shared by historical and non-sequence tokens, while disentangling user actions from historical token and candidate-specific context form non-sequence tokens. Based on this aligned space, EASformer enables complementary historical action retrieval and state-conditioned action prediction. Furthermore, the disentangled design supports a Cloze-style self-supervised objective by masking historical actions and reusing the candidate prediction pathway. On an offline benchmark from a real-world industrial app marketplace, EASformer achieves an AUC of 0.89411, yielding a 0.88% relative improvement over the deployed baseline. Online A/B tests further support this advantage, with relative eCPM lifts of 1.26% and 3.94% across two real-world ranking scenarios.
  </details>

- **[SCODEL: Shared Canonical Ontology Definitions for Food Entity Linking and Ontology-Grounded Recipe Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27637)**  `arXiv:2610.27637`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nuhu Ibrahim, Rishi Ravikumar, Ayuba Dauda, Fuqi Xu, Robert Stevens, Riza Batista-Navarro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying recipe entities and linking them to ontology concepts is fundamental for semantic recipe understanding. However, food mentions in recipes are often ambiguous, context-dependent, and lexically diverse, making ontology entity linking (EL) challenging. We propose SCODEL, a streamlined, model-agnostic approach based on a central idea: contextual food mentions and ontology concepts that refer to the same entity should learn the same canonical ontology-oriented definition. SCODEL uses these shared definitions as a semantic normalisation layer, providing complementary semantic information to existing EL models without requiring a new linking architecture. We further introduce ReciLinkD, the first benchmark for contextual food ontology entity linking in recipes. Experiments show that SCODEL consistently improves three modular EL models (ReFinED, GSM-EL, and BLINK): SCODEL x BLINK achieves the highest Hit@1 on seen concepts (88.33%), while SCODEL x GSM-EL achieves the highest Hit@1 on unseen concepts (83.15%), outperforming the strongest large language model (LLM) based EL baseline in each setting. Experiments on a biomedical ontology benchmark further demonstrate that SCODEL generalises beyond the food domain. Finally, we use SCODEL to ground food mentions at scale and construct an ontology-grounded recipe knowledge graph, demonstrating its utility for ontology-aware recipe retrieval and corpus-level food analysis.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Experience: Knowledge-Guided Evolution for NPU Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27588)**  `arXiv:2610.27588`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xi Chen, Ming Shen, Haotian Yan, Ruoyu Sun, Tianjun Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents have become capable kernel engineers on mainstream GPU stacks, but they struggle on emerging accelerators such as Ascend NPUs, whose programming model is barely represented in pretraining data. Recent NPU agents address this gap largely through experience: they accumulate memory from their own generation and optimization trials and reuse it on later tasks. We argue that experience alone is not enough for kernel generation. An agent can only remember what it has tried, and it rarely tries a mechanism it does not know. As a result, such memory offers little help when a new operator calls for a different mechanism, and even familiar operators raise unseen situations as optimization proceeds. We present ASCENDEVOLVE, which grounds every step of an evolutionary kernel search in a large external knowledge base built from platform documentation, toolkit headers, and operator repositories. Because this corpus far exceeds any context window, ASCENDEVOLVE indexes it and routes each agent to a small, task-specific slice of evidence instead of the corpus itself. The retrieved knowledge seeds diverse implementation strategies, guides repair when a candidate fails, and constrains how strong candidates are recombined. On 53 Ascend C operators from CANN-Bench spanning four difficulty levels, ASCENDEVOLVE achieves an overall score of 71.39, improving over Reflexion and OpenEvolve by 11.6% and 17.2%.
  </details>

- **[DuplexLedger: Benchmarking Transactional Memory in Full-Duplex Speech Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27581)**  `arXiv:2610.27581`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Li, Xiangchen Guan, Kunrong Li, Jin Wang, Kaiwen Luo, Yang Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full-duplex speech agents must update persistent memory while listening and speaking, yet it remains unclear which events should be stored, revised, or excluded. Existing benchmarks isolate interaction timing from memory outcomes, obscuring the state transitions behind a correct answer. We introduce DuplexLedger, a bilingual benchmark of 1,094 persistent sessions aligning four full-duplex scenarios with event-level Write/Update/Ignore/Retain supervision, explicit pre/post memory states, and 6,564 same-session questions and checks. Evaluating five full-duplex models reveals an interaction-memory gap: no system combines strong boundary control with coherent final-state memory, and the best final-state fact coverage is 2.01%. We propose ACT-Mem, an 8.92M-parameter sidecar comprising an event controller, a versioned transactional ledger, and a typed retriever coupled with a frozen MiniCPM-o 4.5 backbone. On 219 held-out sessions, ACT-Mem increases latest-value accuracy from 4.11% to 8.68% while reducing stale assertions and distractor adoption. It improves interaction boundaries but slows interruption stopping. These results motivate selective, auditable full-duplex memory rather than indiscriminate transcript retention. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RaspAssistant as an Agentic Framework for Automating Adsorption Simulation Research in Porous Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27572)**  `arXiv:2610.27572`  `cs.AI`  
  _Marko Petković, Susanne Gooijer, José Manuel Vicent-Luna, Vlado Menkovski, Sofía Calero_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Porous-material adsorption studies require coordinated structure preparation, force-field selection, simulation, validation, and interpretation, making end-to-end automation difficult. We test whether RaspAssistant, a hierarchical multi-agent framework, can carry an adsorption study from force-field preparation through validation and downstream separation analysis. Across twelve multi-agent workflow-reproduction runs spanning four adsorption and separation benchmarks, overall scores ranged from 8.50 to 10.00 on a rubric evaluating workflow correctness, simulation validity, and analysis consistency. In a separate eight-case force-field extraction benchmark, the mean weighted score was 0.967, combining parameter agreement and RASPA readiness.
