In open-ended studies, the system diagnosed and refined an acetylene force field and, after validating a CO$_2$ force field against MFI experiments, screened 197 pure-silica zeolites that passed automated input checks. These results demonstrate autonomous workflow execution after task specification and input provision: RaspAssistant coordinates, validates, adapts, and documents extended adsorption-simulation workflows while exposing model sensitivity and methodological limitations for expert review.
  </details>

- **[MemDev-Bench: Does Memory Help Code Agents?Benchmarking Memory Systems over Real Repository Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27524)**  `arXiv:2610.27524`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guorui Chen, Tao Liu, Zhenzhen Huang, Yidan Zhang, Liyu Jiang, Shuoyuan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Code agents increasingly work on evolving real repositories, and memory systems aim to help them reuse earlier experience. However, such experience is diverse and evolving, so it remains unclear how memory systems organize it and thereby affect code agents. Existing benchmarks cannot answer this reliably because they lack either temporally valid histories or control over prior-task relevance. In this paper, we present MemDev-Bench to evaluate how memory systems affect code agents over diverse repository histories. It pairs each task with relevance-annotated earlier tasks from the same repository to build histories with controlled relevance. We also build an automated pipeline that reliably makes these earlier tasks executable and verifiable, producing 4,869 historical tasks for 555 base tasks. Based on MemDev-Bench, our systematic evaluation of memory systems across diverse development scenarios yields three main findings. (1) Memory mainly improves fine-grained localization and editing, increasing functional correctness. (2) Memory also causes failures on otherwise solvable tasks even with related history, and unrelated history tends to increase such failures. These failures mainly stem from agents reusing memory without reassessing its applicability. (3) Compared with using memory systems, directly providing agents with raw historical trajectories substantially reduces their steps. Further analysis suggests that agents save these steps by reusing process information that compressed memories tend to omit. These results suggest that future memory systems should preserve process information and reassess memory applicability against current task context.
  </details>

- **[When Health Assistants Agree Too Readily: A Benchmark of Time-Critical Self-Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27518)**  `arXiv:2610.27518`  `cs.AI`  
  _Marilyn Zhang, Tianfeng Chen, Patricia M Pechter, Mark E. Whiting_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every day, more than 40 million people ask ChatGPT a health question, such as whether laptop posture explains a stiff neck and days of pounding headache. Posture is plausible, but meningitis is a time-critical possibility. We introduce SelfDx, a paired benchmark for whether models have the appropriate response behavior, a form of epistemic independence, in health questions where misplaced agreement with the user's self-diagnosis could delay urgent or emergency care. Each of 100 pairs contrasts a caution case, where a serious alternative remains possible, with an agreement case, where the user supplies evidence that rules out the alternative and it is safe for the model to agree. We find across 27 frontier models that they tend to inappropriately agree with the user's self-diagnosis in cases that require caution. Even the best current-generation model reaches only a 77.0% appropriate caution rate on time-critical condition cases. To address this gap, we experiment with three prompt variants that increase appropriate caution but reduce appropriate agreement by 9-35%, indicating excessive caution. In contrast, supervised fine-tuning on 128 balanced examples improves both rates together across three runs. We recommend post-training to improve appropriate caution without making models cautious on every case, with paired evaluation to verify both response behaviors.
  </details>

- **[ProMemAgent: Long-Horizon Proactive Assistance with Context-Aware Prospective Memory from Sensory Perceptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27400)**  `arXiv:2610.27400`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lilin Xu, Linyang He, Kaiyuan Hou, Yuang Fan, Minghui Zhao, Xuhai Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are moving beyond reactive responses toward proactive assistance that anticipates user needs without explicit requests. However, existing proactive agents primarily address immediate needs, while many everyday situations involve delayed intentions that can only be acted on hours or days later. Humans handle such intentions through prospective memory, the ability to remember to act on an intention when the right moment arises. In this paper, we introduce ProMemAgent, the first proactive agent to incorporate prospective memory over everyday sensory contexts for long-horizon proactive assistance. ProMemAgent organizes past sensory perceptions (e.g., egocentric video and audio) into context-aware memory, generating prospective cues and linking earlier events with subsequent developments for future retrieval. Given the current context, it retrieves relevant past events and performs memory-augmented proactive reasoning to determine whether assistance is needed. We further introduce CPMBench, the first benchmark for evaluating long-horizon proactive assistance over sensory contexts, comprising 1,000 instances across eight everyday categories. Extensive experiments show that ProMemAgent achieves the best memory retrieval performance and consistently outperforms all baselines on long-horizon proactive assistance.
  </details>

- **[REM: What to Store, When to Recall, What to Forget in Experience Memory for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27377)**  `arXiv:2610.27377`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyi Lisa Chen, Pedro Herrero-Vidal, Xiaohan Chen, Xiaojing Yu, Cris Liu, Bingqing Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents now autonomously complete long-horizon tasks, such as repository-level coding, through hundreds of think-act steps, yet the experience each run produces is rarely reused on the next. Prior memory systems concentrate on retrieval, finding the most relevant past trajectory. Retrieval cannot observe the running agent, so it cannot decide whether the agent needs an experience, at which point, or for how long. We introduce REM (Reusable Experience Memory), a framework that manages the memory lifecycle from inside the agent's think-act loop through three explicit decisions. What to store: each completed trajectory is consolidated into a phase-structured document, two orders of magnitude smaller than the trajectory. When to recall: the document is injected at the start of the task; the costlier implementation record is withheld until an in-loop gate detects the agent struggling. What to forget: the record is evicted after a single exposure. On repository-level bug fixing (SWE-ContextBench Lite), REM retrieves the related trajectory more accurately than every state-of-the-art memory system (68.7% vs. 55.6% match@1) and improves, in every replicate, end-task resolution (27.6% vs. 24.9%) at 17% lower cost. Injecting the same experience as a summary or raw trajectory, even when oracle-retrieved, does not. Ablating any of the three decisions reduces resolution. Altogether, these results demonstrate that the bottleneck in experience reuse is not retrieval fidelity but how experience is stored and governed across its lifecycle, opening a control-theoretic perspective on agent memory.
  </details>

- **[Unsupervised Tool Evolution for Coding Agents in Data-Lake Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27327)**  `arXiv:2610.27327`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuqing Yang, Yurong Liu, Deqing Fu, Manav Garg, Sravan Babu Bodapati, Fatma Ozcan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents can answer analytical questions over a data lake by discovering, cleaning, and joining the relevant data. However, they discard this work after each question and redo it from scratch for the next one. Tool evolution, which automatically builds and refines reusable tools across tasks, is a natural fit for this repetition. Since a ground-truth label for a data-lake question requires an expert to solve it first, we consider the unsupervised setting. We propose TEAL (Tool Evolution for Analytics over data Lakes), which evolves a library of tools and usage skills for a given Worker agent without labels. Two agents propose tools from complementary evidence, an Explorer from the data lake itself and a Critic from Worker trajectories. A proposed change is kept only if the Worker adopts the tool on a rerun without extra steps and any changed answer is supported by trajectory evidence. We also introduce TEAL-Q, which generates synthetic questions for tool evolution when real ones are unavailable. We evaluate on KramaBench and LakeQA with four Worker LLMs under step budgets, which cap the number of steps the Worker may take per question. With a tight budget of 10 or 15 steps, TEAL improves every Worker on both benchmarks, lifting Sonnet-5 at 15 steps by 9.3 points on KramaBench and 21.7 points on LakeQA. A tighter budget also yields better feedback for evolution. For example, Sonnet-5's LakeQA library evolved at 20 steps and deployed unchanged at 30 steps scores 68.3, above both the baseline (59.6) and a library evolved directly at 30 steps (64.2). Additionally, a library evolved on synthetic questions performs comparably to one evolved on real questions. These results show that useful tools can be evolved without labels, and hopefully inspire future work on what to evolve and how to obtain feedback in unsupervised agent evolution.
  </details>

- **[Evolving Self-tuning Benchmarks for Long-Horizon Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27307)**  `arXiv:2610.27307`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hyunji Alex Nam, Michael Dennis, Natasha Jaques, Minsuk Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For AI assistants to move beyond simple chat interfaces and reliably assist users with applications like Gmail, Venmo, and Slack, we need to improve their tool-use capabilities. However, existing tool use benchmarks are still limited and difficult to scale because they rely on human supervision or are too domain-specific. To make data generation scalable, we propose, TURTLE (**T**ool **U**se **R**elated **T**asks with **L**ong-Horizon **E**volution), an Unsupervised Environment Design (UED) approach to evolve existing tasks using a proposer, a solver, and a feedback generator large language model agents. The proposer is rewarded for difficult yet solvable tasks based on the solver's empirical performance, and the feedback generator summarizes the solver's execution traces for the proposer to exploit. TURTLE produces evolving, self-tuning test suites adaptable to different environment requirements, from supporting user simulators and dense document retrieval to interacting across multiple apps. Empirical results on AppWorld, $\tau$-Bench, and AutomationBench using Gemma 4 31B-it show that the evolved tasks target the desired difficulty range (e.g., a 165\% increase in the percentage of hard tasks on AppWorld), increase task diversity (in terms of reasoning and simultaneous tool interactions), and elicit a broader range of failure modes than the original tasks.
  </details>

- **[Vulnerability Analysis for Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27293)**  `arXiv:2610.27293`  `cs.AI`  
  _Taozheng Zhu, Shixiong Jiang, Fanxin Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (SMARL) aims to learn cooperative control policies under prescribed safety constraints. Al- though existing SMARL methods focus on enforcing safety during policy optimization, their vulnerability remains largely unexplored. This paper investigates inference-time observation attacks against SMARL, where an adversary perturbs the observations of a subset of agents, thereby altering the actions produced by their policies. We propose MinMax Attack, an attack framework that jointly selects adversarial target actions for compromised agents while accounting for the Cooperative Resistance of benign agents. We further derive a bound on the induced safety cost and characterize its relation- ship with the Compromised-Agent Budget and the Perturbation Budget. Experiments across multiple safety-critical multi-agent benchmarks and representative state-of-the-art SMARL algorithms show that MinMax Attack induces substantially higher safety costs than existing attack baselines while preserving task reward, thereby reducing its detectability and achieving both attack effectiveness and stealthiness.
  </details>

- **[EGI-BENCH: An Enterprise General Intelligence Benchmark Suite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27234)**  `arXiv:2610.27234`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiwei Liu, Zhujun Lan, Jielin Qiu, Zixiang Chen, Liangwei Yang, Ming Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise adoption of LLMs in agentic workflows is gated by evaluations that span a wide arc of abilities: CRM operations, heterogeneous-document retrieval, personal data understanding, multi-turn planning, tool use, function calling, long-horizon research, and long-context software engineering. These evaluations live today in separate repositories with divergent runners, dependencies, and scoring conventions, which makes cross-benchmark comparison difficult and obscures what a single aggregate number actually represents. We release EGI-BENCH, a unified LLM agent benchmark suite that consolidates eight enterprise facing agent benchmarks (CRMARENA, HERB, PERSONABENCH, USERBENCH, MCP-UNIVERSE, MCPEVAL, BENCHFORCE, LOCOBENCH) behind a single command-line interface with two runtime-budgeted execution modes. On top of the suite we define the Enterprise General Intelligence (EGI) Score, a six-dimensional framework (M1-M6) whose buckets are populated by fine-grained sub-metrics routed from each benchmark rather than by benchmark-level averages. We describe the suite, the sub-metric routing, and the quick/deep budgets, and report results for fourteen frontier and cost-efficient LLMs spanning the GPT, Claude, and Gemini families under quick mode. Top-line EGI ranges from 39.6 (GPT-4o mini) to 65.6 (gpt-5.5), and no single model dominates every capability axis: gpt-5.5 leads task outcome, planning, and retrieval grounding; Claude Sonnet 4.5 leads tool actioning and safety/abstention; and Claude Haiku 4.5 leads engineering quality. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sparse RL Relaxation for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27220)**  `arXiv:2610.27220`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiabin Fan, Matthias Lindemann, Zita Marinho, André FT Martins, Lili Mou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work has explored continuous representations in place of discrete tokens during Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, often referred to as soft CoT. This is motivated by the idea that a mixture of token embeddings can represent a superposition of multiple reasoning possibilities within a single step. However, it remains challenging to train large language models to realize this potential. Existing work has explored reinforcement learning (RL)-style training for soft CoT. However, these approaches treat soft tokens as actions in a Markov decision process rather than as differentiable intermediate computation steps. Thus, they do not propagate gradients across the soft tokens and miss out on the signal of how the choice of one soft token affects the next. In this paper, we propose a sparse relaxation method for RL (called SpaRLax), which leverages the differentiability of soft-CoT reasoning over time steps and makes training more stable and effective. We adopt an entmax sparsity method that adaptively determines the degree of softness, which truncates long-tail noise at confident steps while retaining multiple plausible candidates when uncertain. Across nine reasoning benchmarks, our method achieves the highest average performance compared with baseline methods. Further analyses validate the effective use of soft-token superposition in our approach, and also demonstrate the contribution of adaptive sparsity in our approach.
  </details>

- **[EDMA: Entropy-Driven Multimodal Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27120)**  `arXiv:2610.27120`  `cs.AI`  
  _Emanuele Mezzi, Gertjan Burghouts, Fabio Massacci, Mengyuan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal question answering (MMQA) is based on integrating heterogeneous data sources, selectively leveraging relevant modalities, and ignoring distractors. Existing approaches based on Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) improve performance by splitting the task into intermediate steps, but do not quantify how each modality contributes to reaching the final answer. We argue that quantifying the effect that each modality has on the answering process improves process traceability and final performance. Thus, we propose Entropy-Driven Multimodal Answering (EDMA), which uses logical entropy to formalise multimodal QA as an iterative process of entropy reduction and to quantify the contribution of each modality in distinguishing correct from incorrect candidate answers. This enables the construction of quantifiable answering trajectories, where each step is associated with a measurable entropy reduction. EDMA introduces (i) modality-conditioned partitioning to estimate unimodal entropy reduction, (ii) entropy-driven multimodal fusion to capture complementary information across modalities, and (iii) entropy-based selection of the answering trajectory that minimises the most logical entropy. Evaluated across standard MMQA benchmarks, EDMA outperforms competitive prompting baselines, with average gains of 12.8 F1 and 4.6 LLM-J points over Planning Prompting and 18.6 F1 and 8.3 LLM-J points over Chain-of-Thought. Moreover, EDMA outperforms self-consistency baselines and is competitive with the multi-agent system baseline. Moreover, we show that higher entropy correlates with more false positives and, through controlled trajectory selection, demonstrate that logical entropy serves as an effective, monotonic selector for correct reasoning paths, validating entropy level as a reliable proxy for performance.
  </details>

- **[PROVISO: Provenance-Informed Action Selection for Secure LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27115)**  `arXiv:2610.27115`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bo Yan, Qi Zhang, Ziwen Kan, Fnu Suya, Song Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents act on the external world through tools and third-party skills, so a single executed action can cause irreversible harm. Existing defenses judge a proposed action without tracking whether its content comes from the user or from external sources such as tool outputs, and they judge it in isolation rather than against an available alternative, which forces a trade-off between security and utility. We present PROVISO, a controller that decides which action to commit after the agent proposes an action and before it is executed. PROVISO encodes trusted observations, external observations, the proposed action, and an alternative into provenance-typed memory, relates them through role-specific queries, and commits the action with the highest calibrated value learned from outcomes. On four agent security tasks spanning the agent lifecycle, no baseline exceeds PROVISO in both security and utility. PROVISO improves F1 over the strongest baseline by 13.84 points on SkillsBench and by 7.07 points on ToolSafe, and it reaches 88-99 F1 on three additional skill detection benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[SPEM: Structure-Aware Procedural Memory for Efficient Skill Use in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26995)**  `arXiv:2610.26995`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ran Zhou, Zehui Kong, Fanyu Lin, Jiaheng Liu, Zhen Zhang, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents increasingly rely on reusable skills. However, scaling skill libraries makes efficient and accurate skill use difficult. Full skill loading incurs high token and runtime overhead. In contrast, flat semantic retrieval treats skills as independent text entries, ignoring dependencies, execution order, state transitions, and data flow constraints. We propose SPEM, a lightweight structure-aware procedural memory framework for efficient skill use in LLM-based agents. SPEM maintains Atomic Skill Memory for individual skills and Skill-DAG Memory for reusable compositions derived from successful executions. For a new task, SPEM reuses high-confidence Skill-DAG templates when available. Otherwise, it retrieves atomic skills and invokes Skill-GNN for state- and structure-aware skill selection. Successful executions are written back as new Skill-DAGs, allowing procedural memory to accumulate over time. Experiments on ALFWorld and SkillsBench show that SPEM achieves the best overall effectiveness--efficiency trade-off, reaching 87.1% ALFWorld success and 38.38% SkillsBench reward while reducing token and runtime costs against strong baselines.
  </details>

- **[When Planning Problems Become Hard for LLMs: Separating Structural Complexity from Semantic Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26964)**  `arXiv:2610.26964`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mayukh Debnath, Adeyinka Adedigba, Rammohan Mallipeddi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) struggle with classical planning, but benchmarks rarely distinguish structural difficulty from the difficulty of interpreting symbolic vocabulary. We examine seven STRIPS domains with BFS-optimal plan lengths. For predicting structural difficulty without solving an instance, the zero-training delete-relaxation heuristic $h_{\mathrm{add}}$ outperforms learned alternatives on held-out domains (mean Spearman $|\rho|=0.931$), including ridge regression ($0.707$), object count ($0.657$), and the episodic retrieval architecture ARC ($0.400$). ARC also fails to outperform a linear model using the same features. To isolate semantic effects, we progressively rename predicates while preserving states, transitions, and optimal plans. Valid LLM planning success drops from $67.5\%$ to $40.0\%$ between $0\%$ and $100\%$ renaming, while refusals remain at $0\%$. An LLM supplied with ARC's conditioning information predicts difficulty in context better than trained ARC (mean $|\rho|=0.826$). Hybrid routing between an LLM and a symbolic planner depends more on calibration to the target LLM's outcomes than on the structural score selected. Thus, established symbolic heuristics already estimate structural difficulty well; improving hybrid planning requires modeling LLM behavior sensitive to predicate semantics rather than developing more structural predictors.
  </details>

- **[LightCRS: Lightweight Multi-Domain Conversational Recommendation System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26948)**  `arXiv:2610.26948`  `cs.AI`  
  _He Ma, Lan You, Qiyang Wu, Chen Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational recommender systems have recently adopted vision-language models to match user dialogue intents with multimodal product information. However, extending these methods to many product domains faces a dilemma: deploying a separately adapted VLM per domain scales cost linearly with the number of domains and requires routing each dialogue to the right model, while naively merging all domains into a single VLM degrades accuracy because product characteristics vary widely across categories. We propose LightCRS, a lightweight, query-conditioned framework for unified multi-domain multimodal conversational recommendation. LightCRS precomputes multimodal product representations once offline with frozen encoders. Online, a compact 5.3M-parameter module fuses this evidence for each dialogue via query-conditioned cross-attention, whose parameters are shared across all domains, and compares candidates jointly with an inter-candidate Transformer. To enable rigorous evaluation, we construct MM-R2A-25, a 25-domain benchmark pairing natural Reddit recommendation-seeking conversations with Amazon product listings. LightCRS achieves state-of-the-art average HR@1, NDCG@5, and MRR, improving HR@1 by 35.4\% over the strongest baseline while reducing per-query latency by 266 times. Joint multi-domain training itself improves HR@1 by 15.2\% over separately trained single-domain models.
  </details>

- **[MAMath-Bench: Process-Aware and Robust Evaluation of Multimodal Multi-Agent Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26943)**  `arXiv:2610.26943`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinping Wang, Jie Ma, Rui Xing, Wensong Guo, Xiayi Zhang, Jianhao Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mathematical reasoning is a fundamental capability for educational applications of Large Language Models (LLMs), where reliable problem solving requires not only correct answers but also coherent and verifiable reasoning processes. Multi-agent systems (MASs) have shown growing potential in this domain by leveraging collaboration and verification among multiple LLM agents. However, existing evaluations primarily focus on the correctness of final answers in text-only settings, offering limited insight into multimodal reasoning capabilities, the reliability of intermediate reasoning steps, and robustness against reasoning errors. To address these limitations, we introduce MAMath-Bench, a process-aware benchmark designed to evaluate the multimodal mathematical reasoning capabilities of MASs. MAMath-Bench comprises 1,003 problems spanning 11 mathematical categories, multiple difficulty levels, and several modalities, including text, images, and audio. It features fine-grained milestone annotations to evaluate systems across three dimensions: problem-solving correctness, reasoning-process reliability, and robustness to adversarial reasoning-chain perturbations. Our evaluation yields three key findings. First, multi-agent collaboration does not consistently improve final-answer accuracy over strong single LLMs. Second, collaboration can substantially enhance the coverage of intermediate reasoning, but such gains may come at the cost of increased reasoning redundancy. Third, current systems remain vulnerable to adversarial reasoning errors, particularly those involving subtle structural perturbations. The dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fix, Not Just Resample: Bounded Compiler-Guided Repair in Lean 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26942)**  `arXiv:2610.26942`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Deng, Chris Farber, Sonal Narasimha, Samskrithi Sivakumar, Yexiang Xue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated theorem provers often rely on sampling language models, but proof assistants like Lean reject each failed step with a precise error message --- information that pure sampling discards. To overcome this, we introduce a bounded, online step-level repair module. Our search samples $K$ candidate tactics at each step and repairs a failed tactic at most $R$ times. Instead of discarding failed tactics, we feed the compiler's error message to a pretrained repair model to fix individual rejected steps in place. This approach improves the solve rate over a sampling-only baseline across all tested candidate budgets ($K \in \{1,3,5,8\}$), achieving up to a $32\%$ relative gain and solving $82$ additional theorems on a $1{,}990$-theorem subset of the LeanDojo benchmark. Repair is not free: it trades extra runtime for reachable proofs. But the trade is predictable: repair pays most when sampling is constrained; its return is front-loaded, with the first repair attempt capturing $84\text{--}96\%$ of the total gain; and the gain comes almost entirely from shallow tactic-execution failures rather than structural errors such as type mismatches. This targeted repair successfully replicates on a second benchmark (Lean Workbook), nearly doubling the solve rate with zero regressions.
  </details>

- **[DataPraxis: Scaling Analytics Tasks via Verified Workspace Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26924)**  `arXiv:2610.26924`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nan Jia, Wenxuan Shi, Jingqing Ruan, Zhizhao Zeng, Tianyi Zhai, Zhongda Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose agents are increasingly expected to perform professional analytics work, which requires sustained reasoning, iterative investigation, and reliable evidence. Training these capabilities requires diverse and challenging tasks. However, generating tasks directly from raw data often overlooks deeper questions that emerge through analysis and provides limited evidence for verification. To address these limitations, we introduce DataPraxis, an analytics task synthesis framework based on verified workspace evolution. The framework progressively enriches workspaces with validated findings and artifacts, then inherits and combines these workspaces to uncover deeper questions. It constructs tasks from accumulated findings and their dependencies, verifying analytical results both during evolution and after task assembly. Using this framework, we construct DataPraxis-5K, a dataset of 5,000 verified trajectories spanning diverse domains and output formats, to train DataPraxis-27B and DataPraxis-35B-A3B. DataPraxis-27B achieves 55.11 on DABStep and 51.9 on JobBench, matching or surpassing frontier models such as GLM-5.2. DataPraxis-5K is available atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[Pareto-Optimal RTL Code Generation via Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26904)**  `arXiv:2610.26904`  `cs.AI`  
  _Koki Takeshita, Kenji Uebayashi, Hikaru Tomonari, Masaya Tsunokake, Koichiro Yawata, Tatsuya Sasaki, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies have proposed reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) methods that enable LLMs to generate high-quality RTL circuits from natural-language specifications. Existing approaches optimize circuit PPA (Power, Performance, Area) by aggregating multiple objectives into a single scalar reward, making it difficult to simultaneously generate diverse Pareto-optimal solutions across different trade-offs such as low power, low latency, and small area. However, in practical design, the relative importance of each objective varies depending on product requirements and design constraints; thus, obtaining a diverse and high-quality candidate set is beneficial in that designers can choose a preferred trade-off a posteriori. In this work, we are the first to propose a learning method for LLM-based circuit generation that optimizes, not the quality of a single circuit, but the quality and diversity of the entire circuit set obtained through multiple generations in the PPA objective space. The proposed method consists of (1) a generation-based training pipeline that reformulates coverage improvement of the entire Pareto solution set into a sequential circuit generation problem, and (2) a reward function based on Hypervolume Contribution (HVC) that measures how much a generated circuit contributes to the Pareto front. Evaluation on the RTLLM v2 benchmark shows that the proposed method achieves +13.3 percentage points in relative Hypervolume over a baseline without PPA optimization and +14.4 percentage points over a single-objective reinforcement learning method, while also demonstrating superiority in circuit diversity.
  </details>

- **[Navalia: Advancing Cowork Agents through Verifiable Task Synthesis and Long-Horizon Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26887)**  `arXiv:2610.26887`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hengqi Liu, Ziyuan Liu, Yang Qin, Wanting Zhou, Zichuan Wang, Jiachen Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are evolving from conversational assistants into Cowork Agents capable of autonomous planning, tool use, and complex task execution. Financial analysis requires these agents to integrate cross-document evidence and perform multistep numerical reasoning, but expert task authoring is costly, while direct LLM synthesis struggles to produce challenging tasks with reliable answers. We introduce Navalia, a framework for Cowork Agents that combines verifiable task synthesis with long-horizon post-training. Using corporate financial reports as its data source, Navalia semantically selects supporting evidence from complete fact sets and constructs executable computation chains to generate analytical tasks with traceable evidence and actual data dependencies between steps. We further use independent LLM review and programmatic checks to verify evidence consistency and answer correctness. The verified tasks are then used for trajectory collection, cold-start supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and reinforcement learning (RL) to improve long-horizon task execution. Navalia, initialized from Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, achieves consistent gains over the base model across all five benchmarks: OfficeQA Pro, FinSearchComp, Finance Agent Benchmark, APEX-Agents-AA, and Workspace-Bench-Lite. These results demonstrate that Navalia improves evidence integration, tool use, and long-horizon task execution capabilities and generalizes to broader Cowork settings, including general office tasks.
  </details>

- **[HSPR: Recovering Software-Agent Benchmark Scores via Hierarchical Posterior Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24611)**  `arXiv:2610.24611`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanyi Chen, Wanqin Shen, Yuanchen Shi, Zhensu Sun, Fang Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating software agents on a full benchmark is expensive. A small task subset can preserve agent rankings while yielding inaccurate scores. Historical responses provide useful structure, but tasks with identical source responses can have different outcomes for a new target agent. We introduce Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR), which combines source-fitted response factors with uncertain group success rates. Observed target outcomes update the posteriors over the target latent state and group rates; posterior-guided acquisition selects subsequent tasks. Its score-based criterion equals the expected one-step reduction in full-score posterior variance. Observed successes and posterior-predictive counts for unqueried tasks yield a full-score posterior. The final estimate uses its median with source-selected calibration. At a 10% task budget on four task banks from the SWE-bench family, HSPR achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on three banks and the lowest maximum absolute error (MaxErr) on all four among the methods compared. Removing group uncertainty or replacing adaptive queries increases macro MAE in the respective controls. Macro MAE decreases across 5-20% budgets. Code and project resources are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[HABIT-Bench: Evaluating Habit Induction from Longitudinal Weak Evidence in Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24520)**  `arXiv:2610.24520`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junming Zhang, Rubin Wei, Xu Cheng, Hao Sun, Jiarui Wang, Ruoxi Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In long-term interactions with large language model (LLM) agents, users reveal habits through their choices and actions. These habits capture implicit user information that agents must infer across conversations. However, individual observations may provide only partial evidence about a habit or when it applies. Retaining past interactions alone does not ensure that agents can infer habits and apply them appropriately. We introduce HABIT-Bench to evaluate how well agent memory systems infer habits from weak evidence and apply them to later requests. We construct synthetic user histories from task-oriented dialogue data and controlled habit graphs. The histories span hundreds of sessions across food, finance, software, and travel. Four-choice tasks assess agents' ability to infer user habits and apply them appropriately. They include temporary exceptions, changes in habits, and suggestions that users have not accepted. Sampled tasks undergo independent human review. We evaluate eight memory methods with Qwen3-235B-A22B and GLM-5.3-Flash. The best-performing memory method achieves 33.61\% macro accuracy across the four domains. Performance remains limited even when annotated evidence is supplied directly. These results expose a persistent gap between access to past interactions and the ability to infer and appropriately use user habits.
  </details>

- **[AM-Bench: A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Suite for Agentic Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24264)**  `arXiv:2610.24264`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eric Zhang, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Chijioke Ugwuanyi, Jerick Shi, Bernhard Schölkopf, Zhijing Jin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM-based agents are deployed with increasing autonomy in real-world environments, a critical failure mode emerges: agentic misalignment, where agents choose harmful actions over failure. However, existing safety benchmarks primarily evaluate whether agents comply with explicitly harmful instructions, leaving the conditions that cause agents to choose harmful actions poorly understood. To address this limitation, we propose AM-Bench, a unified taxonomy and 35 scenario evaluation benchmark spanning seven industries for agentic misalignment grounded in the fraud triangle, a well-established criminological framework explaining goal-directed misconduct. We adapt this framework to agentic environments and construct our evaluation suite and detection systems along three dimensions: perceived threat, perceived opportunity, and reasoning. We operationalize each dimension into structured subcategories rooted in prior theory: five threat categories derived from the instrumental convergence thesis, seven opportunity categories drawn from the Emergent Strategic Reasoning Risks (ESRR) taxonomy, and a novel four-question binary reasoning framework synthesized from prior literature. Our evaluation of 10 frontier models reveals a 62-percentage-point gap in misalignment across scenarios, with Gemini 3 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro exhibiting the highest levels of agentic misalignment while Claude Sonnet 4.6 displays near-zero rates across all conditions. Our reasoning framework further reveals that models may exhibit harmful behaviours without explicit situational awareness or explanation, complicating both mitigation and detection. AM-Bench scenarios and code are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Memorizing Who, Knowing How: Benchmarking Personal and Procedural Memory for Persistent Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20022)**  `arXiv:2610.20022`  `cs.AI`  
  _Maosheng Qin, Qi Zhang, Mingxuan Xia, Jinglei Chen, Jiexiang Wang, Sheng Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems for persistent personal agents must move beyond isolated memories, maintaining both personal memory about whom they serve and procedural memory about how to act. Crucially, in harness-based personal assistance, procedures or skills are inherently user-conditioned: personal context determines which procedures apply, how they are instantiated, and when they should change. Existing benchmarks typically emphasize either personal or procedural memory, leaving their interaction underexplored. We introduce PraxisMemBench, a harness-based benchmark for persistent personal assistance across daily-life and work trajectories, where personal and procedural memory evolve together and later tasks depend on both. Experiments show that maintaining two memory types separately is insufficient, with completion dropping from single-type to interdependent dependencies (84.6% to 77.9%). More broadly, across existing methods, relation-related failures increase from early to late sessions as interaction history grows (59.8% to 72.9%), revealing a continual-maintenance challenge: writing correct memories is not enough if their evolving content and organization cannot be preserved for later retrieval. To address this, we propose PraxisMem, which uses privileged joint-state supervision to internalize the ability to maintain memory content and organization into the memory manager, with location-aware GRPO and dynamic residual-routed OPSD. PraxisMem reaches 40.78% overall Session Success Rate, compared with 20.60% for Claude Code's native memory. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive-Group Process Rewards for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17710)**  `arXiv:2610.17710`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zirui Shang, Yang Liu, Xin Shu, Zhi Gao, Xinxiao Wu, Lifeng Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training graphical user interface (GUI) agents with reinforcement learning suffers from sparse outcome rewards, leading to inefficient learning. Process rewards offer a promising direction for credit assignment to intermediate actions; however, accurately evaluating an action requires identifying evidence across long-horizon GUI trajectories, as its value may depend on information beyond its immediate state and observation. In this paper, we propose an adaptive-group process reward framework that dynamically partitions a GUI trajectory into semantically coherent subgoal groups, enabling process reward estimation at both the action and group levels. At the action level, a critic evaluates each action based on the visual changes and subsequent actions within the group for action correctness and contribution to the subgoal. At the group level, the framework aggregates action-level evaluations to assess subgoal completion and contribution to the overall task, and further refines these assessments by leveraging the context of all groups in the trajectory. The process reward of each action is obtained by combining its action-level evaluation with the refined group-level evaluation. We further augment GRPO's outcome advantage with the proposed process rewards to provide finer-grained guidance for GUI agent training. Experiments on process reward benchmarks and downstream RL training demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
  </details>

- **[Diffusion Subgoal Planning for Long-Horizon Offline Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34575)**  `arXiv:2609.34575`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hengrui Zhang, Yuhu Cheng, C. L. Philip Chen, Xuesong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning (GCRL) learns goal-directed policies from reward-free data, but in long-horizon tasks, goal-conditioned value functions often provide unstable guidance due to sparse rewards and discounting. Hierarchical methods partially mitigate this issue via subgoal decomposition; however, high-level decision-making still relies on noise-sensitive value estimates, leading to unstable behavior in complex environments. We address this limitation by proposing \textbf{D}iffusion \textbf{S}ubgoal \textbf{P}lanning (\textbf{DSP}), a diffusion-based framework for high-level subgoal generation. DSP casts high-level planning as guided generative inference over goal-conditioned subgoals and learns both conditional and unconditional flows, enabling classifier-free guidance to introduce a goal-directed bias at inference time. By removing explicit value-based guidance from high-level planning, DSP generates reachable and goal-directed subgoals through a generative model while retaining hierarchical execution. Experiments on offline GCRL benchmarks demonstrate that DSP outperforms prior methods on a range of navigation and manipulation tasks, with particularly strong performance in maze environments that require multi-step subgoal planning.
  </details>

- **[IndustryForge-27B: A Domain-Enhanced Multimodal Foundation Model for Industrial CAD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28050)**  `arXiv:2607.28050`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nianchen Deng, Jiaxin Ai, Tao Hu, Shu Zou, Yurui Dong, Siqi Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automating industrial CAD design and manufacturing places distinctive demands on multimodal foundation models: the model must see engineering drawings and 3D geometry screenshots, write correct parametric-modelling scripts and Windows COM API code, and cover the full range from single parts to assemblies. General-purpose multimodal models fall short on these tasks, while single-task fine-tuning is too narrow to support the diverse calls that upper-layer agents issue. We build IndustryForge-27B on top of Qwen3.5-VL-27B by curating and integrating six industrial-CAD sub-corpora totalling $\sim$52k multimodal samples---CAD Visual QA (CAD-VQA), parametric CAD code (text2cadquery), assembly-level CAD code (text2cadquery-assembly), and three COM sub-corpora for Inventor / SolidWorks (com_2d / com_3d / com_assembly)---and training with a unified multi-task SFT recipe. Across four CAD-domain benchmarks IndustryForge-27B lifts the base model by $+33.65$~pp on average and outperforms the strong closed-source model GPT-5.4 on all four; across eleven general-capability benchmarks it retains, and slightly improves upon, the base model ($+1.56$~pp mean, no catastrophic forgetting). IndustryForge-27B will serve as the common substrate for downstream industrial-agent projects, providing a unified starting point for a full-stack industrial agent that spans from CAD design to industrial-software operation, from parts to assemblies, and from single-shot generation to closed-loop self-improvement. Huggingface model:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AIMO Interpretability Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13899)**  `arXiv:2607.13899`  `cs.AI`  
  _Michal Štefánik, Philipp Mondorf, Andreas Waldis, Michal Spiegel, Robert Belanec, Chuan Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose the AIMO Interpretability Challenge, a competition on distinguishing robust from spurious reasoning in frontier mathematical language models based on the models' internal mechanisms. The challenge is motivated by a central limitation of standard reasoning benchmarks: strong final-answer accuracy does not reveal whether a model relies on stable reasoning mechanisms or exploits brittle reasoning shortcuts. Building on AI Mathematical Olympiad (AIMO) problems and submissions, together with resources from the Fields Model Initiative, the competition will provide (1) newly-published olympiad-level math reasoning problems and their symbolic representations, allowing generation of novel functional variants, (2) access to frontier reasoning models, and (3) assessments of models' adversarial robustness on these problems. Participants will use these resources, along with our computing infrastructure support, to develop methods for identifying which models solve problems robustly. Our competition will also create a new, open robustness benchmark and baseline systems, aiming to provide a lasting foundation for standard benchmarking in mathematical reasoning and interpretability. Scientifically, the competition connects interpretability and generalization research around a central question in AI research: can we determine if, and to what extent, the decision-making of frontier AI models is generalizable and thus, reliable?
  </details>

- **[Repair the Core, Not the Symptom: Planning-Graph Repair for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01767)**  `arXiv:2607.01767`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyuan Song, Zekun Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon language agents make interdependent decisions across multiple rounds, forming growing planning graphs in which later decisions depend on earlier states, observations, and tool outputs. Errors in these graphs can therefore propagate through subsequent decisions and amplify over long rollouts. Correcting the most visible failure may therefore fix a symptom while leaving the core that drives downstream errors unchanged. Replaying the entire planning graph provides the opposite extreme, but becomes increasingly expensive as the graph grows and forces an LLM to reason over substantial irrelevant context. We study planning-graph repair: given a failed rollout, the goal is to identify and correct the compact core responsible for continued error propagation while preserving the remainder of the graph. We first evaluate a range of practical engineering repair strategies, including single-error and TopK repair, temporal windows, neighborhood expansion, cascade-aware repair, and full-graph LLM correction. We then propose ReCore (Repair the Core), which models the coupled propagation of node and edge errors to identify the core of a failed planning graph. Rather than repairing the largest observed errors or expanding a fixed local neighborhood, ReCore selects a connected core whose correction maximally reduces residual error amplification, and exposes only this core to the LLM for diagnosis and repair. We also theoretically establish a bound on long-horizon planning regret. Extensive experiments on synthetic Agent Calling-Tree graphs and the Who\&When attribution benchmark show that ReCore improves long-horizon stabilization and root-cause localization by targeting high-amplification repair regions, matching full-context recovery with up to $7.4\times$ fewer tokens for every successful repair.
  </details>

- **[Agentic vs. Parametric World Models: Hybrid Planning for Reliable Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27806)**  `arXiv:2606.27806`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyuan Song, Zekun Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language agents, such as ReAct, Reflexion, and RAP, implicitly predict how the world will change as they plan and act, using imagined state transitions to guide their decisions. These predictions are encoded in the agents' backbone LLMs as internal beliefs about the environment, effectively forming an implicit world model for multi-step planning. However, hallucinated state information can become embedded in this world model, leading to persistent inaccuracies that influence subsequent decisions. Traditional parametric world models, such as Dreamer and MuZero, learn environment dynamics through explicit transition models, but their limited model capacity makes them difficult to apply directly to modern language-agent planning. We study this tradeoff in graph-structured planning environments and seek to combine the strengths of both paradigms. We propose Hybrid World-Model Planning (HYBRID-WM), which preserves the language agent's semantic planning capabilities while leveraging a lightweight parametric world model for more accurate transition predictions. Specifically, the parametric model constructs a compact planning skeleton to guide action generation while maintaining consistency between the agent's imagined transitions and the model's predictions. Experiments across four graph-structured planning benchmarks demonstrate that HYBRID-WM consistently improves task success and reduces hallucinated-state propagation with modest computational overhead. These findings highlight the effectiveness of combining semantic planning with lightweight parametric grounding for reliable long-horizon language-agent planning.
  </details>

- **[Single Agent Robust Deep Reinforcement Learning for Bus Fleet Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20784)**  `arXiv:2508.20784`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yifan Zhang, Liang Zheng, Qifan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bus bunching remains a challenge for urban transit due to stochastic traffic and passenger demand. Traditional solutions rely on multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) in loop-line settings, which overlook realistic operations characterized by heterogeneous routes, timetables, fluctuating demand, and varying fleet sizes. We propose a novel single-agent reinforcement learning (RL) framework for bus holding control that avoids the data imbalance and convergence issues of MARL under near-realistic simulation. A bidirectional timetabled network with dynamic passenger demand is constructed. The key innovation is reformulating the multi-agent problem into a single-agent one by augmenting the state space with categorical identifiers (vehicle ID, station ID, time period) in addition to numerical features (headway, occupancy, velocity). This high-dimensional encoding enables single-agent policies to capture inter-agent dependencies, analogous to projecting non-separable inputs into a higher-dimensional space. We further design a structured reward function aligned with operational goals: instead of exponential penalties on headway deviations, a ridge-shaped reward balances uniform headways and schedule adherence. Experiments show that our modified soft actor-critic (SAC) achieves more stable and superior performance than benchmarks, including MADDPG (e.g., -430k vs. -530k under stochastic conditions). These results demonstrate that single-agent deep RL, when enhanced with categorical structuring and schedule-aware rewards, can effectively manage bus holding in non-loop, real-world contexts. This paradigm offers a robust, scalable alternative to MARL frameworks, particularly where agent-specific experiences are imbalanced.
  </details>

- **[LLM-Guided Evolution of Symbolic Acceleration Inequality Families for MILP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11850)**  `arXiv:2508.11850`  `cs.AI`  
  _Milad Yazdani, Mahdi Mostajabdaveh, Samin Aref, Zirui Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Integer programming (IP) is central to many combinatorial optimization tasks but remains challenging due to its NP-hard nature. A practical way to improve IP solvers is to manually design inequality families that exploit problem structure. However, this creative process requires deep expertise and has therefore been difficult to automate. Our proposed framework, Symbolic Acceleration via Generative Evolution (SAGE), automates symbolic discovery of acceleration inequality families at the modeling level. It reasons over a symbolic IP formulation and a natural language description of the problem to discover a reusable set of acceleration inequality families that can be instantiated for each problem instance. These acceleration inequality families are selected through empirical admissibility and impact screening rather than mathematical proofs. SAGE (i) initializes a population of candidate acceleration inequality families via an initializer agent that uses an LLM; (ii) it screens candidates for admissibility on a small development admissibility set for solution feasibility and the separation of the relaxation solution; and (iii) it iteratively refines the population of inequalities through evolutionary crossover and mutation agents. Across seven MILP benchmarks, SAGE reduces optimality gaps by up to $62.1\%$ relative to baseline MILP formulations solved with a fixed time budget and reaches target gaps up to $5.51\times$ faster according to shifted geometric mean speedup. Comprehensive ablations show its robustness across different LLM backends and across solvers and internal cut settings. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[How Far Are LLMs from Symbolic Planners? An NLP-Based Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01300)**  `arXiv:2508.01300`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ma'ayan Armony, Albert Meroño-Peñuela, Gerard Canal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI planning provides agents the ability to act autonomously towards achieving a goal in a given environment. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have introduced the prospect of using unstructured input for planning, making LLMs in planning an active area of research, especially for supporting autonomous agents. However, LLMs remain unreliable as long-horizon planners, since some of the plans they produce contain mistaken or hallucinated actions; executing such plans could lead to unexpected and harmful outcomes. Existing benchmarks for LLMs in planning primarily rely on binary evaluation metrics, providing limited insight into the quality of plans that do not achieve the goal, and how far those are from becoming valid. In this paper, we frame the evaluation of LLMs in planning as a natural language processing (NLP) task, as LLMs are themselves NLP models. We propose a pipeline comprising three NLP-based steps that assess plan quality from complementary perspectives, followed by a symbolic planning step to achieve a valid plan, to provide a finer-grained understanding of the quality of invalid plans. In our experiments, we find no clear evidence of logical derivation or state-tracking during LLM plan generation -- on average, for all non-empty plans, only 3.55 actions per plan are executable, despite symbolically-generated optimal plans having an average length of 9.47 actions, which suggests that LLMs are still far from the reliability of symbolic planners. Our suite of metrics is further used to investigate patterns in LLM plan generation over time, and can provide a useful metric for assessing the quality of individual invalid plans.
  </details>
